

# Xikari: On Conditions, Structure, and Transformation

By Xikari

Objects, Conditions, Effects, and Bounded Action

October 2026

## Scope and Reading Approach

This book examines how empirical objects form structures, the relations through which they operate, and how conditions and events change their states. A structural judgment always concerns a specific object, question, time window, and set of available materials. Whether different judgments can be connected depends on conditions concerning representation, evidence, mechanisms, resources, and permissions. The book develops structural explanation, event projection, conditional foresight, and bounded choice in separate treatments. This allows bodies of domain knowledge to be checked against one another while retaining their own objects and methods. The common thread is to identify which conditions support objects and relations, which effects can be transmitted across periods, and which distinctions must be preserved in conversions of representation and action.

The universal layer addresses the empirical world in a broad sense. Human action, meaning, responsibility, and authorization enter their respective specialized layers. They must not be projected back onto natural processes as a shared purpose. Actors and multi-circle configurations are represented jointly only when the representation can preserve the distinctions needed for the task.[^p00-tn-circle] Understanding can be an endpoint. Well-grounded local answers need not wait for every question to be resolved, nor does complexity constitute explanatory or foresight capability.[^p00-tn-foresight]

Judgments about the future require the target, horizon, baseline, and failure conditions to be stated. Recommendations require normative premises and the price to be paid to be disclosed. Actual adoption and execution also require the applicable protections, review, and authorization. Definitions, formal proofs, teaching scenarios, domain experience, and effects of actual use each bear their own evidential responsibilities and must not stand in for one another as evidence.

Parts 1 and 2 explain the reading approach and evidential responsibilities. Parts 3–6 develop the universal grammar, conditional propositions, empirical testing, scale transformations, and mechanisms of operation. Parts 7–11 address human experience, collective prototypes, actors, multi-circle configurations, and dynamic projection. Parts 12–16 discuss foresight, choice, tools, norms, intervention, and governance. Appendix A provides the full interfaces for human variables. Appendix B provides a terminology index. Appendix C presents the common method and self-contained formal arguments. Appendix D connects explanations and applications across 32 domains.

The book's basic commitment is to provide an analytical language that can be checked and arguments that hold under stated conditions. It does not presuppose that the four families of empirical questions suffice to deduce all domain knowledge. Proofs of formal propositions, support from instance evidence, and the practical benefits of the method as a whole each have their own standards of judgment. As the range of application expands, domain mechanisms may be added, or a simpler and sufficient method may take over.

# Part 1 Introduction

This framework provides a working language for describing structures, testing mechanisms, and constraining inferences about action. It connects objects, conditions, actors, events, and consequences, allowing explanations at different scales and in different circles[^p01-tn-circle] to be checked against one another. Using it should make clearer what supports a judgment, where it applies, which competing explanations remain, and when to stop drawing further conclusions.

Analysis can represent actors and the multiple circles they inhabit as a joint state. Circles may be parallel, nested, overlapping, bridged, or in competition, and may also form only temporarily around particular events. Whether to use this representation depends on whether it helps answer the current question. A technical fault may be adequately explained by a local technical model; conflicts involving family care, work arrangements, and public services may require observing several circles at once. Adding variables and layers requires stating which necessary distinctions are retained relative to a simpler explanation. Complexity must not substitute for explanatory capability.

## 1.1 Four Kinds of Output and Eight Layers of Responsibility

The framework distinguishes explanation, projection, conditional foresight[^p01-tn-foresight], and bounded choice. These four kinds of output answer different questions, require different materials, and each have their own stopping point.

Explanation addresses phenomena that have already occurred, specifying objects, relations, conditions, and possible mechanisms while retaining competing accounts that cannot yet be distinguished. Projection starts from explicit states and conditions and unfolds paths that may follow an event. Conditional foresight goes further by making a judgment about the future with a target, a horizon, and a simple baseline, one that can be evaluated and is allowed to fail. Bounded choice discloses normative premises, evidence, the price to be paid, and affected positions, comparing action, delay, probes, exit, and non-action. Analytical recommendations for deliberation may be formed under these conditions; actual adoption and execution must separately pass the applicable protection, review, and lawful authorization procedures.

| Output | Question Asked | Main Entry Point | Limits on the Conclusion |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Explanation | Why this phenomenon occurred | Parts 2–8 | An existing explanation does not by itself acquire eligibility for prediction or authority to act |
| Projection | Which conditions and events may cause states to change along different paths | Parts 9–11 | Retain branches, unknowns, residuals, and stopping conditions |
| Conditional foresight | Which outcome is more reasonably expected within explicit horizons and conditions | Part 12 | Register targets and baselines before results; check and write back after results |
| Bounded choice | Whether one should act, wait, probe, exit, or do nothing | Parts 12–15 | Constrained by norms, protections, evidence, authorization, and the action ceiling |

The eight layers of responsibility are U, the universal structural core; S, cross-scale and cross-circle transformations; H, the human structured world; A, actor states; MC, multi-circle joint states; P, path projection and conditional foresight; I, inference and real-world interfaces; and N, normative choice and authorization. These layers represent a division of responsibilities, not a ranking of value. Nor do they form a ladder of maturity that objects must climb stage by stage.

The four kinds of output can connect within the same task, but each connection requires the corresponding conditions to be supplied. An explanation may hold while prediction remains difficult; a forecast may be accurate while a particular action remains unjustified; an option may merit discussion without yet having authorization for execution. Predictive capability and decision-making authority are established separately.

## 1.2 Reading Paths through the Book

Parts 1–4 explain the scope of application, terms, evidential responsibilities, empirical question families, outcome contracts[^p01-tn-contract] and mechanism contracts, and conditional formal propositions. Parts 5–8 address scale transformation, structural operation, adaptation to the human domain, and collective state prototypes. Parts 9–12 address actors, multi-circle joint objects, event projection, conditional foresight, and bounded choice. Parts 13–16 connect these topics to tools, norms, intervention, and governance.

When analyzing a specific event, one may first freeze the question, event scope, and data cutoff, then identify actors and candidate circles, stating material conditions and experience–meaning conditions separately. Next, register the update clocks of different variables, unfold paths along channels supported by evidence, and list early and contrary signals that can distinguish the paths. Only when a future judgment needs to be released must the further responsibilities for registering and evaluating conditional foresight be borne. Only when real-world action or high-impact adoption is needed does the analysis enter the corresponding normative, protection, and authorization procedures.

Appendix A provides complete interfaces for human variables; Appendix B offers an index of identifiers and terms; Appendix C explains the common method kernel and bridges across disciplines; and Appendix D provides entry points into domain explanations and applications. The appendices help readers return to the contracts they need; they do not replace domain research itself.

## 1.3 The Framework and Domain Knowledge

The framework does not replace disciplinary evidence, legal procedures, professional assessments, or the decision-making authority of those concerned. Its function is to let different explanations correct one another within the same question and to make their respective grounds and limits visible.

Technical research can show what can actually be done; institutional research can show who can decide; actors' experiences can show how an arrangement enters everyday life. These materials may together constitute an explanation, but the validity of one does not automatically provide evidence for the others. When a more precise domain model is needed, that model's variables, units, objects, and identification conditions should be retained. More abstract structural terms are useful only when they preserve the distinctions at issue.

Different methods can also fulfill their own tasks separately. Historical understanding, textual interpretation, empirical description, formal derivation, and normative argument need not first be fully rewritten into structural variables to acquire standing as knowledge. Formal eligibility within the framework constrains the relevant strong claims made in its name; it must not be expanded into a universal admission institution for all knowledge.

## 1.4 Explanation, Diagnosis, Normative Choice, and Intervention[^p01-tn-diagnosis]

### 1.4.1 Explanation

Explanation gives an account of phenomena and their possible mechanisms. Structural explanation pays particular attention to objects, relations, flows, feedback, and conditions, but must still compare other explanations sufficient to account for the same materials. A coherent story can raise questions; coherence alone cannot establish causation.

### 1.4.2 Diagnosis

Within the scope supported by evidence, diagnosis locates constraints, points of destabilization, and gaps in carrying. The object, scale, time window, and risk level of a diagnosis must be explicit. Identifying a risk does not automatically authorize others to act on it, nor does it deprive the person being analyzed of standing to dissent and appeal.

### 1.4.3 Normative Choice

Normative choice discloses value premises, claims of right, the consequences of options, and conflicting reasons. Moving from facts to an evaluation or recommendation requires stating the normative premises that connect them. The same facts may leave room for disagreement under different normative reasons. Value choices must not be rewritten as factual necessity in order to produce a unified conclusion.

### 1.4.4 Intervention

Intervention specifies the level of actual action, source of authorization, scope, stopping conditions, and paths to remedy. When the window permits, information can change later choices, and the benefits of a probe can sufficiently offset the price to be paid, reversible small steps may be considered first. Imminent harm, accumulated exposure, or a critical window may also make waiting more dangerous. Reversibility needs to be verified from the affected positions: the possibility of rolling back a technical configuration does not mean that losses others have already borne disappear with it.

Opinions, comparisons of options, and institutional drafts for deliberation do not by themselves generate power to constrain others. If these outputs will in practice be used for sanctions, eligibility decisions, resource allocation, or other high-impact decisions, review and authorization must be completed according to their foreseeable use, and responsibility must not be reduced merely by calling such an output “advice.”

Outcomes also need to be judged separately. Industrial capability, workers' lives, political participation, and cross-border connections may move out of step. The cessation of violence, dispute resolution, political arrangements, accountability, recovery of everyday life, and reconciliation may also change separately. Established local improvements should be retained; other outcomes that are not yet supported still require separate evidence.

## 1.5 Choosing an Entry Point by Task and Risk

### 1.5.1 Entering by Task

When a formal analysis is needed, first state the object, question, time window, scale, and required output. Understanding concepts can begin with the universal grammar; comparing different scales requires checking transformations; analyzing real events requires distinguishing evidence and mechanisms; and real-world action proceeds further into the procedures for choice and intervention.

### 1.5.2 Escalating According to Risk

The intensity of scrutiny increases with the use of a conclusion and the actual risks. Insufficient evidence, unequal power, irreversible consequences, and high-pressure situations require more careful review. Everyday reading, local understanding, and ordinary communication need not run through every contract.

Procedures themselves have costs in time, care, privacy, and expression. Priority should be given to gaps that would change the current judgment or protective arrangements, while retaining local conclusions supported by the available materials. Those concerned must not be required to learn the entire terminology before their experiences are acknowledged or their justified needs addressed.

### 1.5.3 Scope of Reading and Use of the Method

The scope of theoretical study should match the judgments for which responsibility is being borne. Evaluating the book's overall consistency, comparing the entire scheme, or transferring its common grammar requires reading it in full. For conceptual clarification and local applications, one may explore organizing one's reading around the common constraints, the Parts required by the task, and the specific sections cited. Conclusions from partial reading cover only the materials obtained and understood; they do not automatically represent the judgment of the book as a whole.

Reading in layers first retains the common grammar, evidential status, the specialized meanings of core terms, relations of nonequivalence, and output boundaries, then covers the complete sections on which the current task depends. Continue reading when cross-chapter references, competing explanations, exceptions, exclusions, or scale conversions arise. If the scope of the dependencies cannot be determined, expand the reading to consecutive Parts or the whole book. Method summaries, indexes, and other people's accounts can only help locate material; they must not stand in for the conditions in the source text being used.

Whether reading in layers can replace reading in full is a question about method use that requires comparison. The comparison should hold the task, materials, model conditions, and evaluation method fixed, observing consequential misjudgments, effective answers, retained unknowns, and reading burden separately. Acceptable differences must be set in advance; the criteria must not be moved after results have been seen. Before the comparison has been completed, experimental applications with explicitly marked scopes may be undertaken, without claiming on that basis that the two approaches are equivalent. Nor does a reading log or a completion marker establish understanding.

## 1.6 Ten Questions for Strong Judgments

A strong judgment should be checked against 10 questions: whether the object is established, whether the evidence is sufficient, whether a causal bridge exists, whether the scale has been switched without acknowledgment, whether alternative explanations have been compared, whether rights have been impaired, whether authorization is valid, whether action can be stopped, whether consequences can be remedied, and whether the conclusion can be appealed.

The 10 questions are not a checklist for adding points to a conclusion. Each addresses a gap that could invalidate the current judgment or cause it to exceed its authority. When a question cannot be answered, it should be specified whether the gap limits the factual judgment, mechanism account, scope of extrapolation, eligibility for public release, or real-world action, and proportionate measures to downgrade or stop should be taken.

## 1.7 The Protection Floor

The protection floor requires preserving a person's standing as a subject, channels for dissent, the possibility of exit, minimal harm, traceable responsibility, and actual rollback. Protective requirements must enter specific options and the conditions of execution; they must not remain merely statements of objectives.

Inability to exit, difficulty expressing oneself, or unwillingness to disclose must not automatically be interpreted as consent, absence of loss, or absence of dissent. The specific protective arrangements and their conditions of application are developed in the Parts on norms, intervention, and governance. Possession of more materials or greater confidence in an explanation does not thereby expand the analyst's authority to act.

## 1.8 Entering an Analysis through an Event

First separate what happened, what materials it left, and what we have inferred from those materials. Freeze the data cutoff and the existing disputes. When a multi-circle representation is needed, then identify actors, the primary circle, and the necessary neighboring circles. Actor states are addressed in Part 9; circle relations and joint states in Part 10.

Material conditions and experience–meaning conditions need to be addressed proportionately at the same time. A lack of resources must not automatically be rewritten as an attitude problem, and conflicts of meaning must not be explained solely by the total amount of resources. For every proposed connection, state the layer to which the materials provide support.

Next, distinguish the clocks. Immediate emotions may change within hours; expectations about relationships may take several rounds of interaction; organizational resources may be adjusted weekly or quarterly; institutional rules may require longer procedures; and long-term capabilities and identities may change over years. An event's direct effects update only the variables reached through its actual channels, whereas autonomous dynamics and internal clocks update, as time advances, according to declared and supported rules. Variables without a corresponding basis in effects or dynamics must not change along with the narrative, and states whose evidence has expired need to be checked again or marked unknown.

When path projection is needed, inject an event into a frozen snapshot, recording direct effects, returning feedback, and cross-circle impacts. Create branches where conditions, thresholds, actor choices, or external disturbances may change subsequent paths, and list signals and stopping conditions for each path. For differences that existing variables cannot explain, multiple candidate variables and minimal tests may be proposed; an explanation that has just been thought of must not be entered directly as a fact.

If a task ends with understanding, it may conclude once a supported explanation has been formed and what remains unresolved has been made explicit. Releasing a future judgment requires registering the target, horizon, simple baseline, and evaluation arrangements before results appear. Choosing real-world actions requires a further review of action, delay, probes, exit, and non-action against the same comparative baseline, and requires authorization, ceilings, stopping, and remedy to be made explicit.

## 1.9 Boundaries of Capability

The information a model can connect is always limited. Unobserved variables, mechanisms not yet understood, unavailable materials, subjects' choices, and unforeseen events may all change paths. Written contracts make these limits easier to inspect; they cannot copy reality in full into a text.

Explanation needs to retain unexplained residuals; foresight needs to retain simple baselines and outcomes at the due date; and choice needs to retain non-action and different cost-bearing positions. Foresight capability can be supported only by continuing comparisons registered before results and checked afterward. The framework's size, number of terms, and length of exposition cannot establish it.

## 1.10 A Common Grammar and Multiple Paths to Understanding

Analysis can begin with a specific puzzle: why the same rule produces different results for different people, why a real improvement has not changed a long-term predicament, or why a particular lived experience does not appear in existing indicators. First state the difference to be explained, then choose the materials and methods. Structure, psychology, interests, culture, or technology cannot acquire the standing of an ultimate cause merely by being named.

Shared practices, the circumstances of those who bear costs, and intertemporal relations can complement one another. Practice shows how action occurs; cost-bearing positions make time, the body, resources, and the cost of refusal visible; intertemporal relations bring recurring rules and distributions into the explanation. These paths have no fixed order of value. Equipment failure may be explained primarily by technical mechanisms, local care may have value in its own right, and public rules may change the lives of many people who have never met.

Knowledge and meaning hold not only when they change collective states. A person's learning a skill, understanding history, appreciating a work, completing a game, or finding comfort can be described on the basis of the relevant materials. Specific terms such as CM-LEARNING, HV03, and HV11 address only their explicitly specified mechanisms or interfaces. Failure to satisfy a narrower contract is not the same as the absence of learning, meaning, love, or value. When ordinary concepts enter formal judgments, the meaning being used needs to be stated, avoiding silent switches between everyday and specialized uses.

“Small repairs” in everyday accounts may refer to local remedies, renewed contact, or adjustments to living arrangements. Analysis of these materials should retain the distinctions between who names them, what actually changes, and what losses continue, without declaring on behalf of those concerned that recovery has already occurred. Ordinary help does not depend on advance registration, but a judgment of repair in the framework's formal sense must still satisfy the target criteria and responsibility boundaries in §2.8 and §6.10.

A complete analysis should let readers see the question, evidence, main explanation, strongest opposing case, judgment, and its limits, as well as what new materials would change that judgment. What can be answered need not wait until every problem is solved; what cannot be supported must not be concealed by fluent narration. Understanding can itself be an endpoint; eligibility for foresight, high-impact adoption, and real-world action each bear further responsibilities.

## 1.11 Conditions for Connecting Judgments

The key to structural explanation is to specify which judgments can connect under which conditions. The same materials may suffice to answer a question about a total without necessarily sufficing to answer a question about its distribution. Two local causal links may each hold without necessarily being borne by the same set of objects. Each option may be feasible on its own without necessarily being possible to complete at the same time as the others using shared, limited resources. Lawful authorization does not guarantee that actual action reaches the intended endpoint, either. A failed connection may lie in representation, identification, clocks, capacity, rules, access to materials, or permissions. These failures should not all be assigned to vague complexity.

Analysis begins with the difference at issue, retaining inputs, outputs, conditions, and modes of failure for each connection. If information necessary for the task has been lost, a minimal distinction may be added. If a rule is being used incorrectly, it should be corrected. If materials cannot be obtained in time, access or decision arrangements need to change. If resources or permissions are lacking, greater explanatory precision alone will not supply them. Revisions should address the actual point of failure while retaining the limited results already obtained at the preceding stage.

Conditionality is not the same as rejecting conclusions across the board. Mathematical function decomposition can prove representational sufficiency for a given question; specialist research can support bounded total effects; and auxiliary conditions can enable actual recovery of expression and function. Each positive conclusion bears only the content it actually addresses. When the task, operations, object, or window changes, check again whether the original basis can support the connection; what cannot yet be supported remains an identifiable unresolved matter.

This also determines the standing of the framework itself. The common grammar organizes questions; formal contracts delimit inference; domain evidence supports empirical conclusions; normative reasons constrain choices; and engineering implementation enables the method to run. These can help one another, but none can by itself secure eligibility on behalf of another layer. A shorter, sufficient specialist method should be permitted; a more complex joint representation must state which necessary distinctions it preserves and what costs it adds.

## 1.12 Theoretical Skeleton and the Burden of Argument

Definitions give questions clear meanings; method constraints make the status of materials, scope of inference, and conversion processes checkable; conditional formal propositions state necessary relations within a given model; specific assertions within empirical question families, together with mechanism propositions, are subject to support or refutation from real-world materials; and normative reasons and operational procedures constrain adoption and action. These elements connect, but do not form a single chain of deduction from a few natural axioms to every domain.

G1–G4 organize four kinds of empirical question concerning grouping, channels, history, and scale; specific answers belong to given objects, windows, and comparative designs. C1–C12 bear different responsibilities, including outcome registration, combinations of additional mechanisms, independent pattern identification, and procedural bridging. F1–F6 state formal relations with explicit objects and proofs. These three sets of identifiers help locate questions, use results, and check derivations, respectively; their number does not add up to theoretical strength.

For example, a person's practice can affect later performance through current skills. If those skills adequately represent the relevant impacts, a complete practice history does not necessarily add further predictive information; this does not negate learning. Similarly, a total may answer a question about the total accurately while remaining insufficient to identify local overload. The first example requires distinguishing historical formation from conditional residuals; the second requires matching representation to task. Distinguishing relations that can hold, relations that cannot be inferred in sequence, and relations that require supplementation is the common method by which this book organizes materials from different domains.

The theory itself also needs comparisons that can change its conclusions. A proposition's fitting the data does not establish that the classification used is the simplest; an instance's receiving positive support does not establish that every object has that mechanism; and a rigorous manuscript does not establish that its adoption by readers or tools has improved real-world performance. Comparisons with specialist methods and streamlined methods should retain the same necessary materials and protective requirements, evaluating consequential misjudgments, effective answers, retained unknowns, time spent, and burden separately.

<a id="part_2"></a>

# Part 2 Boundaries and Methods

The ultimate object domain of this framework is the broadly conceived empirical structural world. Natural processes, technical systems, ecological processes, human actions, organizations, and institutions can all enter structural explanation if they can leave comparable materials and allow researchers to declare candidate objects, states, relations or transitions, interfaces, time windows, scale profiles, and identity criteria. The human structured world is not a synonym for the world as a whole. It is a specialized domain within that world, requiring additional human-domain clauses, normative commitments, and authorization procedures. Purely metaphysical assertions, objects for which no empirical fields can be supplied, and “systems” established solely through naming or narrative coherence do not enter empirical judgment. They may be discussed, but they must not borrow this framework's levels of empirical conclusions.

The current scope of application must still be declared separately each time: the object, SP/T/K, region or environment, data window, extrapolation unit, and exclusions. Belonging to the broadly conceived empirical structural world means only that a contract[^p02-tn-contract] can in principle be established. It does not imply that any conclusion can be directly generalized across windows, objects, or domains.

This choice of scope also sets the framework's limits. The framework seeks to explain how objects form identifiable structures, how they operate, how they change or undergo dissolution under the effects of conditions, and how descriptions at different scales can be converted. It does not presuppose a unified purpose for the world, project human intentions onto nonhuman processes, or infer “how one should choose” directly from “what is.” Real-world choices are taken up by the normative layer and operational procedures separately.

## 2.1 Two-Stage Object Admission

Structural modeling begins with candidate objects, not with objects already demonstrated to be valid. When formal support for root instances is sought within this framework, object admission has two stages whose order must not be reversed. This procedure governs strong empirical claims under G, and under C and CM where they depend on G. It is not a universal truth check for all domain knowledge, historical records, or everyday understanding.

The first stage is the declaration of a candidate object. The analyst registers S*=<B,X,R,I,T,SP,K> under a D0 contract: boundary B, state variables X, relations or transition constraints R, interface I, time window T, scale profile SP, and identity criterion K. The asterisk indicates that the object remains an analytical candidate. Complete fields establish only that the question has been stated clearly. They do not establish that the grouping has relatively independent empirical standing in the world. When fields are incomplete, only exploratory questions may be proposed.

The second stage is testing of root instances. When the analyst claims object identification, effects of specified channels, historical conditional increments, or scale closure under G1–G4, and applies for eligibility for formal support within this framework, a corresponding G-instance must be established. Before the results are viewed, an immutable contract version and an auditable timestamp must be used to freeze the null model N0, a single subtype, one success criterion selected from that subtype's admissible list, controls, models, samples and extrapolation units, evaluation metrics, positive thresholds, decision rules, rules for null conclusions, falsification conditions, and suspension conditions. Instances with multiple targets or models must also freeze the primary target or aggregation, model selection or ensembling, and rules for multiple comparisons. This support eligibility belongs to the specific instance that passes the test, not to the D0 declaration or the abstract templates G1–G4.

Support eligibility requires that exploratory work be kept separate from confirmatory work, positive results from null conclusions, and completed freezing from post hoc deviations. Only confirmatory or replication instances completed under a contract frozen before the evidence can provide positive support. Falsification instances support only the pre-registered falsification or null-model judgment. Exploratory work, drafts, post-evidence deviations, or undisclosed deviations can only generate candidates, register deviations, or remain unresolved. They must not be used as formal instances to support C branches that require that eligibility. Failure to pass the positive-support check does not automatically support N0 either. Unless equivalence or sufficiency, statistical power or sensitivity, and tolerance have been pre-registered and their requirements met, the conclusion can only be “not positively supported” or “unresolved.” Thus, “the candidate object has been registered,” “positive G is not supported,” “the null model is supported,” and “the object receives limited positive support” must remain distinct states.

This two-stage structure prevents two opposite errors: treating the analyst's naming, clustering, or boundary choice as establishing an object objectively; and piecing scattered materials together after the fact into an unfalsifiable “system” before the object has even been declared.

### 2.1.1 Domain Evidence and Formal Instance Eligibility

Existing studies, archives, oral accounts, archaeological materials, and locatable source texts can support the relevant descriptions, explanations, or limited causal conclusions according to their actual research designs. When these materials are used, whether they are pre-registered confirmatory work, post hoc findings, exploratory work, historical reconstruction, or other research should be preserved accurately. A pre-result contract must not be fabricated for them. These materials must not on that basis be automatically credited with G-instance support, nor does their not yet having been converted into G-instance form deprive them of their originally supported content.

If an existing finding is to be used in a new formal root instance, it may first serve as a source of questions and models, then be tested using independent materials, a later window, or an appropriate replication design. Data used for exploratory work must be separated from data used for confirmatory work, and model selection from final evaluation. Where no feasible new test is available, retain the domain conclusion and the relation awaiting testing; do not fabricate confirmation.

Formal proofs hold under their axioms, semantics, and rules of derivation. Textual interpretation is open to criticism on the basis of editions, contexts, and argumentative materials. Normative claims disclose their value premises and reasons. They can contribute to analysis, but must not masquerade as one another's empirical mechanisms, factual truth, or real-world authorization.

### 2.1.2 Connecting Formal Conclusions and Empirical Instances

Formal propositions first specify the state domain, mapping, target, admissible operations, and logical premises; proof then establishes their conclusions. Exhaustive enumeration of a complete finite domain can test every case within that domain. Numerical computation must also specify precision, boundary determination, and implementation error. Proof, exhaustive enumeration, and numerical checks can cross-check one another, but together they do not generate real-world samples, and more computations do not increase independent empirical support.

Applying a formal conclusion to a real-world object requires a separate account of how real-world quantities correspond to model variables, which premises are already supported by materials, whether the errors and applicable ranges are acceptable, and what counterexamples would invalidate the mapping. “The proposition holds in the given model” and “whether the real-world object satisfies the model remains undetermined” can both be acknowledged. A mathematical counterexample is already sufficient to refute the unconditional inference it addresses. The absence of a completed real-world experiment does not erase this formal answer. Conversely, formal feasibility, the existence of an algorithm, computability within budget, timely availability of data, correct execution in practice, and authority to adopt are six judgments, each requiring its own basis.

## 2.2 Proposition Roles and the Burden of Argument

Formal judgments first distinguish six commonly used roles: D, G, E, H, N, and O. Conditional formal propositions provable within a model are designated separately as F. If a single natural-language sentence mixes definitional, empirical, formal, methodological, or normative commitments, it must be separated into its components, and the basis on which each holds must be stated separately. The identifiers organize the burden of argument; they do not mean that all kinds have the same truth conditions.

D, definitions. These specify how terms are used within the framework; they are not empirical facts about the world. Definitions can make judgments consistent, but cannot by themselves establish the existence of objects, mechanisms, or historical effects.

G, empirical question families. G1–G4 organize empirical questions about grouping gains, specified channels, historical conditional increments, and representational closure, respectively. Specific instances must take the form of factual assertions with an object, target, conditions, comparison, and mode of failure—for example, whether a specified difference exceeds a threshold. Whether a fact is true and whether evidence is sufficient to support it are different questions. Pre-registration, review, and release rules govern eligibility for formal support; they do not define whether an effect exists in the world.

E, epistemic constraints. These specify the valid scope of observation, measurement, inference, and cross-scale transfer, requiring that blind spots, competing explanations, and residuals be retained.

H, human-domain clauses. H1, H4, and H5 are empirical hypotheses requiring case-specific evidence. H2 is an operational classification rule. H3 is a classification rule for institutional write-back. H6 is a normative boundary. H marks adaptation to the human domain; it is not a logical type mutually exclusive with definition, empirical content, or normative content. H clauses must not be treated as an unclassified set of “axioms of human nature,” nor written back as universal roots for the nonhuman world.

N, normative commitments. These explicitly declare what the framework chooses to protect and constrain. They must not be disguised as factual propositions that empirical data could prove.

O, operational procedures. These specify the freezing of descriptions, evidence audits, normative registration, authorization, comparison of options, constrained execution, review, stopping, withdrawal, and remedy.

F, conditional formal propositions. Conclusions are established by proof under explicit state domains, mappings, targets, operation sets, and mathematical assumptions. Formal counterexamples can refute universal inferences that exceed those conditions. Whether real-world objects satisfy the premises still requires independent evidence. See §4.18 for F1–F5 and §4.19 for task families and dynamic refinement.

C is not an eighth kind of fact. It combines contracts that delimit the use of outcomes with mechanism conditions; C12 provides a bridge to normative procedures. Common method constraints govern the use of representations, evidence, and inference. They do not serve as additional laws of the world.

Descriptive conclusions cannot generate normative conclusions on their own, nor can explanation or diagnosis[^p02-tn-diagnosis] generate authority to act on their own. The minimal bridge to real-world action is:

A descriptive or explanatory conclusion + explicit normative premises + current evidence + lawful authorization + an action ceiling + conditions for stopping or withdrawal

If any element of this bridge to real-world action is missing, real-world action should stop, and the associated action requirements should remain at the level of description, candidate explanations, obtaining further evidence, non-action, or proposing a reversible probe, rather than bypassing authorization through theoretical language.

## 2.3 Four Kinds of Dependency Must Be Kept Separate

Relations between propositions use four different contracts. They must not be merged into an ambiguously defined “dependency.”

| Kind of Dependency | Meaning | May It Enter the Inferential Acyclic Graph? |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `inferential_requires` | A hard inferential premise without which the conclusion cannot be obtained | Yes |
| `protocol_requires` | Method checks concerning evidence, causation, analogy, sources, or domain classification | No |
| `specializes` | Restricts a universal proposition to a particular domain, object, or subtype | No |
| `applies_to` | Specifies which objects, propositions, or actions a norm or procedure applies to | No |

Method checks determine whether a judgment may be released, but do not thereby become additional empirical causes. Domain specialization narrows the scope of application, but does not turn classification rules into factual evidence. Normative applicability constrains action, but does not in turn prove a description true. Only hard inferential premises enter the acyclic graph of roots and inferred conclusions.

## 2.3.1 Layered Responsibilities of the Common Method Kernel

This framework and *World Model* may share methodological responsibilities, while each text retains its own objects, arguments, and mode of operation. Neither serves as an authoritative source or double empirical evidence for the other. Interoperation requires checking definitions, method constraints, formal propositions, empirical questions, outcome contracts, and governance requirements one by one. A shared name must not stand in for a correspondence.

| Layer | Question Answered | Limits of Its Force |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Definitions | What objects and terms refer to | Specify decidable semantics; do not establish that something holds in reality |
| Common method constraints | How representations, the status of materials, and inference are governed | Constrain recording and use; are not laws of the world |
| Conditional formal propositions F | What necessarily follows under explicit mathematical premises | Established by proof; real-world mappings require separate verification |
| Empirical question families G | Whether a specific empirical relation holds | Instances specify the object, comparison, mode of failure, and evidence |
| Outcome and mechanism contracts C | What the existing basis permits to be registered and used | Valid only within their dependencies, additional conditions, and applicable windows |
| Governance invariants | How to release, evaluate, suspend, and act | Limit power; do not serve as empirical causes |

Identifiers such as D, G, and C provide stable references. The common kernel's Chinese identifiers serve only to align texts. They must not stand in for the complete contracts in the body, and they must not obtain double support from another text's use of the same name.

## 2.4 Causal Protocol[^p02-tn-protocol]

A causal claim first registers the specific version of the treatment or cause, the outcome, the object and population, the time window, the scale, and the effect being asked about. It then explains why the comparison can identify that effect. Common inputs, selection, attrition, changes in measurement, reverse causation, and interference between units must all be addressed where relevant. A claim about a specified structural channel or a complete mediation mechanism must also provide the mechanism chain, temporal sequence, and discriminating evidence at that level: whether blocking, replacement, or natural variation meeting identification conditions changes outcomes as expected; whether alternative channels can explain the difference; whether cross-layer conclusions have a D3/E5 bridge; and what observations would make the mechanism claim fail.

Temporal sequence, correlation, and a coherent story cannot by themselves identify causation. A design may identify the total effect for a specified population and window under explicit assumptions while still leaving the mediator undetermined. In that case, “limited identification of the effect; the specific mechanism is unresolved” should be reported, and unsupported structural effects must not be filled in. Conversely, the separate existence of several local links does not automatically establish that the chain is connected as a whole in this case or that the net effect holds. Opposing paths may cancel one another out, and a total effect near zero does not establish that all mechanisms are absent. Causal strength, mechanism resolution, and the scope of extrapolation are evaluated separately.

### 2.4.1 The Estimand and Mechanism Responsibilities

Offering someone an opportunity, that person actually receiving an intervention, and expansion of an entire institution are different treatments. A population average effect, the local average treatment effect for a particular kind of compliers, and each individual's outcome are also different estimands. When an identification assumption is impaired, downgrade only the conclusions that depend on it. For example, if an interpretation based on a particular instrumental variable is no longer reliable, that does not automatically invalidate the effect of credible random assignment on “receiving an opportunity.”

More variables do not make observational control more reliable by default. Controlling for a mediator may change the total effect being asked about; controlling for a variable jointly determined by different causes may introduce bias. A larger sample cannot compensate for explanations that cannot be distinguished, either. When point identification is unavailable, supported bounds, sets of possible directions, exclusions based on necessary conditions, or specific process knowledge may be retained, with a clear statement of what these conclusions have not answered.

“Offering an opportunity” and “actually accepting, remaining, or exiting” are different treatments. The text of an agreement, verification, execution, and everyday use are also different outcomes. An announcement, public understanding, arrival, action at a border crossing, and full clearance must each be evidenced separately along the event chain. Neither a total effect nor the existence of an agreement can fill these gaps in mediation and distribution.

### 2.4.2 Local Effects, Joint Responses, and the Chain as a Whole

When local channels are combined, the correspondence between an earlier output and a later input, the identity of the same case and its temporal sequence, the common scope of support, joint responses, and terminal outcomes should be retained. Even when two studies concern the same population, their two average effects generally cannot be multiplied directly: the people whose mediator changes may be precisely those whose outcome would not respond to that mediator. Accurate averages on both sides do not guarantee that the common conditions needed for the sequence have been retained.

A finite counterexample is a population composed of two equally weighted types, with type denoted by s∈{0,1}, binary treatment by a, and mediator by m. Let m(a)=a(1−s), with outcome y(a,m)=sm. The average effect of treatment on the mediator and the average effect of directly setting the mediator on the outcome are both 1/2. Yet in each type, the effect of treatment on the final outcome is 0, and the effect of the chain as a whole is 0. Multiplying the two averages gives 1/4, which is not the effect of the chain as a whole. This is a counterexample under given functions. It neither estimates a real-world effect nor denies a total effect independently identified by a qualified design.

“Complete” in a claim about the chain as a whole is relative only to the declared outcome, window, and dependency route. If the purpose is to identify a particular total effect with a specified scope, there is no requirement to exhaust all microscopic processes first. If the purpose changes to manipulating a mediator, transferring across domains, or allocating responsibility, supply the connections on which that purpose actually depends. Shared resources, offsetting paths, and feedback can change the result of the connection. Separate evidence for each node must not stand in for a joint test in the same case.

## 2.5 Evidence Protocol

The evidence chain distinguishes sources, materials, observation records, evidence, counterexamples, judgments, and authorization to act. Each layer needs its own reasons for conversion. Register the object, data-generation process, research design, measurement and selection, uncertainty, competing explanations, and limits of extrapolation according to the specific task. Formal root instances and foresight[^p02-tn-foresight] must also register null models, thresholds, and evaluation under their own contracts. The support relation between a material and the current claim must not be omitted on the ground that the material has a source.

Absence and silence are not negative evidence by default; inability to observe is not the same as nonexistence. Justified opacity must state its reason, minimum scope, duration, and trusted reviewer, and the strength of public conclusions must be reduced accordingly. If counterexamples or residuals are found after evidence has been frozen, the failure record should be retained, and the claim downgraded, suspended, or withdrawn under the contract. The object, subtype, threshold, or success criterion must not be rewritten after results appear.

### 2.5.1 Measurement Objects and Data Generation

Directly recorded quantities, composite indicators defined by construction, measurement of latent attributes, formative conditions, and predictive proxies carry different explanatory responsibilities. A test score can accurately reflect performance within the current set of questions while not yet representing long-term capability. Usage can fall while the required services are still insufficient. More diagnoses may also reflect easier access to testing. First state what is to be measured and how the materials are generated, then decide which errors and counterexamples are relevant to the current use.

Comparisons across groups or periods require comparability proportionate to the task. Estimating latent means generally requires explaining the unit and origin of the measurement scale. Person-specific threshold decisions must also address individual error and consequences. An identical scoring mapping does not establish identical psychological strategies. If the common anchors themselves drift, more samples do not necessarily make it possible to distinguish genuine change from changes in the measurement scale; in that case, external anchoring should be sought, or sensitivity reported.

Changes in an indicator can result from genuine improvement, genuine adaptation to a narrow target, changes in the sample that enters or is reported, or falsification of records. These mechanisms can coexist and require separate investigation. Nor does the fact that two legitimate indicators answer different questions and point in opposite directions amount to one of them necessarily being wrong; still less may manipulation be concluded directly from it.

## 2.6 Analogy Protocol

Analogy is only a tool for generating questions. Every use must be explicitly marked as an analogy and specify the source domain, target domain, shared structure, key differences, nontransferable conditions, variable mapping, and discriminating tests. Similar words, similar diagrams, compelling narratives, or similar outcomes do not constitute evidence for a bridge across domains.

Analogy can help ask “which channel, boundary, or competing explanation should also be checked.” It must not stand in for a G-instance, and it must not be used to transfer causal relations, normative evaluations, or action plans directly from the source domain to the target domain. If contrary conditions have not been specified, the conclusion from the analogy remains a heuristic candidate.

### 2.6.1 Analogy, Formal Instances, and Tested Comparisons

A heuristic analogy, a formal instance satisfying explicit mathematical premises, and a target-domain relation tested against independent materials are three different states. For the first, state similarities and differences; for a formal instance, check variables and assumptions; for empirical transfer, test operations, formation processes, and alternative explanations in the target domain. A comparison supported by independent evidence can contribute to a specific argument. Its support comes from those materials and bridges, not from the word “like.”

Historical and archaeological comparisons must also distinguish the physical formation of traces, the functions of activities, and social relations. Similar tool marks do not automatically imply the same institutions of power; kinship does not automatically imply relations of rule; and accurate dates do not automatically yield an event's full meaning. Respect for different sources of knowledge does not require giving them equal weight on every question. Different materials can each supply content that cannot be substituted for that of the others.

## 2.7 Source Protocol

Source records must specify the source domain, case type, time and region, scope of support, and limits of extrapolation item by item, and complete counterexample registration. Original materials, secondary interpretations, and this framework's inferences are kept in separate columns. Judgments in the text and their original locations are linked in both directions, so that readers can return from a judgment to the materials and can also see, from a material, which limited claim it has been used to support.

Conceptual definitions, field mappings, and method descriptions specify only what is to be tested, how it is to be recorded, and where to stop drawing conclusions. They must not enter `empirical_evidence_records` as object facts or mechanism support. Internal terms, identifiers, examples, and the framework's own formulations must not stand in for external materials. In other words, a method definition is not the same as empirical support.

Empirical support must come from independently registered instance materials and include the object and scale, time window, research design, N0, outcomes, uncertainty, review or reproduction, and evidential level. Without corresponding external instance materials, specific empirical assertions within the G question families cannot acquire formal empirical support. C is used separately for outcome registration, mechanism conditions, and normative bridging; real-world mechanism judgments under CM still need the corresponding instance evidence. A question catalog, outcome contracts, and the framework's own descriptions must not stand in for the missing materials.

### 2.7.1 Relations between Sources and the Scope of Actual Use

Reading a table of contents, an abstract, relevant chapters, or the full text should be recorded separately. Finding a link, completing a download, and understanding an argument are also different actions. Relations between sources should be retained for different analyses of the same data, a preprint and its published version, and an original report and an institution's account of it. Such related sources must not be added up by citation count into independent reproductions. When a version changes outcomes, samples, or reporting definitions, first explain the changes, then compare conclusions.

A theory's historical adoption, mere mention, explicit restriction or rejection, compatibility with the current text, and a new connection now proposed by the analyst are distinct matters. Failure to find a record of adoption is not the same as its never having been considered; transcribing a catalog is not the same as checking the original document. Failure to obtain a document within one research window does not mean that other researchers or the project as a whole lack it.

The generation, preservation, destruction, opening, and retrieval of archives, as well as narrative choices, can each cause absence. Absence has the corresponding force as counterevidence only when it is clear that a certain kind of event should have left records that could be found. Inaccurate dates in oral accounts still need correction, while the way the account is told may also be important material for understanding memory and experience. Neither cancels the other.

### 2.7.2 Evidence Independence and the Propagation of Defects

Independence must be judged relative to the claim being supported. Adding later outcomes to the same random assignment can add temporal and outcome information. Testing the same data with another algorithm can uncover implementation or interpretation problems. Neither increases the number of independent randomizations or original cohorts. Conversely, studies on the same subject but with different underlying samples must not be merged merely because their names are similar. Records should specify shared data, common measurements, version inheritance, and genuinely new observations, rather than turn a file count into an evidence score.

Traceable dependencies should be retained between source values, transcriptions, model inputs, derived statistics, and judgments in the text. When a local discrepancy is found, first distinguish conflict concerning the same object, legitimate differences between different objects, and apparent inconsistency caused by display precision. Then suspend the precise conclusions that actually depend on that discrepancy. Reconciliation of the component sum with the total does not by itself validate every component, nor does a source anomaly reveal the cause of a discrepancy on its own. Other judgments that still have independent support should continue to be used within their actual scope.

## 2.8 Core Terminology Locks

Once the following terms enter a judgment, they must satisfy their specified contracts. Their broad everyday meanings must not be substituted for those contracts.

Cost. First declare the quantity's type, unit, time window, accounting boundary, and cost-bearing position. Shifting may be traced only for quantities of the same type, or quantities of different types with an explicit conversion and accounting mapping. “Resources are occupied” is not the same as the existence of a unified cost across domains, nor does it establish any conservation principle.

Externality. An externality first requires a cross-boundary causal channel and the positions across which its effects are distributed, and then an institutional or accounting rule defining what is excluded from decision accounting. Without the latter, the effect can only be called a cross-boundary spillover, impact, or accounting gap. “Externality” must not be used to assign responsibility in advance.

The exclusion rule here may come from an explicit economic model or actual institutional and accounting arrangements. It does not require an advance determination of legal liability. The current inability to identify who bears legal liability does not prevent recording cross-boundary harm for which evidence already exists. C7 addresses cross-boundary spillovers and the positions across which they are distributed under its specified conditions; it does not cover all economic externalities or propagation in general. Where changes of scale are actually involved, D3/E5 must be checked separately; claims of closure failure or conversion of objects or interventions also require testing the corresponding G4.

Repair. The target identity criterion K* or functional criterion F* must be disclosed before results. Repair is not the same as recovery of a historical state, nor is it the same as improvement in value. Whether the target is justified, who decides it, and who may carry it out require separate review under N and O.

Selection and choice. First distinguish modes of systemic selection, choices by acting subjects, and governance choices. The systemic process of variation–differential retention–reproduction does not possess intent as a matter of course. A subject's decision is not the same as environmental selection. Governance choice additionally requires normative premises and authorization.

Responsibility and authorization. Uptake carriers, bearers of costs of the same type, beneficiaries, rights to stop, and responsible parties are registered separately. Actual execution, the greatest capability, being most affected, being most visible, or possessing knowledge cannot by themselves establish responsibility. An expansion of observational scope or of confidence in an explanation must not expand authority to act, either.

Bearing obligations requires a separate account of the relations among commitments, roles, causal contributions, benefits, capabilities, and applicable norms. These reasons may act together; no one of them can automatically settle the whole matter. Disclosing bounded obligations and their boundaries does not make anyone a person required to take up obligations without limit, unilaterally, and without the possibility of exit.

## 2.9 Missing Fields and Visibility States, Downgrading, Suspension, and Exit

Every contract field must fall within an explicit state, rather than be filled with vague wording.

Unknown: knowable in principle, but current evidence is insufficient.

Not applicable: the field has no valid counterpart in the current object contract; a reason must be stated.

Unobservable: materials cannot currently be obtained because of limits in measurement capability, the window, or the channel; this must not be rewritten as nonexistence.

Withheld for protection: materials exist, but disclosure would violate privacy, security, or other explicit protections. The basis for protection, minimum scope of non-disclosure, duration, and a trusted review path must be recorded.

When key variables are unknown, conditions under which the object is not applicable remain unaddressed, core channels are unobservable, or non-disclosure for protection cannot undergo trusted review, conclusions are downgraded successively to candidate hypotheses, lists of questions, or description only. When authorization to act is unclear, potential harm is irreversible, appeals and stopping are inaccessible, or the limits of professional capability would be exceeded, real-world action should be suspended and referred to an appropriate procedure. Downgrading is not analytical failure. It keeps the evidential ceiling, boundaries of power, and what is still unknown visible. Each downgrade is registered proposition by proposition with its dependencies and the effects of what is missing. Other judgments with independent support are not downgraded along with that proposition, but failure of a global source, common premise, or object boundary blocks all related outputs.

## 2.10 Methodological Boundaries for Multi-Circle Objects

Circles[^p02-tn-circle] are first of all candidate groupings, not natural entities established by naming. Families, organizations, platform groups, occupational communities, or cultural categories should specify boundaries, members, roles, channels, time windows, and identity criteria. Documents, relationship records, or exposure records can support the relevant facts about objects. A claim that the grouping brings explanatory or foresight gains must then be compared against commensurate simple baselines, such as no grouping or a single circle. Formal G1 support does not replace other grounds for the object's existence. Parallelism, nesting, and overlap are judged along the declared dimensions. Potential bridging, actual transmission, and effects of transmission require separate evidence. Competition specifies constraints on the available set; temporariness specifies a time limit or evidence concerning duration. Both can be combined with other relations.

Multi-circle analysis must not turn every background factor into an entity. Before a circle is added, the necessary question it answers, the distinction that would be lost if it were removed, and the measurement and privacy costs should be stated. If it is used for explanation or propagation, the relevant channels and competing explanations must also be specified. Where its current purpose cannot be stated, retain it as an environmental condition or candidate variable. When a complex model lacks stable comparative support, reduce the relevant gain claims and give priority to an adequate simple model. Retaining an object to describe supported responsibilities, differences in membership, or matter-specific permissions does not first require proving that it increases predictive accuracy.

## 2.11 Methodological Boundaries for Personality and Actor States

Personality enters only as a slow-variable hypothesis with an evidential level, not as an ontological definition of a person. A single behavior, self-report, report by someone else, scale score, or model-generated cluster cannot by itself establish a stable personality. Analysis must also retain competing explanations such as role activation, material constraints, immediate emotions, strategies, cultural norms, observer position, and measurement bias.

Actor variables are divided by timescale into slow, intermediate, and fast bands. Each is tied to an observation window, source, counterevidence, circle conditions, privacy, and permitted uses. The framework does not perform clinical or psychiatric diagnosis, nor does it record silence, refusal to disclose, or unobservability as pathological evidence. A model may propose candidate variables and minimal tests; it must not automatically fill in real-world values.

## 2.12 Methodological Boundaries for Foresight and Projection

Path projection may use observations, reports, plans, hypothetical events, and simulated events, but must retain the event type. A report's content is not automatically true; a plan is not the same as execution; hypothetical and simulated events are not written back as real-world facts. Each run freezes the joint snapshot, evidence cutoff, mechanisms, parameter ranges, clocks, propagation rules, and stopping conditions. Information obtained after the results may enter only a new version.

Conditional foresight must register, before results, the target, horizon, scope of objects and circles, input cutoff, simple baseline, paths, form of expression, calibration metrics, early signals, contrary signals, suspension, retirement, and outcome write-back. Unregistered post hoc explanations do not count as successful predictions. Changing the target, extending the horizon, narrowing the population, or merging paths all require a new record.

## 2.13 Limits on Autonomous Variable Refinement

“Autonomous variable refinement” permits only three actions: proposing multiple competing candidates from residuals; stating which channels a candidate might act through and what observable implications it might produce; and designing a minimal test with acceptable risk. Candidates move in sequence through the states proposed, under test, supported candidate, rejected, or retired. They must not jump directly from a model suggestion to an observed fact, a personality conclusion, a valid circle, or a causal mechanism.

If a candidate concerns privacy, health, safety, legal standing, or a position with little power, the necessity of obtaining the information, its use, and its harms should be reviewed first. When the value of the information is insufficient to outweigh the risk, retaining the unknown status is more appropriate than actively filling in the information.

[^p02-tn-contract]: Translator's note: *Contract* renders 合同 in an analytical sense: a specification registered and frozen before results are known, close to pre-registration. It is not a legal instrument.

[^p02-tn-diagnosis]: Translator's note: Diagnosis within this framework is nonclinical. Section 2.11 expressly excludes clinical and psychiatric diagnosis; references to diagnoses in measurement examples do not change that limit.

[^p02-tn-protocol]: Translator's note: Protocol translates 契约 and denotes a methodological check that a judgment must pass. It is distinct from contract, which translates 合同.

[^p02-tn-foresight]: Translator's note: Foresight in this book requires registration of a target, horizon, and baseline, together with calibration. A single such judgment is called a forecast.

[^p02-tn-circle]: Translator's note: *Circle* renders 圈层. Circles may be parallel, nested, overlapping, bridged, or competing, and may also form only temporarily around particular events; the term does not imply a hierarchy.



<a id="part_3"></a>

# Part 3 Universal Structural Grammar

This Part provides a shared descriptive interface for discussing the broadly conceived empirical structural world. It is not a set of assertions that “all things are isomorphic,” but a set of interfaces that can each be declared, tested, withdrawn, and converted across scales. The definition layer D0–D3 specifies how to state objects and states clearly; U01–U11 specifies which structural positions need to be distinguished in explanation. Neither definitions nor primitives automatically become empirical facts by being written into a contract[^p03-tn-contract]. The 11 primitives are not claimed to be a logically independent, unique, or complete classification of the world. A task may use a sufficient subset, or add domain variables after stating the reasons. Empirical support still comes from specific materials, G-instance, and the corresponding evidence procedures.

## 3.1 D0 Structural Object

Proposition: A candidate structural object is denoted S*=<B,X,R,I,T,SP,K>; the null model N0 is frozen only in a specific test instance.

Definition: D0 declares only a candidate object S*=<B,X,R,I,T,SP,K>. B is the boundary. X comprises the state variables. R specifies relations or transition constraints. I is the interface. T is the time window. SP is the scale profile. K is the identity criterion. This declaration makes the object available for discussion, but does not establish its validity; N0 belongs to the comparison contract of a specific G-instance.

The candidate object is written as:

S*=<B,X,R,I,T,SP,K>

B is the boundary, specifying which states and relations are included in the current object and which remain outside it.

X comprises registered state variables, not a synonym for “all actual states of the object.”

R specifies relations or state-transition constraints; endpoints, direction, time, and conditions of application must be stated.

I is the interface through which the object has inputs, outputs, filtering, transmission, or conversion with its exterior.

T is the time window used for observation, prediction, intervention, or feedback.

SP is the nine-axis scale profile. It records the object's positions in aggregation, space, time, organization, causation, observational resolution, influence, network topology, and jurisdiction.

K is the identity criterion, specifying which changes still allow the object to be recorded as the same object.

D0 declares only the candidate object for this analysis. The same object can serve different tasks; each task must separately specify the target being asked about, horizon, environment, admissible operations, error, and evidential requirements. The seven object fields must not be taken to imply that the object already suffices to answer every question. N0 is not entered into the object vector; it is specified within a particular comparative claim. Complete object fields do not establish a grouping gain; naming, clustering, drawing a boundary, and narrative coherence must not stand in for G1 evidence. Existing records, observations, or other reliable materials can support facts concerning specified people, organizations, events, and relations; their sources and identity conditions must be stated. That G1 has not yet been tested does not make these facts disappear, nor do these facts automatically establish that the grouping has a predictive gain, a unified subject, or a common will.

## 3.2 D1 Basic State Terms

Proposition: Change, operation, persistence, evolution, dissolution, and repair are each used according to pre-declared detectable criteria; evolution does not imply a path, direction, or value; repair must satisfy K or F, while its value and authorization undergo separate N/O review.

Definition: Change is a detectable difference in registered variables between two observation positions, without implying its cause, whether the change will last, or its direction. Operation is a state transition in X while B, R, I, SP, and K still hold under the current contract. Persistence is the current K continuing to hold within a specified time window. Evolution is a detectable change over time in the boundary, variable set, relations, interfaces, scale position, or identity conditions of an object contract, exceeding a declared fluctuation window; the definition itself does not include durable traces, path dependence, selection, direction, or value judgments. Dissolution means that the K declared in the original contract no longer holds. Repair is the formation of a new state satisfying a pre-declared target identity criterion K or functional criterion F; whether repair should occur, what its target should be, and who should carry it out must undergo separate review in the N and O layers.

D1 does not define “structure,” nor does it judge whether a D0 candidate object is valid. Complete object fields accomplish only a candidate declaration. Only a G1-instance that passes pre-registered thresholds can give the object limited empirical support relative to a complexity-matched N0, and that support is constrained by SP/T/K and the extrapolation unit. The applicability of state terms such as change, operation, or persistence must not in turn be used as proof of the object's validity. Persistence describes only the continuation of the current K within a specified window; it does not mean health, justification, progress, or that continuation is warranted.

Evolution registers only detectable changes in object-contract fields over time that exceed a predetermined fluctuation window; it does not imply durable traces, path dependence, historical determination, a selection mechanism, direction, maturity, progress, decline, or a value judgment. Whether historical conditional increments exist requires separate testing under G3; whether variation–differential retention–reproduction exists requires separately passing C8/CM-SELECTION. An evolution record can hold while G3 or a selection mechanism still does not hold.

Dissolution means that the specified K no longer holds. Dissolution may mean the object's termination, its conversion, or the need for reclassification; it does not automatically amount to failure. Repair is the formation of a new state satisfying a pre-declared target identity criterion K* or functional criterion F*. It can retain old components, or change boundaries, relations, interfaces, and carriers; it does not require a return to the historical state. Whether repair should occur, which K*/F* should be its target, who decides, and who carries it out must each undergo review in the N and O layers. These matters must not be authorized by the definition of “repair” on its own.

## 3.3 D2 Effective Feedback

Proposition: A prior state or output constitutes effective feedback only when it returns through a channel and changes a subsequent state, transition probability, or constraint.

Definition: A prior state or output returning through a traceable channel and producing a detectable difference in subsequent states, transition probabilities, temporal sequence, or constraints relative to the absence of that return is called effective feedback.

Effective feedback contains at least four traceable positions: the prior state or output, the return channel, the receiving position, and the subsequent fields that are changed. Reception of a signal, generation of a receipt, recording of an expression, or preservation of an archive establishes only arrival or a trace. Feedback is registered as effective only when subsequent states, transition probabilities, temporal sequence, or constraints show a detectable difference relative to the absence of return or to a blocked channel. D2 does not guarantee that feedback is accurate, timely, or beneficial, still less that it produces learning.

The effects of feedback, its stability, and improvement relative to a target must be kept separate. A return that has changed subsequent states can coexist with oscillation, amplification, or deviation from the target. Fixed rules can also produce feedback; retraining parameters is not a necessary condition. A claim of deviation correction must separately state the deviation from the target, gain, execution delay, operating range, and destabilization criteria. A claim of improvement must also evaluate transient peaks, cost, and distribution. Stability criteria for a specified linear model constrain only that model. They must not be generalized, merely on the strength of the term “negative feedback,” into a claim that real-world processes necessarily stabilize themselves.

## 3.4 D3 Scale Transformation

Proposition: A cross-scale judgment must declare M(SP0→SP1) and record what is retained, changed, and lost, the operator, error, and applicable range.

Definition: A scale transformation M(SP0→SP1) records the source and target scale profiles, retained variables, changed variables, lost variables, the conversion operator, error, and applicable range. Each sufficiency judgment must also specify the target query or task family, horizon, environment, and admissible operations. Static answers, dynamic progression, responses to intervention, and feasible action are judged separately.

A scale transformation is written as:

M(SP0→SP1)=<retained variables, changed variables, lost variables, conversion operator, error, applicable range>[^p03-tn-fields]

Using the same word at different scales does not constitute a scale transformation. An aggregated object is not simply an enlarged local object, and aggregate statistics must not be assigned back to individual local units.
