All variables retain interfaces for counterexamples, appeal, and rollback. Their `appeal` and `rollback` fields reference the shared `appeal_and_rollback_rule`. An appeal channel must be safely accessible and provide anti-retaliation protection. Depending on the risks and context, ways to submit anonymously, through a representative, with low exposure, or after a delay should be available. These allow people in low-power positions, people being evaluated, people taking up the work, people excluded, dissenters, and people unable to speak publicly to submit counterevidence. An appeal must also trigger a review independent of the original judgment or decision chain. The original decision maker must not confirm the decision on their own and then close the appeal. The variables may generate only these procedural requirements. They must not directly set up or operate the channels.

Once rollback is approved, it must actually change or restore the object, records, rules, resources, tasks, rights, or state of downstream judgments. It must also specify who is responsible for execution and the completion deadline, and it must retain the version history and verification of completion. Publishing a statement, accepting an appeal, or promising future improvements does not count as completing rollback. Judgment levels must not exceed evidence coverage, and action levels must not exceed authorization on the J axis. Even when H1, H4, and H5 receive instance support, they only increase the strength of description or explanation and do not automatically generate a normative conclusion. Any protection, harm reduction, repair, attribution of responsibility, resource reallocation, or formal action still must separately pass C12, rely on explicit N premises at runtime, obtain J authorization, and follow O1–O4 procedures.

## 7.5 Interface for Actor–Circle Mutual Constitution

When HV01–HV11 are applied in multi-circle operation, do not treat people as passive components of circles[^p08-tn-circle] or circles as shells for personality. Each actor retains slow, intermediate-timescale, and fast states and multiple roles. Each circle retains its boundary, members, channels, and K. Register the effects in both directions separately. Specify how a circle changes an actor through resources, information, roles, rewards and penalties, reputation, status, and exit conditions. Specify how an actor changes a circle through labor, expression, refusal, forming alliances, exit, bridging, and institutional write-back.

| Interface | Record for the Individual | Record for the Circle | Evidentiary Ceiling |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Entry and roles | Basis for membership, identification with the circle, conflict, and ability to exit | Qualifications, expectations, rights, and responsibilities | Membership does not establish that a person identifies with the circle |
| Resources and load | Body, time, funds, care, and capabilities | Allocation, capacity, externalization, and recovery | Shared pressure does not establish an identical response |
| Meaning and identity | Feelings, interpretation, commitments, shame, and hope | Shared narratives, legitimacy, memory, and boundaries | A public narrative does not represent all members |
| Feedback and write-back | Action, appeal, refusal, and exit | Whether records, rules, roles, and resources actually changed | Being received does not amount to effective write-back |

Personality hypotheses may serve only as candidates for an actor’s slow variables. S0–S6 may serve only as state prototypes for human intentional collectives. The two must not be applied to one another. An individual must not be labeled as a “lower stage,” and a member’s personality must not be used to infer a circle’s state. Materials about individuals may enter a judgment about a circle only through an explicit mechanism of aggregation, representation, or propagation, or an explicit institutional mechanism.

## 7.6 Practice, Institutional Reproduction, and Institutional Change

Human structures can change outside formal reforms and can be sustained without changes to fields. Actors continually interpret and invoke rules, allocate resources, handle exceptions, teach skills, and confirm eligibility. These practices can reproduce existing relations or gradually change how the structures actually operate. Written rules, public claims, everyday working rules, and actual consequences must be kept distinct. Practices can change while documents stay the same. Practices can also continue after documents change. These possibilities must be recorded separately. Formal write-back is still classified under H3. Everyday institutionalization and habit formation must not be equated with a single write-back on the basis of naming alone.

In analyzing a round of change, first reconstruct separately the rules, resources, and relations already in place, the actors’ projects and sets of available options under those conditions, the interactions that followed, and the arrangements later retained, reorganized, or interrupted. The next round may inherit results from the previous one, but later success must not be entered retroactively as an initial condition, and causation must not be inferred directly from temporal sequence. Those involved are not necessarily able to articulate fully the knowledge they exercise in skilled practice. Reflection and innovation are also constrained by time, resources, power, and others’ responses.

Generation and reproduction admit competing mechanisms. Compliance can arise from acceptance, dependence, the risk of punishment, or convention. Similar organizational forms can result from technological effects, coercive requirements, occupational training, or imitation of exemplars. Each explanation must identify the process materials that can distinguish it from competing explanations and state its failure conditions. Being constrained by conditions does not make individual action disappear. A structural explanation does not automatically absolve of responsibility those who are informed and genuinely able to choose.

[^p08-tn-contract]: Translator's note: *Contract* renders 合同 in an analytical sense: a specification registered and frozen before results are known, close to pre-registration. It is not a legal instrument.

[^p08-tn-circle]: Translator's note: *Circle* renders 圈层. Circles may be parallel, nested, overlapping, bridged, or competing, and may also form only temporarily around particular events; the term does not imply a hierarchy.

<a id="part_8"></a>

# Part 8 Human State Prototypes

## 8.1 What This Part Explains

S0–S6 apply only to human intentional collectives: teams, organizations, institutions, movements, communities, or governance arrangements that people form around a shared problem, commitment, goal, or arrangement of meaning that can be articulated, and that continue to operate through roles, resources, rules, relations, and feedback. The prototypes are not universal laws and must not be applied directly to individual personality, a purely physical system, the evolution of a species, or the universe as a whole. Even within their domain of application, S0–S6 are not a ladder of maturity or a required historical sequence, still less a ranking of value.

The numbers serve only to distinguish prototypes and make them easy to find. They permit us to ask, “What structural configuration predominates now in this object, in this subsystem, and within this time window?” They do not permit us to say, “The number is higher, so the people are better, the institutional arrangements more justified, or the civilization more advanced.” A large collective can have S5 at its center, S3 at its periphery, S6 in a technical unit, and still have S1 in its intergenerational uptake. The same function can also show two prototypes at once. Such cases should be registered as `parallel` or `mixed` rather than collapsing the complexity into a single “overall stage.”

A state prototype yields only a descriptive classification that can be withdrawn. It confers no authorization to act. Any real-world intervention must separately state its normative premises and pass C12. It must compare options including taking no action, obtain atomic J authorization covering the specific subject, object, action, geographic area, and validity period, and be subject to O procedures and to appeal and rollback. A state label must not be used as evidence for these checks.

## 8.2 Minimum Contract[^p09-tn-contract] for State Judgments

For each judgment, first freeze the object’s `object_id`, the identity criterion K, the nine-axis scale profile SP=<A,X,T,O,C,R,I,N,J>, the time window, and the scope of the subsystem. Then record entry conditions, exit conditions, observables, counterexamples, falsification results, and missing data separately. There are only four classification modes: `single_dominant`, `mixed`, `parallel`, and `unknown`. The freeze applies to the criterion for tracking the object in this analysis. It does not require observed membership, actual boundaries, roles, commitments, or self-identification to have stabilized. If the definition of K needs to change, a new versioned record should be created that preserves the original criterion, materials, and judgment. Object identity should then be checked again. Until that check is complete, records under different K must not be joined directly as a path of the same object across windows.

| Field | Required Question | Stopping Point |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Object and K | Are we tracking the same human collective, or has the object changed while keeping the same name? | If K is not frozen, cannot be used to track the object, or leaves object identity unresolved, assess the object and its identity first and assign no prototype. Changes in membership or actual boundaries do not automatically amount to a change in K |
| Scale and time | Which units, spaces, windows, levels, affected positions, and networks are visible? | Wider observation does not expand J authorization |
| Entry and exit | What materials make a prototype a candidate, and what materials show that it no longer predominates? | “Resembling” a prototype does not amount to having entered it or to being certain to exit it |
| Observables | Which behaviors, records, channels, costs, recovery, and write-back can be reviewed? | A slogan, aggregate total, or official self-report alone is insufficient |
| Counterexamples and falsification | What else could produce the same appearance? What result would lead to withdrawal of the judgment? | A label assigned without actively seeking counterevidence counts only as an impression |
| Evidence coverage | What external materials support the judgments about entry, observations, and counterevidence? Can the evidence be resolved, and does it cover the current object? | Internal definitions, IDs, and accounts provided by the framework must not substitute for evidence about the object |
| Missing data | Was it not collected, is it unobservable or inapplicable, are the sources in conflict, or is it unknown? | Missing data must not be filled in as “normal,” “absent,” or zero |
| Norms and action | Does the classification confer value, an obligation, or authority to act? | All states are `descriptive_only` and `action_authorization=none` |

The five missing-data statuses must be kept separate. `not_collected` means that the data can be obtained but have not been collected. `not_observable` means that they are unobservable under the current permissions, safety conditions, or measurement conditions. Subjects in vulnerable positions must not be required to expose themselves to complete a form. `not_applicable` requires an applicability check. `conflicted` means that qualifying sources conflict with one another. `unknown` means that attempts have been made and the distinction still cannot be made. A missing key entry, counterevidence, or scale field by itself supports only `unknown` or suspension of the relevant classification. `mixed` and `parallel` each require positive evidence of multiple prototypes and their corresponding positions. Lack of knowledge must not be renamed as a mixture. Local materials already established are still retained within their original scope.

### 8.2.1 Runtime Classification Record

A prototype card yields a reviewable classification only when entered in a closed runtime record. The record must freeze all of the following together: `record_id`, the object and its identity criterion, the nine-axis scale, time window, subsystem scope, classification mode, candidate states, candidate paths, results for the five kinds of conditions, missing data, evidence about the object, uncertainty, independent review, appeal, rollback, and the boundary of non-authorization. Each entry, exit, observation, counterexample, and falsification result uses the same structure, `item_id + state_id + status + evidence_refs + reason`. The results must cover each candidate prototype item by item. A single overall assessment must not substitute for them.

`single_dominant` has exactly one candidate. `mixed` and `parallel` each require at least two candidates and an explicit record of the corresponding paths. `unknown` assigns no supported prototype and must explain which key materials are still insufficient to distinguish the prototypes. Local facts and observations of components already established remain in the evidence about the object. They must not be deleted on the ground that the overall prototype is unknown. If the runtime record does not allow supporting items in the candidate conditions field for `unknown`, the materials should be kept in the evidence about the object and the rationale, and a complete prototype must not be fabricated. At a minimum, each candidate requires evidence about the object for entry and observation items. Once any falsification item is `supported`, the candidate is blocked. It must not be kept in the record as a candidate by weakening the wording. Each validation must also be supplied with an external `evidence_ids` registry. At every level of the record, `evidence_refs` must resolve to evidence about the object in the record and recursively to that external registry. A missing registry, a top-level self-report, or a reference that resolves to nothing must not pass. Internal IDs, definitions, and self-reported judgments must not substitute for evidence about the object.

The reviewer record has the structure `reviewer_id + independent + reviewed_at + material_version + outcome`. Appeal and rollback each link to a structured procedure ID and status. After NFKC normalization, removal of leading and trailing whitespace, and case normalization, `reviewer_id` must differ from the object ID and must not be an alias derived from it. A different string cannot turn the object itself into an independent reviewer. Review, appeal, and rollback must not be represented by self-reported strings such as “reviewed” or “appeal available.” Regardless of the outcome of review, the record always has `normative_status=descriptive_only` and `action_authorization=none`. When a state label is withdrawn or rewritten, the original evidence, objections, and chain of versions should be preserved.

The U, H, G, and C IDs serve four distinct purposes in this Part. U provides interfaces for objects, states, channels, load, and phases. H supplies adaptations for meaning, uptake, institutional write-back, power, and historical carriers in the human world. G specifies when upgrading a pattern to an empirical mechanism requires a qualified independent instance. C specifies where inference must stop. These contract links supply no evidence about the object at hand.

Descriptions of S0–S6 configurations do not require the multiple-interval pattern of CM-PHASE. An object that meets the necessary conditions of one prototype throughout the observed window may still receive a corresponding limited classification. That classification does not establish that a phase transition has occurred. A further claim about multiple intervals, a threshold, or a mechanism of phase transition is checked under CM-PHASE and the relevant G instance. Conversely, establishing a formal pattern with two intervals does not by itself show that an object fits a particular human collective prototype.

## 8.3 S0–S6 State Prototypes

### 8.3.1 S0 Prototype of Coalescence Around an Anchor

S0 describes “a candidate collective taking shape,” not “a low-grade organization.” A shared problem, candidate goal, or expression of meaning begins to draw together attention, relationships, time, and small amounts of resources. Membership, actual boundaries, roles, ways of making commitments, and expressions of shared identity are still forming or changing. The analysis must still use a K frozen for this analysis and capable of tracking the object. Changes in a state that is taking shape do not substitute for a judgment about identity. At least one candidate generative actor repeatedly translates, convenes, tries things out, or bears actual costs. An independent uptake layer has yet to form. A candidate collective may disperse, go dormant, be incorporated into another arrangement, or turn to a new goal at this point. Estimating how often each path occurs requires the relevant population of opportunities and observational material. The rates must not be inferred from the prototype definition. The process ending does not by itself constitute a finding of failure.

| Aspect | S0 Diagnostic Card[^p09-tn-diagnosis] |
| --- | --- |
| Entry | `S0-E1` Under a K frozen for this analysis and capable of tracking the object, a shared problem or arrangement of meaning produces visible local clustering, but membership, actual boundaries, or ways of making commitments have yet to stabilize. `S0-E2` Candidate generative action recurs and uptake remains thin. |
| Exit | `S0-X1` While the same K remains applicable and object identity is traceable within the windows, the anchor, actual boundaries, and generative roles stabilize across windows, and traceable commitments and early uptake appear. S1 may be tested. `S0-X2` The collective disperses, splits, is absorbed, or goes dormant and need not pass through S1. If the original K no longer applies, assess object conversion or termination separately. |
| Observables | `S0-O1` Competing formulations are still under negotiation. `S0-O2` Resources cluster amid high rates of entry and exit. `S0-O3` Generative actors bear the work of translation, convening, or trial and error. `S0-O4` Failed attempts and the history of alternatives are still retained. |
| Counterexamples | A trending topic or platform recommendation can create short-lived synchronization. Administrative naming can create a formal team. Observe whether commitments and object boundaries still persist after rewards, exposure, or orders are removed. |
| Falsification | Withdraw S0 if shared direction, the clustering of action, and generative roles all fail to differ from those in a matched temporary gathering. S0 also does not predominate if stable roles, uptake, and repeated operation have long been in place. |
| Scale | Generative actors, early participants, potential leavers, and outside parties who assign the name must be distinguished. Individual personalities must not be aggregated into a collective state. |
| Evidence for stronger claims | A difference in resources, action, or coordination caused by an arrangement of meaning requires an H1-instance. Formation caused by a specified channel requires a G2-instance. Check historical traces under H5 and obtain evidence for future effects of current carriers under §4.5. Add G3 only for a historical increment beyond the current representation. |
| Contract links | U01/U02/U03/U08/U09/U11, H1/H2/H5, G1/G2/G3, C1/C2/C4/C5/C10/C12. |

S0 permits only a finding of compatibility with the prototype of coalescence around an anchor for the specified object, scale, and window. It cannot establish that participants lack capability, value, or willingness, and it must not be used to authorize support, dissolution, screening, or exclusion.

### 8.3.2 S1 Prototype of a Generative Core Taking Shape

Under S1, a candidate anchor, object boundaries, and at least one generative role can already be identified repeatedly across decisions. Basic rules, language, resource channels, and the first carriers of memory are taking shape. The main work of translation, decision-making, resource mobilization, conflict handling, or repair of relationships remains concentrated in one or a few generative roles. Independent parties able to take up the work are scarce. Centrality is structural dependence, not proof that the leadership is justified.

| Aspect | S1 Diagnostic Card |
| --- | --- |
| Entry | `S1-E1` K, the anchor, and the generative roles can be identified repeatedly. `S1-E2` Key functions depend on the generative core on an ongoing basis, rather than through a single emergency concentration. |
| Exit | `S1-X1` New entrants form sustained arrangements for uptake, responsibility, and boundary interfaces. S2 may be tested. `S1-X2` The generative core splits, exits, is taken over, or goes dormant, or the object disperses. The case then moves to the corresponding path. |
| Observables | `S1-O1` The anchor and boundaries show initial stability. `S1-O2` Key decisions depend on the generative core. `S1-O3` Rules and carriers of memory begin to recur. `S1-O4` Entry from outside is limited and targeted. |
| Counterexamples | A founder may still announce decisions publicly in a mature organization even though the actual uptake layer is already extensive. A newly created administrative unit may be only a role unit of a higher-level object. Trace actual execution, rights to stop, feedback, and operation when the core is temporarily absent. |
| Falsification | Dependence on the core is refuted if uptake across multiple centers remains stable with little involvement from the generative core. If the anchor is unstable but the same K still applies and the object can be tracked, S0 may be retested. If the original K no longer applies or object identity is unclear, object conversion or termination should be checked first, or `unknown` retained, before objects that can be identified are classified. |
| Scale | Freeze the record of generative roles, early parties taking up the work, higher-level parties who assign the name, and external supporters. Record `conflicted` when the organizational chart conflicts with actual channels. |
| Evidence for stronger claims | Effects of the anchor and of the generative core on outcomes require H1 and G2, respectively. Obtain evidence under §4.5 for carrier effects of early institutions or trauma, test traces under H5, and test historical conditional residuals separately under G3. |
| Contract links | U01/U02/U03/U04/U06/U07/U09/U11, H1/H2/H3/H4, G1/G2/G3, C1/C2/C3/C5/C9/C10/C11/C12. |

S1 must not be read as “immature” or “backward,” or as something that “should be integrated.” It must not be used to give generative actors standing to lead permanently, to expand their power, to impose institutional arrangements, or to replace others.

### 8.3.3 S2 Prototype of Initial Expansion and Differentiated Uptake

S2 describes continued expansion of boundaries, membership, the population served, geographic reach, platform interfaces, or networks of collaboration. New entrants bring different understandings, skills, resources, and risks. Positions outside the generative core begin to emerge for taking up work, exiting, maintaining dissent, and participation that remains unresolved. Expansion can improve the capability to coordinate and also adds problems involving translation, feedback, recovery, and power. Disagreement by itself does not amount to destabilization. There are grounds to describe a structural problem only if an observable mechanism persistently blocks uptake, feedback, or recovery.

| Aspect | S2 Diagnostic Card |
| --- | --- |
| Entry | `S2-E1` At least one kind of boundary continues to expand and changes relations. `S2-E2` Distinguishable positions of uptake, cost, exit, and unresolved participation emerge outside the generative core. |
| Exit | `S2-X1` Repeated loads and pressures produce interconnected constraints that persistently divide channels. S3 may be tested. `S2-X2` Expansion stops or recedes, or the object jumps, splits, merges, is taken over, goes dormant, or collapses. |
| Observables | `S2-O1` The pattern of entries and exits and the composition of interfaces change. `S2-O2` Roles and responsibilities differentiate. `S2-O3` Feedback volume, translation costs, time lags, and recovery needs change. `S2-O4` Identification with the group, dissent, exit, and safety are recorded separately. |
| Counterexamples | Growth in membership or user counts does not necessarily change the structure. Conflict becoming public may also reflect improved safety of expression that finally makes old problems visible. |
| Falsification | With the statistical definitions frozen, withdraw S2 if growth is not sustained and changes none of the boundaries, roles, responsibilities, interfaces, or loads. The classification should also be withdrawn if the supposed differentiation comes solely from labels assigned by the center. |
| Scale | The whole, the center, new parties taking up the work, leavers, and externalized costs must all be included. Wider observation does not increase J permissions over new members. |
| Evidence for stronger claims | Differences in outcomes caused by expansion or by a channel require G2/CAUSAL. Cross-scale claims separately go through D3/E5, and claims of closure or conversion are tested separately under G4. Coordination by an anchor requires H1. Observational effects of expression, disclosure, and power require H4. Historical traces require H5, carrier transmission is checked under §4.5, and historical conditional residuals are tested separately under G3. |
| Contract links | U01/U02/U03/U04/U06/U07/U08/U09/U11, H1/H2/H3/H4/H5, G1/G2/G3/G4, C1/C2/C3/C4/C5/C6/C7/C9/C10/C11/C12. |

S2 does not permit labeling people who do not identify with the collective, who leave, or whose participation is unclear as “negative subjects” on that basis alone. Nor does it permit a “need for dimensional expansion” to be used to authorize enforcing uniform beliefs, restricting exit, or excluding dissenters.

### 8.3.4 S3 Prototype of Development Under Interconnected Constraints

Under S3 the collective still operates or develops, but several recurring constraints have become connected. Approvals, resources, trust, responsibility, information, platforms, or external dependencies form mutually reinforcing bottlenecks that persistently fragment feedback, uptake, recovery, or the return flow of costs. What matters here is not that “there are many problems” but whether the problems form a connected structure that can be reviewed. Nor is it a matter of naming any person as a barrier.

| Aspect | S3 Diagnostic Card |
| --- | --- |
| Entry | `S3-E1` At least two kinds of constraint reinforce one another and affect channels across windows. `S3-E2` Operation still continues, but uptake at the downstream end, feedback, resources, or recovery bear a sustained extra load. |
| Exit | `S3-X1` Key relations, permissions, resources, or interfaces begin to be reconfigured in practice. S4 may be tested. `S3-X2` The object enters regression, a pseudo-steady state, dormancy, takeover, forced rewrite, migration, or collapse. |
| Observables | `S3-O1` A network of constraints and bottlenecks. `S3-O2` Output persists while losses, rework, and hidden labor increase. `S3-O3` Repair is repeatedly started but does not write back. `S3-O4` The center and periphery, metrics and uptake, and nominal and actual arrangements remain divided. |
| Counterexamples | Protective layering and redundancy can improve reliability in a high-risk environment. Deliberate load reduction for reconfiguration can also slow output temporarily. It must be checked whether the restrictions block necessary feedback or provide error isolation and alternative paths. |
| Falsification | After excluding common inputs, measurement bias, and short-term peaks, S3 does not predominate if the constraints are not connected or if alternative channels, write-back, and recovery at the downstream end of uptake remain effective. |
| Scale | The center, periphery, functional units, downstream uptake positions, and affected positions outside the object must be separated, and `parallel` is permitted. The disappearance of weak signals must not be treated as the disappearance of the problems. |
| Evidence for stronger claims | Masking by power or mediation requires H4. Specified channels and mutual reinforcement require G2. Examine current constraints formed through history under §4.5 and test additional historical residuals under G3. Failure of cross-scale closure requires G4. |
| Contract links | U02/U03/U05/U06/U07/U08/U09/U10/U11, H1/H2/H3/H4/H5, G2/G3/G4, C2/C3/C4/C5/C6/C7/C9/C10/C11/C12. |

An S3 classification must not be used to authorize “breaking barriers,” purges, takeover, forced reform, or shifting transition costs to vulnerable positions. It indicates only that the constraint network and competing explanations should be distinguished further.

### 8.3.5 S4 Prototype of a Window for Structural Reconfiguration

S4 does not mean “the reform is already right”; it means that key structures are actually in a window for reconfiguration. Old relations, permissions, resource channels, feedback write-back, or boundary interfaces are being dismantled, replaced, or bypassed. Old and new arrangements coexist. There are conversion costs, duplicated work, knowledge transfer, short-term declines in output, and growing recovery needs. The outcome remains open. It can be improvement, stagnation, alienation, forced rewriting, or collapse.

| Aspect | S4 Diagnostic Card |
| --- | --- |
| Entry | `S4-E1` At least one key field actually changes, not merely through renaming or publicity. `S4-E2` Old and new arrangements coexist, transition costs can be traced, and K continues to be checked. |
| Exit | `S4-X1` Channels, feedback, uptake, and recovery improve across windows without concealed outsourcing. S5 may be tested. `S4-X2` The new structure is unusable, and the object enters dormancy, regression, takeover, forced rewriting, a pseudo-steady state, or collapse. |
| Observables | `S4-O1` Changes in permissions, resources, roles, responsibilities, or stopping conditions are actually written back. `S4-O2` Dual systems and conversion costs. `S4-O3` Buffers, redundancy, cooldown, stopping, and rollback. `S4-O4` Outcomes from multiple positions enter actual feedback. |
| Counterexamples | Large-scale renaming, replacing people, or redrawing flowcharts may be symbolic reform. Dismantling old arrangements, overload, and declining output may also reflect a destructive purge or withdrawal of resources. |
| Falsification | Withdraw S4 if actual relations, permissions, resources, and execution do not change. If no usable new structure exists and functions remain disrupted, the process must not be presented as reconfiguration that brings improvement. |
| Scale | Cover the windows for the pre-change baseline, transition, cooldown, rollback, and recovery. Distinguish those who initiate reform, those who execute it, those who take up the work, and those who bear the costs. |
| Evidence for stronger claims | A claim that reconfiguration causes improvement or harm requires G2/CAUSAL. Object or intervention conversion requires G4. Whether failed attempts affect the future through a current carrier is assessed under §4.5, and additional historical residuals are tested separately under G3. A “tunneling” analogy must not substitute for that evidence. |
| Contract links | U03/U05/U06/U07/U09/U10/U11, H1/H2/H3/H4/H5, G2/G3/G4, C2/C3/C4/C5/C6/C7/C9/C10/C11/C12. |

A “transition valley” is at most a description to be tested. It must not be used to justify an unlimited price, irreversible harm, the removal of appeal, or sacrifices imposed on those in low-power positions. S4 confers no authorization for dismantling, purges, or forced transfer.

### 8.3.6 S5 Prototype of Efficient Coordination and Flexible Expansion

S5 describes coordination with relatively low losses among channels for resources, information, feedback, responsibility, uptake, and recovery in the current environment and time window. Local disturbances usually fail to produce lasting interconnected barriers. Boundaries can expand and contract with some flexibility. Resource advantages, costs shifted through outsourcing, silence, whitewashed metrics, or institutional loopholes can still make these conditions appear to hold when they do not. High output is therefore insufficient evidence, and long-term persistence is no proof of justification.

| Aspect | S5 Diagnostic Card |
| --- | --- |
| Entry | `S5-E1` Key channels operate with low losses across windows, can be substituted, and permit write-back. `S5-E2` Expansion, preservation, and dissipation work together without relying on closing off feedback or suppressing dissent. |
| Exit | `S5-X1` Distributed uptake, self-renewal, recovery, and new generation hold across disturbances. S6 may be tested. `S5-X2` Loopholes, alienation, a pseudo-steady state, regression, functional transfer, X0, takeover, or collapse cause the prototype to stop predominating. |
| Observables | `S5-O1` Alternative uptake arrangements and channels are actually usable. `S5-O2` Output, feedback, recovery, and maintenance all continue. `S5-O3` Boundaries are open, protection and exit can be implemented, and costs flow back. `S5-O4` Audits of loopholes can produce write-back. |
| Counterexamples | Resource abundance, selective metrics, failed complaint channels, compliance out of fear, or shifting maintenance debt into the future can all create the appearance of “success.” |
| Falsification | Withdraw S5 if the efficiency advantage disappears after maintenance, recovery, exit, and external costs are included, or if local failures cascade rapidly when the center is minimally involved. |
| Scale | Management totals must be broken down to the positions of maintainers, people taking up the work with little power, leavers, and external positions affected. Cover high and low loads and the windows for disturbance, recovery, and maintenance. |
| Evidence for stronger claims | Causes of efficient coordination and effects of loopholes require G2/H4. Historical traces, sustained carrier effects, and additional residuals are assessed under H5, §4.5, and G3, respectively. Replication across scales requires D3/E5 mapping and independent evidence at the target. Claims of closure or conversion are tested separately under G4. |
| Contract links | U01–U11, H1/H2/H3/H4/H5, G1/G2/G3/G4, C1–C12. |

S5 does not label an object as “good,” “mature,” or “ready to export its model.” Persistence, growth, and selection do not amount to justification, and they must not be used to authorize expansion, replication, takeover, or demands that members bear more.

### 8.3.7 S6 Prototype of Adaptive Dynamic Balance

S6 describes a collective with distributed uptake, effective feedback, and the capability to recover, update, and generate anew. Ordinary fluctuations do not lock in its direction for long. Autonomous units can stabilize locally, coordinate laterally, and receive backing from a higher level. A pool of potential successors, memory attached to positions, authorization, and handovers allow newcomers actually to enter and modify existing arrangements. The collective maintains function through continual calibration, not by eliminating fluctuations.

| Aspect | S6 Diagnostic Card |
| --- | --- |
| Entry | `S6-E1` With little involvement from the center, multiple positions can still sense changes, correct errors, recover, and write back. `S6-E2` People taking up the work, rules, and boundaries can be renewed continually without dependence on the same irreplaceable individuals. |
| Exit | `S6-X1` Slower recovery, larger fluctuations, failed write-back, dependence on the center, or interruption of uptake leads to regression or another path. `S6-X2` The end of the mission, functional transfer, a split, a merger, paradigm replacement, or X0 changes the object contract. |
| Observables | `S6-O1` Recovery and renewed destabilization after a disturbance. `S6-O2` Coordination among multiple centers and backup arrangements. `S6-O3` New people taking up the work go from training to taking charge of it in practice. `S6-O4` Feedback, audits, appeals, and rule revisions maintain a closed loop. |
| Counterexamples | Strong control, disappearance of weak signals, protection of resources, or concealed firefighting by the center can all create low fluctuation. Frequent personnel changes and reorganizations may simply treat staff as expendable. |
| Falsification | Under comparable disturbances, withdraw S6 if recovery keeps slowing, local failures cascade, correction depends on a single point, or successors cannot obtain knowledge, permissions, and protection. |
| Scale | Cover windows for disturbances, recovery, and succession, and long windows for slow variables. Overall averages must not be allowed to mask harm at the periphery. Freeze the record of autonomy, lateral coordination, backing from higher levels, and actual authorization. |
| Evidence for stronger claims | Effects of regulation, feedback, and arrangements with multiple centers require G2/CAUSAL. For learning, institutional memory, and intergenerational uptake, first check carrier retention and actual continuity. Test traces under H5 and additional historical increments under G3. Cross-scale extrapolation requires D3/E5 and independent target evidence. Claims of closure or conversion are tested separately under G4. |
| Contract links | U01–U11, H1/H2/H3/H4/H5, G1/G2/G3/G4, C1–C12. |

S6 does not mark an absolute final state, a society without conflict, or the end of history. A collective can regress, split, be taken over, transfer functions, enter X0, or collapse. The classification must not be used to obtain decision-making authority over others for any object.

## 8.4 Library of Nonlinear Paths

State prototypes avoid reverting to a stage theory only when they are used together with the path library. A path describes “how a state configuration or object contract changes.” It does not describe “how better people advance to a higher level.” Several paths can be candidates within the same window. When evidence is insufficient, retain `unknown`.

| Path ID | Meaning | Key Distinction |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `mixed` | Necessary features of two or more prototypes occur together for the same object, scale, and window | If the differences can be mapped consistently to separate subsystems, the record should use `parallel` |
| `parallel` | Different regions, levels, functions, or positions at the center or periphery are in different states at the same time | The states must not be collapsed into an “overall stage.” Preserve K, evidence, and exit conditions for each subsystem separately |
| `jump` | Adjacent observation windows show a shift from one configuration to a nonadjacent one, with no discernible window of persistence in an intermediate prototype | First rule out overly sparse sampling and missing material. Record skipped functions, the price paid, and unknowns |
| `regression` | One or more functions deteriorate relative to the baseline of the same object | Distinguish abrupt and gradual decline, jumps at thresholds, and cascades. Deliberate load reduction, X0, and functional transfer do not count as regression |
| `split` | The original object divides into two or more candidate objects with different K | Temporary project groupings and modularization do not necessarily change the object. Register each new object anew |
| `merge` | Multiple objects form a new object or are absorbed by one of them | An alliance need not constitute a merger. The weaker party’s boundary, memory, exit, and outstanding responsibilities must be listed separately |
| `dormancy` | Visible activity decreases while memory, relationships, symbols, capabilities, or conditions for reactivation remain | Distinguish protective withdrawal, stagnation from exhaustion, disappearance of material, and actual collapse |
| `takeover` | An external subject gains control of key resources, permissions, or interfaces | External support or oversight need not constitute a takeover if genuine autonomy and the right to stop are preserved |
| `functional_transfer` | A core function moves to another object. The function can continue while the original identity, relationships, or memory disappear | Outsourcing one task does not amount to a transfer. Functional continuity does not establish continuity of responsibility or memory |
| `paradigm_replacement` | A new object with a different K, interfaces, or rules bypasses the old object without repairing it | An upgrade within the same object or a change of brand name need not constitute paradigm replacement. Outstanding obligations remain |
| `trauma_built_development` | Boundaries, identity, rules, or ways of taking up work form under harm, deprivation, failure, or threat | A narrative of trauma does not automatically establish future effects. Whether the arrangement provides protection and whether it reproduces the original harm must be tested separately |
| `pseudo_steady` | Surface indicators are stable while actual uptake is being exhausted, weak signals disappear, feedback fails to write back, or debts increase | Keep two ledgers, one for appearances and one for actual conditions. Revisit costs, recovery, the right to stop, and feedback from the downstream end |
| `forced_rewrite` | A subject with greater power rewrites the object when correctable consent, exit, and protection of memory are lacking | Distinguish this from authorized, negotiated revision that can be withdrawn and appealed. Check for irreversible erasure of identity |
| `external_carrier_migration` | When repair is closed off, personnel, capabilities, resources, relationships, evidence, or memory move to a safer system for taking them up | A lack of capability to receive what is transferred, romanticizing departure, or privatizing harm does not constitute safe migration |
| `collapse` | The original K ceases to hold, and functions, uptake, relationships, or boundaries break down without a plan | Distinguish collapse from X0, functional transfer, protective dormancy, and object reorganization. Releasing resources does not justify harm |

Path names likewise do not confer value or impose responsibility. For example, `regression` does not amount to moral decline, `split` is not necessarily failure, `merge` is not necessarily progress, `takeover` is not automatically unlawful, and `external_carrier_migration` does not require individuals to flee on their own. Paths only allow competing explanations into the record. Mechanisms, justification, and action still require their own contracts.

## 8.5 X0 Orderly Exit and Benign Demise

X0 does not designate an eighth state or S7, and it is not counted as an eighth stage. It is an independent life-cycle path for human collectives: when maintaining the original object no longer serves the registered anchor, its core functions can be completed, terminated, or taken up more safely, and personnel, relationships, resources, memory, debts, and appeals can be dealt with explicitly, the path records how the object closes out its mission, transfers functions, releases resources, preserves memory, settles outstanding responsibilities, and retains restricted weak ties.

X0 requires neither S5 nor S6. A temporary initiative in S0 can close in an orderly way when it finds that the problem is already being taken up elsewhere. An expansion project in S2 can wind down if continuing to expand would only create harm. An institution stuck in S3 may also begin its exit if repair is infeasible and external uptake is sufficient. Conversely, a collective in S6 must not declare itself eligible to disband on the strength of a “higher-stage” label. Capabilities at a stage, normative justification, and operational conditions are three distinct matters. No state ID may become a prerequisite for eligibility for self-initiated exit.

The machine contract encodes this boundary with `origin_policy` set to `any_registered_origin`. There is no minimum state requirement. `required_state_ids` is empty, and `s5_or_s6_prerequisite` is always `false`. The entry and completion conditions for X0 must not refer to S0–S6 as eligibility requirements. The IDs may only describe the starting position and must not become a threshold for exit.

### 8.5.1 Candidate Entry for X0

First, the anchor has been fulfilled, ceased to apply, or been taken up by a safer object, or continuing to maintain the object chiefly serves the organization’s own persistence. This judgment must be independently reviewable. The center must not certify completion for itself after rewriting the goal. Second, personnel, functions, resources, relationships, records, debts, and irreplaceable values have been inventoried, and there are verifiable receiving parties or protective arrangements. Short-term setbacks, tight funding, leadership fatigue, or evasion of responsibility can create similar appearances. Satisfying the narrative alone therefore must not lead to a finding of “benign completion.”

### 8.5.2 Completion Conditions for X0

| Aspect of Completion | What Must Be Preserved | Signs of Failure |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Anchor and object | Independent verification of completion, termination, or invalidity. The time when K ends and matters still open | Redefining the goal, deleting failures, or the center unilaterally declaring completion |
| Core functions | Mark each as completed, terminated, transferred, or unresolved. Record the receiving party’s capability, permissions, resources, and rollback provisions | A declaration alone, with no continuity for maintainers or users |
| Personnel and relationships | Safety, basic rights and interests, the right to stop, protection against retaliation, and arrangements for continuing care and relationships of dependence | Privatizing organizational obligations by leaving members to bear them on their own |
| Resources | Traceable objects, boundaries, sequence, timing, and destinations to prevent capture by those with greater power | Dividing resources internally first, then leaving the losses to low-power positions |
| Memory and evidence | Preserve successes, failures, repairs, and memory attached to positions. Specify access, privacy, correction, and protection against retaliation | Keeping only a record of victories while deleting or exposing materials from vulnerable positions |
| Debts and responsibility | Continuing responsible parties for contracts, labor, data, maintenance, environmental matters, appeals, and future risks | Responsibility, review, and maintenance all disappear when the object is deregistered |
| Weak ties | Limited to advice, shared experience, and buffering, with clear boundaries and duration | Reviving the original power structure without new authorization |

X0 outcomes should at least distinguish “candidate,” “in progress,” “partially complete,” “complete,” and `unknown`. If anything about the affected groups, outstanding obligations, the receiving party’s capability, appeals, or the safety of memory is still unknown, the result must not be recorded as “benign completion.” A transfer of functions without continuity of memory and responsibility may be only `functional_transfer`. Unilateral closure and rewriting of identity by an external power may be `takeover` or `forced_rewrite`. A breakdown with no uptake is `collapse` and must not be dressed up as “orderly exit.”

An X0 classification grants no authorization to dissolve the object, abolish posts or units, transfer assets, publish archives, waive debts, or consent on members’ behalf. It identifies only the objects, obligations, evidence, protections, and unknowns that must be entered in the same ledger to judge whether an exit is orderly.

## 8.6 How Regulation, Early Warning, and Uptake Enter State Judgments

States are not determined from static snapshots. The regulation layer requires observing the target, sensing, comparison, execution, feedback, and time lag. The early-warning layer requires checking whether slower recovery, larger fluctuations, more severe consequences of similar disturbances, and local flickering form a cluster. The repayment layer requires tracing who makes commitments, who bears the costs, to whom returns flow, and whose costs or claims are deferred to the future. If the apparent stability of S5 or S6 depends on off-ledger labor, silence, and future debt, the case should be treated as a candidate for `pseudo_steady`.

Clues generated by the same sequence or shared channel of change do not become independent pieces of evidence merely because they cluster. Dependent clues may still be used jointly. Their value for early warning must be evaluated under compatible dynamics, noise, sampling, and observation windows, against fixed targets, horizons, real-time baselines, and signals in the opposite direction (Scheffer et al., 2009). See D.31 and §12.6.

Clustering is not a universal prerequisite for early warning. A single domain-specific signal can also be sufficient for a limited judgment. The absence of critical slowing down or a second signal does not prove safety or invalidate an existing specialist prediction. A joint rule requires a joint distribution or corresponding validation. An increase in its positive predictive value still does not guarantee a reduction in total loss given false alarms, missed detections, and action costs.

Uptake, too, must not be judged only by whether “there is a successor.” Check whether a pool of potential successors, shadow roles, memory attached to positions, authorization paths, handover windows, translation of legitimacy, and protective conditions are actually in place. A clean exit by generative actors leaves a vacuum of authority if those taking up the work cannot enter. Frequent changes in personnel do not amount to adaptive renewal if key knowledge and permissions remain locked in the original center. Use H2 to distinguish uptake carriers, cost bearers, beneficiaries, rights to stop, and responsible parties. Use H3 to determine whether feedback actually changes fields and whether those changes are executed. Use H4 to examine power and observation channels, H5 to verify traces, and §4.5 to test actual continuity through the carrier. If a historical increment beyond the current representation is also claimed, test it under G3.

## 8.7 Limits and Exclusions of the Current Prototypes

S0–S6 are prototypes of human collective states. A proposed classification can be refuted, can lack necessary data, can apply in parallel, and can be withdrawn. Each candidate must be checked item by item for entry, exit, observations, counterexamples, falsification, scale, evidence, missing data, and the boundary of non-authorization. If these conditions are missing, the candidate may be retained only as an impression or as unknown. The numbers do not represent a universal evolutionary path from 0 to 6. Regression, parallelism, mixing, jumps, dormancy, splits, mergers, takeover, functional transfer, and collapse are permitted between states.

X0 is an independent life-cycle path and does not depend on S5, S6, or any minimum state. Capabilities at a stage, normative justification, and the operating conditions for exit must be judged separately. No match to a prototype may by itself generate authorization for dissolution, advancement, elimination, or forced conversion.

A physical metaphor must supply a formal mapping, the dimensions of the quantities involved, falsifiable predictions, and conditions for ending the analogy to enter the formal mechanism library. For example, “quantum tunneling” cannot explain why a human collective jumps to another configuration. In this framework, recording failed attempts as a historical conditional increment beyond the currently registered state still requires support from the corresponding G3-instance (see §4.5). No necessary coordination is presumed between biological death and social renewal, or between love and intergenerational transmission. Intergenerational uptake is recorded only through roles, memory, authorization, and handover interfaces.

The seven prototypes make no claim to exhaust the states of human collectives. If cases consistently fall outside them, the unexplained residuals should first be preserved. Independent materials should then be used to test whether prototypes need to be added, merged, or split, rather than the cases being forced into the numbered categories.

Prototype fit, interobserver agreement, stability across windows, and practical gains over using variables directly are distinct questions. Clear cards can make a classification easier to review but do not establish that it is reliable or more effective than a specialist description. Without actual comparisons, do not report accuracy, predictive gains, or savings in reading time. If the task calls only for direct description of members, channels, and load, the prototypes may be skipped.

## 8.8 Boundary With Actor States and Multi-Circle Projection

S0–S6 describe candidate states of human intentional collectives under a specified object contract. They do not describe individual personality, psychological maturity, or moral rank. Part 9 covers actors’ slow, intermediate-timescale, and fast variables. Part 10 covers parallel, nested, overlapping, bridging, competitive, and temporary relations among multiple collectives. Classifying a circle[^p09-tn-circle] to which a person belongs as S2 does not imply that the person’s personality is at S2. A person displaying stable capability likewise does not imply that their circle is in S5 or S6.

Multi-circle projection can represent different circles as fitting different prototypes at the same time. It can also represent the same circle as having a mixed state on different scale axes. State changes must be tied to events, channels, and time windows. An institutional change in an outer layer must not automatically update inner relations, and individual exit does not automatically cause the collective to dissolve. If prototype classification adds no value beyond using the variables directly, the prototypes should be skipped in favor of a simpler joint state.

[^p09-tn-contract]: Translator's note: *Contract* renders 合同 in an analytical sense: a specification registered and frozen before results are known, close to pre-registration. It is not a legal instrument.

[^p09-tn-diagnosis]: Translator's note: Diagnosis here denotes a nonclinical assessment of a collective configuration against the stated criteria.

[^p09-tn-circle]: Translator's note: *Circle* renders 圈层. Circles may be parallel, nested, overlapping, bridged, or competing, and may also form only temporarily around particular events; the term does not imply a hierarchy.

<a id="part_9"></a>

# Part 9 Actor States and Personality Hypotheses

This Part brings people into multi-circle projection without reducing anyone to a fixed label. An actor in an event brings long-term tendencies that change relatively slowly. They occupy roles and positions in relationships and access to resources that are specific to a period. Immediate emotions, bodily states, information, and opportunities also affect them. The same person may act differently in different circles[^p10-tn-circle]. These differences need not be explained by hypocrisy, a split in personality, or a change in essence. They may also arise from changes in role activation, risk structure, visible information, rewards and penalties, ability to exit, and affected positions.

## 9.1 An Actor Is Not a Single Variable

The minimum form of an actor record is:

`A* = <actor ID, observation window, circle memberships, roles, slow variables, intermediate-timescale variables, fast variables, material conditions, experience–meaning conditions, unknowns, evidential status, privacy and permitted uses>`.[^p10-tn-actor-tuple]

The asterisk indicates that this remains a candidate state description. No actor’s name, occupation, social status, or single action may automatically fill in their state. Information that is unobservable, has no source, is withheld because those concerned decline to disclose it, or conflicts with other information must be retained as distinct kinds of unknown, rather than being filled in by the model with “common sense.”

| Field | Question Answered | Minimum Record | What Cannot Be Inferred |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Actor ID | Whom or what unit of action does the current analysis address? | Stable reference, time window, and limits of identity | Authority to represent the actor’s entire life |
| Circle memberships | Which relational and institutional positions does the actor occupy now? | Circle ID, entry/exit, and basis for membership | Identification with the circle’s goals |
| Role | What is the actor expected, permitted, or compelled to do? | Source of the role, rights, responsibilities, and conflicts | Actual capabilities and genuine willingness |
| State variables | Which states are observable or candidates at present? | Time band, source, status, and counterevidence | A stable essence of personality |
| Conditions | What makes a particular behavior more feasible or riskier? | Register material conditions and experience–meaning conditions separately | Determination of behavior by a single cause |
| Unknowns | Which key information is unavailable for now? | Kind of missingness, impact, and limits on obtaining the information | Freedom for the model to impute values |

## 9.2 Slow, Intermediate-Timescale, and Fast Variables

The three time bands are neither personality types nor independent compartments. They constrain the rate of updates: what may change after one event, what takes multiple rounds to change, and what should be updated only after more time has passed, evidence across contexts is available, or the person’s life has been substantially reorganized.

### 9.2.1 Slow Variables

Candidate slow variables include long-term value priorities, habitual ways of interpreting, long-term capabilities and resource positions, identity commitments, and tendencies of trust and attachment. Calling them “slow” means only that they change relatively slowly within the current research window. It implies neither that they are innate or fixed nor that they express a true self or hold across all contexts.

Slow variables require evidence across time sufficient for the persistence claimed. A further claim of stability across contexts also requires materials covering different contexts. The validity of a measurement scale cited for a single measurement is not the same as the person having been observed in every setting. If a tendency appears in only one high-pressure circle and does not appear in others, the competing explanation that it is “activated by that circle” must be retained. If resource positions, trauma, major changes in relationships, long-term learning, or moves between institutional settings change patterns of action, slow variables should also be updated, split into conditional variables, or retired.

Learned tendencies can develop through repeated practices in families, schools, workplaces, and peer groups. Who has time to practice, who receives correction or encouragement, and which kinds of speech, posture, qualifications, and judgment are rewarded can affect which actions later feel natural, possible, or worth trying. This process of historical formation differs both from a single strategic choice and from an essence of personality. Opportunities for training, repeated practice, institutional responses, and differences across contexts need to be connected. Family background or an entire life history must not be inferred backward from a single action.

The same actor may carry different tendencies from several experiences of socialization. An old tendency may fit poorly in a new environment. A resource recognized in one domain may bring no return in another, and repeated new practices may change earlier tendencies. Slow variables should be open to splitting by context, revision, or retirement. An average association at the group level does not fill in an individual’s state. Deviation from what is typical in the group does not automatically make the individual abnormal.

### 9.2.2 Intermediate-Timescale Variables

Intermediate-timescale variables include role identity, goals for the current period, expectations in relationships, positions in alliances, accumulated fatigue, recent reputation, and trust specific to the period. They generally span several rounds of interaction but may change with the end of a project, organizational restructuring, repair of a relationship, a public event, or a change in resources.

Intermediate-timescale variables are the main interface for mutual constitution between individuals and circles. Circles shape them through roles, metrics, reputation, rewards and penalties, and a sense of belonging. Actors in turn change circles through the fulfillment of responsibilities, resistance, exit, alliance formation, interpretation, and institutional write-back. Observing only one side leads either to misrecording structural pressure as a personality defect or to reducing individual choice entirely to determination by the environment.

### 9.2.3 Fast Variables

Fast variables include immediate emotions, arousal, attention, fear, impulses, current beliefs, short-term bodily states, perceptions of opportunity, and current strategies. They must be tied to a specific time and event. A single state of a fast variable must not be used to infer long-term personality.

Although short-lived, fast variables may feed into intermediate-timescale and slow variables through thresholds, public traces, irreversible actions, or feedback from others. For example, a brief fear does not by itself amount to lasting distrust. But if the fear leads to a public retreat, punishment by the organization, rupture in relationships, and repeated avoidance, it can form a more durable path through multi-circle feedback. A projection must spell out that channel. The result must not be read backward to conclude that “they were distrustful to begin with.”

| Time Band | Typical Rate of Change | Evidence Required | Permitted Updates After an Event | Common Misuses |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Slow variables | Slow relative to the current window, possibly spanning months, years, or major periods | Assess persistence using materials across time. Claims across contexts additionally require corresponding coverage. Record source dependence and counterexamples separately | New evidence may change the level of support or the variable’s value. Check separately whether the change persists | Treating a label as innate or prescribing the same update rate for everyone |
| Intermediate-timescale variables | Several rounds of interaction to one period | Repeated observations within the same period, with role and circle conditions | May be updated as relationships, projects, organizations, or resources change | Treating a response to a role as personality |
| Fast variables | Minutes, hours, or several days | Tie to the time, event, bodily state, and information state | May be updated after each event | Using one emotion to predict the entire future |

## 9.3 Personality Only as a Conditional Hypothesis About Slow Variables

In this framework, “personality” is not a forbidden word, but it must be brought down to its proper place: it is a set of hypotheses about slow variables that seek to compress variation in behavior across time and contexts. At minimum, a personality hypothesis must state what behavior was observed, in which contexts it was or was not observed, how long it lasted, what sources and counterexamples exist, what simpler explanations are available, and what the hypothesis may be used for.

A personality hypothesis may have only the status of observation record, supported hypothesis, candidate, disputed, unknown, or retired. An observation record describes specific verifiable behavior and provides no conclusion about personality. A supported hypothesis is still qualified by its time window, context, and error. A candidate only awaits testing. Disputed means the evidence or explanations conflict. Unknown means no judgment can currently be made. Retired means the hypothesis no longer offers a gain or is unacceptably misleading.

| Personality Hypothesis Card | Required Content |
| --- | --- |
| Hypothesis description | Describe the tendency in terms that can be distinguished and refuted, without insulting or pathologizing labels |
| Behavioral indicators | Specific behaviors, choices, or expressions, not replaced by the analyst’s impressions |
| Observed contexts | Circles, roles, pressures, resources, information, and time windows |
| Contexts without observations of the tendency | Settings that have not been covered or where contrary behavior has appeared |
| Competing explanations | Role demands, short-term emotions, material constraints, strategies, cultural norms, measurement bias, and others |
| Counterevidence | Facts, testimony, and results inconsistent with the hypothesis |
| Current status | Candidate, supported, disputed, unknown, or retired |
| Permitted uses | Research, communication, scenario projection, or other limited uses |
| Prohibited uses | Clinical diagnosis, judgments about the essence of personality, discriminatory screening, or automatic authorization or punishment |

This framework does not perform clinical diagnosis, psychiatric diagnosis, or unauthorized psychological assessment. Even if a professional diagnosis exists outside the framework, it may be included only as material with a source, permission, and limits of applicability. The framework must not generate or expand diagnoses itself or use them to take action incompatible with their original purpose.

## 9.4 Role Activation and Circle Conditions

At least six conditions can change when an actor enters different circles. These are visible information, available resources, rewards and penalties, responsibility, identity activation, and the ability to exit. These conditions, independently measured individual differences, and the interaction between the two should be treated as competing explanations for differences in behavior. That an investigation looks first at contexts that are easier to change does not mean that, in the model, all the shared explanatory contribution is assigned to context in advance.

For example, a person may be silent in their family circle, assertive at work, and open among friends. At least the following competing explanations are possible. Past conflicts in the family may raise the cost of speaking. The role at work may grant formal authority. Friendships may entail lower penalties and greater trust. The three settings may activate different identities. Observers may have access to different information in each circle. The behavior samples may cover different time windows. Comparisons should hold the target, data split, available information, complexity, and measurement budget fixed and test models of individual differences, context, and their interaction separately. If a personality indicator improves prediction of behavior in a new window, it can support a qualified prediction. This still does not establish that the indicator is the main causal mechanism, an innate essence, or a priority for intervention. If the same person is always paired with just one setting, repeated observations cannot by themselves separate the contributions of the two.

A role activation record should identify which event activated which role and whether the role comes from formal rules, informal expectations, a commitment by the actor, or a threat. It should also state whether the actor identifies with the role and whether it conflicts with other roles. It should further specify what the role permits and prohibits, what costs it requires the actor to bear, and what conditions are needed to exit the role.

A claim of stability across contexts must also specify whether it concerns rank order, mean level, the distribution of state fluctuations, or a profile of “how the person responds under specified conditions.” Changes in the proportions of different contexts a person encounters can change their overall mean behavior even if their conditional responses are unchanged. Long-term resources and capabilities are also slow variables. A person-specific random intercept in a statistical model represents only individual differences that have yet to be decomposed. It must not be labeled personality directly.

## 9.5 How Individuals and Circles Affect One Another

Effects in both directions must be separated into two testable chains.

The circle-to-individual chain can be described as follows. A circle’s rule or an event changes information, resources, risk, roles, or conditions of meaning. Through the actor’s perceptions and capabilities, this changes fast variables. The change then reaches intermediate-timescale or slow variables through repeated feedback, public traces, or the reconfiguration of relationships.

The individual-to-circle chain can be described as follows. The actor’s expression, labor, refusal, exit, bridging, alliance formation, or innovation changes the circle’s resource flows, role allocation, shared interpretations, membership boundaries, institutional records, or external interfaces. This in turn changes the states of other members and of the circle.

| Direction | Starting Point | Channel That Must Be Found | Observable Outcome | Main Counterexample |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Circle→Individual | Change in rules, events, resources, or meaning | Information, rewards and penalties, physical load, roles, reputation, and belonging | Changes in states, behavior, or probabilities of choices | No change under the same exposure, or an external event explains the change |
| Individual→Circle | Expression, action, refusal, exit, or bridging | Resource flows, imitation by members, institutional write-back, network propagation, and a mechanism of representation | Changes in rules, boundaries, allocation, relationships, or collective states | The action is not received, or the change in the circle precedes the individual action |
| Circle↔Individual Feedback | The two chains above form a continuing closed loop | Return channel, delay, thresholds, and traces | Changes in transition probabilities in subsequent rounds | Only a signal arrives, without changing any subsequent state |

“Affect one another” does not complete an explanation; it requires the analyst to keep looking for direction, channels, delays, thresholds, losses, and counterfactuals.

## 9.6 Evidence Levels and Upgrading Variable Status

Actor variables are upgraded in a conservative order. A model may propose candidates from residuals, but a candidate must not be upgraded directly to a fact. A single self-report, a single account from another person, a single behavioral observation, and a single measurement scale result each carry different biases. Agreement across sources does not automatically rule out a common environment, a shared narrative, or the same measurement bias.

| Current Status | Permitted Use | Requirements for Upgrading | What Must Be Retained |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Unknown | Raise questions and flag impacts | Lawful and appropriate new observations | The kind of unknown and the reason for leaving the information uncollected |
| Candidate | Use in scenario projection and design a minimal test | Predeclared indicators, time window, and counterexample check | Competing candidates, privacy, and risks |
| Supported hypothesis | Use for explanation or foresight in specified contexts | Out-of-sample review, tests across contexts, or independent sources | Limits of applicability, counterevidence, and error |
| Observation record | Describe behavior or a state at a specific time | Integrity of sources and an observation contract | Do not promote behavior to personality |
| Disputed | Present conflicting materials side by side and lower the strength of the output | Independent review or new discriminating evidence | The positions and interests of all parties |
| Retired | Preserve the history and exclude it from the current projection | A new version may append only the reasons for retirement | Impacts caused by the earlier judgment and remedies |

If a variable is proposed only after results are known, it can help explain those results and generate a test for the next round, but it must not be used retroactively to support the original prediction. If the definition, time band, or indicators of the same variable change, a new version should be created. Earlier failures must not be overwritten.

## 9.7 Material and Experience–Meaning Conditions in Actor States

An actor’s material conditions include physical load, observable aspects of health and sleep, time, space, money, tools, transportation, care responsibilities, standing under the law, and the actual ability to exit. Experience–meaning conditions include feelings, expectations, memory, identity, trust, shame, hope, fear, commitments, perceived legitimacy, and conflicts of value.

Record the two groups separately according to their relevance to the current question. If the task only needs a sufficient technical or quantitative indicator, collection of irrelevant private experiences must not be compelled. If the conclusion concerns life opportunities, meaning, or autonomy, material totals must not be used in place of the corresponding experiences. Material deprivation can increase pressure without automatically determining how the actor interprets meaning. Positive beliefs can increase persistence but cannot remove constraints imposed by resources, the body, or institutions. An inference across channels must specify which material change affects behavior through which experiential mechanism, or which change in meaning alters material conditions through which action.

Identity also has an institutional dimension that cannot be reduced to personal experience. Self-identification, classification by others, administrative registration, legal eligibility, and actual treatment must be recorded separately. A designation can alter access to hiring, services, the submission of evidence, or appeals, even if the person does not accept it. Institutional standing and the experience of dignity can affect one another without either establishing the other. The categories and comparison groups used by researchers must also be open to challenge by those concerned and to review of their versions.

Being constrained does not amount to an absence of action. A person signing under a credible threat and a device moving their body to produce the same handwriting involve different processes. The first can still be the person’s own action, even though there is no safe way to refuse and valid consent is absent. Attribution of the action, knowledge, control, feasible alternatives, blame, and remedy each require their own grounds. A causal explanation does not automatically exempt anyone from responsibility or establish that blame is warranted.

## 9.8 Privacy, Absence, and Refusal to Disclose

The closer the analysis gets to an individual, the higher the cost of misuse. Actor records must distinguish information that is public, restricted to a particular context, sensitive, or highly sensitive, as well as information a person declines to disclose. Permitted uses must be consistent with the purpose of collection, authorization, and protections for those affected. Using material for communication does not by itself qualify it for use in hiring, insurance, law enforcement, medical care, punishment, or public evaluation.

Refusal to disclose is not evidence of hostility, deception, ignorance, or risk. Silence can stem from safety concerns, trauma, privacy, language, trust, differences in power, or reasonable noncooperation. The analyst may record only that “there is no usable information” and how this limits projection. Personality inferences must not be used to fill the gap.

## 9.9 Actor State Snapshots

An auditable snapshot must contain at least the following.

| Section | What to Record | Output Ceiling |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Observation boundaries | Actor, time window, observation position, and data cutoff | Limit to the current sample |
| Circles and roles | Memberships, roles, conflicts, and ability to enter and exit | Do not assume identification with the circle or common goals |
| Slow, intermediate-timescale, and fast variables | Status, source, time, and counterevidence for each variable | Do not judge the essence of personality |
| Conditions in both channels | Material conditions and experience–meaning conditions | Do not substitute either for the other |
| Competing explanations | Structural factors, roles, strategies, measurement, and chance | Retain explanations that cannot be distinguished |
| Privacy and uses | Privacy level, authorization, and prohibited uses | Stay within the limits on use |
| Update conditions | Which variables a new event reaches and when to review them | Do not update every state at once |

The snapshot retains its observation cutoff and the time at which information was obtained. When a new event occurs, first determine which channels and time bands it reaches and then update the corresponding variables. Calculate autonomous changes triggered by the passage of time itself separately under the registered rules for dynamics. When neither a new observation nor a reliable rule for advancing the state is available, the last observed value and its time may be retained or the state may be marked unknown, but the old observed value must not be treated as the actual current state. Rerunning the model is likewise no evidence that all variables changed at once.

## 9.10 Example of Actor Analysis in an Event

Suppose a project lead suddenly vetoes the team’s proposal in a public meeting. The observation record can establish only the veto, the context of the meeting, what was said, and the visible consequences. Candidate fast variables might include time pressure, physical fatigue, perceived threat, and immediate anger. Candidate intermediate-timescale variables might include project goals, responsibilities of the role, a recent decline in trust, and reputational risk. A candidate slow variable might be long-term tolerance of uncertainty, but this requires evidence across contexts.

The circles must also be examined. Has management just changed the metrics? Is the team short on resources? Has public opinion on a platform raised the cost of failing publicly? Do care responsibilities at home reduce the time available? Does organizational culture reward forceful decisions? The lead’s veto may change trust and subsequent expression in the team. The team’s silence may reinforce the lead’s belief that “only coercion works,” creating feedback. Writing only “controlling personality” would obscure multiple channels open to intervention. Writing only “determined by structure” would erase the actor’s choices, responsibilities, and capability to bridge circles.
