A reasonable output should be layered: confirmed facts about behavior; hypotheses about states and the kind of support each has received; circle conditions and channels in both directions; the next observation that would best distinguish the alternatives; and what remains unknown when evidence is insufficient. The output may enter path projection in Part 11, but it must not by itself produce a diagnosis of the person, a punishment, or authority to compel.

## 9.11 Where This Part Stops

The actor state layer can describe which observable states and candidate variables are currently available. It can identify changes that roles and circles may activate and the channels through which individuals and circles constitute one another. It can also describe which time bands a new event may reach first and which information remains unknown or protected by privacy.

This framework does not adjudicate the essence of a person. No professional assessment amounts to an ontological definition of a person. The clinical significance and scope of application of personality descriptions should be assessed by qualified professionals on the basis of relevant evidence, valid authorization, and participation by the people concerned. That assessment must not be supplied by this framework alone. Future paths enter conditional foresight. Value and action enter the layer of normative choice and authorization. Prediction, professional status, and labels do not automatically confer authority to act.

[^p10-tn-actor-tuple]: Translator's note: The Chinese edition writes this as `A* = <行动者 ID，观察窗，圈层成员关系，角色，慢变量，中变量，快变量，物质条件，体验—意义条件，未知项，证据状态，隐私与允许用途>`.

[^p10-tn-circle]: Translator's note: *Circle* renders 圈层. Circles may be parallel, nested, overlapping, bridged, or competing, and may also form only temporarily around particular events; the term does not imply a hierarchy.

<a id="part_10"></a>

# Part 10 Multi-Circle Objects and Joint States

Analyzing a single object or circle[^p11-tn-circle] often compresses other conditions into a black box called “environment.” The aim of multi-circle analysis is not to add background without limit but to bring together into one joint state the structural units that can change the path of an event and that can be distinguished by boundaries, relations, channels, and time windows. Families, teams, institutions, platforms, markets, cultural communities, bodily systems, and geographic spaces can all matter at the same time in the same event, but naming them does not automatically make them effective circles.

## 10.1 The Candidate Object Contract[^p11-tn-contract] for a Circle

A circle can be represented as follows.

`C* = <circle ID, candidate boundary, membership eligibility, role rules, entry and exit, internal state, external interfaces, material channels, experience–meaning channels, constraints, time window, scale profile, identity criterion, evidential status, counterexamples>`.[^p11-tn-circle-tuple]

A circle is not “several similar people,” and it need not have shared consciousness or a unified goal. Members may be unaware that the analyst has grouped them into the same circle. They may disagree about goals, and a candidate structure may form solely through shared exposure to a platform, rule, space, or risk. Documents, memberships, sustained interactions, or shared exposure can support the corresponding facts about the object. If it is further claimed that adopting this grouping improves explanation or foresight, a limited gain must then be tested against a complexity-matched alternative grouping or a no-grouping baseline. Support under G1 concerns eligibility for this latter task-specific claim. It does not decide whether the organization, relationships, or people affected exist.

| Admission Question | Evidence Required | If the Check Does Not Pass |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Can the boundary be distinguished? | Membership rules, interfaces, shared exposure, or sustained interaction | Retain it as the analyst’s grouping and do not call it an objective circle |
| Is there differentiated structure within it? | Roles, relations, channels, or constraints | Reclassify it as a population category or an environmental variable |
| If a grouping gain is claimed, does the grouping improve explanation or foresight? | Out-of-sample comparison with a simple baseline | Do not claim that a gain has been established. Retain facts about the object that have independent grounds. Depending on the task, fall back to a single focus, continuous variables, or a candidate grouping |
| Does identity hold within the time window? | K, entry and exit, and conditions for conversion and dissolution | Record separate segments or register object conversion |
| Have counterexamples been retained? | Boundary perturbation or review across time or locations | Downgrade the strength of support for the object |

## 10.2 The Joint Object Contract

The multi-circle joint object is written as follows.

`MC* = <actor set A, circle set C, circle relations Rcc, membership and role mapping Rac, material state M, experience–meaning state Ψ, channels and constraints Q, event stream E, multiple clocks T, scale profile SP, evidential status W, identity criterion K>`.[^p11-tn-joint-tuple]

The value of a joint object lies in preserving local differences and cross-boundary channels. It does not put all related objects into one large circle or assert that a higher-level subject exists. Circles with no identifiable relation may only be observed side by side. If they share only an environment, a direct effect should not be invented. If the relation appears only during an event, the temporary relation and the conditions under which it ends should be registered.

| Joint State Field | Responsibility | Common Error |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A Actor set | Preserve actor states, roles, and privacy boundaries | Substituting a circle average for the individual |
| C Circle set | Maintain boundaries, states, and identity separately | Merging all background conditions into one overarching circle |
| Rcc Circle relations | Record direction, type, delay, threshold, and evidence | Substituting “interrelated” for a mechanism |
| Rac Membership and role mapping | Allow multiple affiliations, role conflicts, and differences in exit | Assuming each person belongs to only one circle |
| M and Ψ | Represent material and experience–meaning conditions side by side | Material determinism or reduction to narrative alone |
| Q Channels and constraints | Specify the extent and capacity of propagation | Assuming impacts are lossless, instantaneous, and reach every position |
| E and T | Event stream and multiple asynchronous clocks | Updating every state synchronously after each event |
| W and K | Evidence, unknowns, residuals, and object identity | Mistaking a complete model for a complete account of reality |

## 10.3 Six Kinds of Circle Relation

The six starting points for description distinguish different questions. They do not form a classification that is mutually exclusive, single-dimensional, or exhaustive of reality. Parallelism, nesting, and overlap describe positions along a declared dimension. Bridging and competition describe connections or constraints. Temporariness describes duration and conditions for ending. These descriptions can be combined. For each actual circle relation, separately register the source circle, target circle, direction, shared members or interfaces, channel, extent, time window, and relevant evidence. Do not force a value for an inapplicable delay or threshold.

### 10.3.1 Parallelism

Under the current contract, parallel circles have no relation of containment in either membership or the scope of their operating contracts. Parallelism does not mean that the circles are unrelated. Two parallel teams may share a budget, a platform, and higher-level institutional arrangements. They may also be triggered simultaneously by the same event. If only shared exposure can be confirmed, register a shared environment. Register cross-circle propagation only when a channel for information, resources, personnel, or institutional write-back has been found.

### 10.3.2 Nesting

Nesting means that one object’s extent is actually contained within another along an explicitly specified relational dimension. The basis of containment must be stated. Membership, roles, resource accounting, institutional jurisdiction, and space are distinct dimensions. If the claim concerns containment of only a part, first identify the contained subobject and its boundary. If two sets intersect but neither contains the other, register overlap on that dimension. Describing the intersection as “partial containment” does not also justify classifying it as nesting. A subcircle’s state must not be taken to represent the higher-level circle directly, and the outer circle’s rules need not take effect in the same way at every position inside it.

### 10.3.3 Overlap

Overlap means that circles share some members, roles, resources, or interfaces without fully containing one another. Positions of overlap may take on bridging, translation, and coordination. They may also face role conflict, conflicting loyalties, duplicated work, and risks of information leakage. The proportion of overlap must not be used in place of a record of the specific items shared.

### 10.3.4 Bridging

Bridging means that an actor, institution, interface, text, platform, or event connects previously separate circles. A bridging party need not have authority to represent others or be loyal to any party. Transmission capability, translation losses, selective filtering, responsibility, substitutability, overload, and exit conditions must be registered separately.

### 10.3.5 Competition

Competition means that circles create identifiable exclusionary constraints on scarce resources, attention, legitimacy, member commitments, interpretive authority, or room for action. Different goals do not automatically amount to competition. Register competition only when one circle’s use of what is available, its rules, or its actions changes the set available to another circle. Cooperation and competition can coexist through different channels.

### 10.3.6 Temporariness

Temporary circles form around events, tasks, crises, shared exposure, or short-term goals. They may dissolve rapidly or become more durable through traces, stabilization of roles, and institutionalization. Temporariness may follow from a task deadline set in advance or be a provisional judgment based on the short window observed so far. The two should be distinguished. Temporariness does not mean that a circle is unimportant, has no responsibilities, or has not yet formed real relations. A temporary alliance can still overlap in membership, compete for resources, and be bridged through particular interfaces.

| Relation | Minimum Criterion | What to Check in Particular | Failure or Relation Change |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Parallelism | No containment relation at present | Shared environment and indirect coupling | Containment, overlap, or bridging emerges |
| Nesting | Explicit containment in membership/contract/jurisdiction/space | Downward constraints and loss from upward aggregation | K changes, exit occurs, or jurisdiction is reconfigured |
| Overlap | Some members, roles, resources, or interfaces are shared | Role conflict and double counting | Shared items disappear or the relation becomes containment |
| Bridging | Identifiable interface for cross-circle transmission | Translation, filtering, overload, and authority to represent | The channel closes, is replaced, or becomes institutionalized |
| Competition | An exclusionary constraint on the available set | Scarce items, time window, and channels for cooperation | Resources expand, rules are coordinated, or a party exits |
| Temporariness | A clearly finite duration or support so far limited to a short window. Distinguish the two | Ending, traces, and who takes up responsibility. Temporariness can be combined with other dimensions of relation | The temporary relation ends, is extended, or becomes institutionalized. Its positions and channel relations are recorded separately |

“Relation change” records a shift in a relation’s type or defining conditions between the states before and after an event. Referring back to the relation by the same name does not by itself establish that it has changed. Relations can persist, strengthen, weaken, change direction, change type, or dissolve. Every judgment must be tied to snapshots before and after, the dimension that changed, and object identity.

## 10.4 Relations Do Not Form a Single Tree

Actual multi-circle structures typically form a directed multigraph of relations with local containment. The same family can be nested within a community and within a legal system. A person can belong at the same time to overlapping family, work, platform, and interest circles. Platforms may bridge parallel groups. Different organizations can compete for funding, cooperate on safety, and overlap in personnel.

Therefore, the framework does not require each node to have only one parent node. Nor does “higher level” automatically mean greater importance, reality, or authority. Each edge expresses just one declared relation. If a pair of circles has multiple edges, the channel and time window for each edge should be recorded separately. If edges at different scales are incomparable, keep them side by side without forcing an order.

The graph representation must pass at least four checks. Has the analyst’s classification been mistaken for an object? Has a shared environment been mistaken for a direct relation? Has membership overlap been mistaken for organizational containment? Has contact with information been mistaken for effective feedback? Register feedback only when a signal returns and changes a subsequent state, transition probability, or constraint.

## 10.5 Multiple Memberships and Role Conflicts

Membership cannot be reduced to a binary, permanent status. Records should include the basis for membership, start and end times, roles, strength of commitment, actual participation, ability to exit, and disputes. Formal members may lack the capability to act. Informal participants may bear key costs. People drawn in involuntarily may be unable to exit. Representatives may lack authorization from the people they represent.

| Membership Dimension | Example States | Implication for Projection |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Basis for entry | Voluntary, employment, kinship, law, platform rules, shared exposure, or coercion | Determines the limits of responsibility and exit |
| Extent of participation | Core, intermittent, peripheral, nominal, or passively bearing the consequences | Determines visible information and channels of impact |
| Role | Decision-making, execution, care, bridging, oversight, affected party, or dissent | Determines rights, obligations, and conflicts |
| Commitment | High, medium, low, disputed, or unknown | Affects paths without amounting to personality |
| Ability to exit | Free, costly, subject to approval, or impossible in practice | Triggers the protection floor and the action ceiling |

An actor’s multiple memberships do not automatically establish actual bridging. Potential bridging capability, actual transmission across boundaries, and outcomes caused by transmission are recorded separately. An identifiable interface supports a description of capability. Records of actual transmission support a description of the process. A causal effect that changes the state further requires a corresponding comparison. If recipients do not change their choices, this does not negate the limited transmission of information that already occurred. Authority to represent, transmission capability, and responsible parties must be kept distinct.

## 10.6 Material Conditions and Experience–Meaning Conditions

The joint state maintains both M and Ψ. M includes the body, time, space, money, energy, equipment, infrastructure, property rights, supply, care, and actual capability to act. Ψ includes feelings, expectations, memory, identity, trust, shame, hope, fear, commitments, conflicts of value, and perceived legitimacy.

The two channels can affect one another. For example, a platform’s rules for traffic change visibility and income, affecting material and informational conditions. Members interpret the decline as exclusion or failure, a change in experience and meaning. This interpretation may affect exit, protest, or cooperation and thereby feed back into platform metrics and resource allocation. Each step must specify a carrier, direction, delay, and counterexamples.

| Bridge Between Channels | Starting Point | Mediating Factors | Outcome | Counterfactual Question |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| M→Ψ | A change in income, space, the body, or time | Sense of safety, sense of control, or interpretation of identity | Changes in trust, fear, or commitment | Do interpretations differ under the same material change? |
| Ψ→M | Legitimacy, hope, shame, or conflicts of value | Cooperation, refusal, migration, care, or protest | Changes in resource flows, labor, property rights, or use of facilities | Would the action occur without the change in meaning? |
| M↔Ψ Closed loop | Effects in both directions occur in succession | Feedback and traces | Path dependence or phase transition | Does the return signal actually change a subsequent state? |

Analyzing material and experience–meaning conditions side by side requires neither equal weights nor full collection on both sides for every narrow task. If the objective concerns members’ control, well-being, or relationships, genuinely relevant differences on both sides should be retained. If the question asks only about something such as the total amount of equipment, for which an adequate summary is already available, the analysis may stop within that scope.

### 10.6.1 Resource Conversion and Multiple Positions

Resources create usable opportunities only under specific conditions for conversion. Income pays current expenses. Wealth provides a buffer or collateral. Qualifications and skills need recognition from the relevant institutions. Relationships must be possible to mobilize, and the other party must actually have resources and be willing to help. Transportation, time, care, standing under the law, information, and the cost of refusal may cause equal amounts of resources to yield different practical options. These quantities must not be added into a single capital or power score without specifying units, conversion rules, double counting, weights, and purpose. A well-grounded monetary valuation or an index for a specific task can answer the question within its limits, but a total must not be used to fill in each person’s control, qualifications, or well-being. Nor does a nominal allocation substitute for resources actually at the recipient’s disposal.

The combined effects of multiple positions need not take the form of simple overlap among several circles. Rules of admission, accumulation of seniority, evaluation, and redress within the same institutional arrangement may change a group’s opportunities in succession. For example, later entry into a position where seniority accrues can continue to produce differences when opportunities are subsequently allocated uniformly by seniority. The sequence of rules, the denominators for entry at each stage, comparison groups, and positions that are absent should be traced. The number of categories, an interaction term, or a group average does not by itself establish the cause of harm to an individual.

Different statuses must not be put in a fixed order of disadvantage in advance. The same cue may receive different responses in different jobs, locations, relationships, or periods. Retain differences within categories, those who did not enter, and those who succeeded. Adding sensitive classifications is warranted only if it can answer a clear question, the material is sufficient to support it, and the privacy cost is tolerable. Do not compel disclosure to fill a cross-tabulation or treat the positions as nonexistent because the material cannot be obtained.

### 10.6.2 Assets, Liabilities, Contracts, and Transmission Through Credit

In financial relations, the material state must at least distinguish stocks at a point in time from flows over a period, asset valuations from funds that can be converted into cash, principal payable from interest, and net worth from the ability to refinance. Assets in excess of liabilities do not guarantee that cash will be available when payments are due. A new loan does not by itself repair a shortfall in asset value. The market price of collateral, the contractual borrowing limit, the actual outstanding loan balance, and recovery on default are distinct quantities. Each must be tied to the currency, maturity, contract, repayment priority, and observation window.

One testable path of amplification starts with changes in assets or cash flow that alter net worth and the amount that can be pledged as collateral. Through loan quotes, credit limits, loan extensions, or margin calls, these changes affect net financing. Changes in financing then affect consumption, investment, hiring, and subsequent income and feed back into demand for assets. A candidate for self-reinforcement may be proposed only when the relevant constraints are binding and cash, equity, alternative loans, or arrangements for bearing risk have not cut the chain. Earnings expectations, interest rates, or policy may also drive prices and spending jointly. Changes in the same direction are insufficient to identify a financial accelerator mechanism.

In a financial network, common exposure, defaults on direct claims, calls on contractual guarantees, price spillovers from fire sales, and withdrawals of funds prompted by information must be recorded separately. Risk transfer changes who holds the risk and what they can bear. Whether it reduces overall risk still depends on the receiving party’s capital, contingent obligations, and who ultimately bears losses. The face values of mutual claims and liabilities across multiple layers must not be added up as net wealth for the system. Recovery of payments, recovery of capital, recovery of borrowers’ cash flow, and recovery of living conditions are different outcomes.

## 10.7 Five Kinds of Clock and Asynchronous Updating

A joint state distinguishes at least immediate, interactive, organizational, institutional, and long-term clocks. Immediate reactions may be measured in minutes, trust in interaction in rounds, organizational budgets in quarters, institutional write-back in deliberation cycles, and long-term learning or generational change in years. The timing of the same event’s effects varies by variable.

| Clock | Typical Objects | Update Trigger | Common Error |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Immediate | Emotion, attention, bodily arousal, local prices | Arrival of a signal or a direct shock | Recording short-term fluctuations as long-term change |
| Interactive | Trust, strategies, expectations in relationships, local norms | Several rounds of action and feedback | Ignoring the order of rounds and memory |
| Organizational | Roles, budgets, processes, metrics | Cycles of decision, execution, and review | Assuming a decision amounts to implementation |
| Institutional | Rules, rights, responsibilities, appeals, records | Actual change in rules or procedures, actual write-back, and execution. Record authorization and legal validity separately | Treating a verbal commitment as institutional change |
| Long-term | Capabilities, identity, infrastructure, culture, and relations across generations | Accumulation, learning, maintenance, and replacement | Extrapolating to the long term from short-term data |

External events directly update only variables reached through their channels and for which the conditions hold. Endogenous processes, expiration, accumulation, and recovery proceed on their own clocks and under the registered rules for dynamics. A variable untouched by the current event is not necessarily unchanged in reality. Without reliable grounds for an update, retain the last observed value, its time, and uncertainty, or mark it unknown. Do not refresh all variables at once to make the joint snapshot “complete.”

Asynchrony can also change the direction of effects. Asset prices can change immediately, while loan contracts adjust only upon revaluation or renewal. The timing of payments differs between fixed-rate and floating-rate debt. Public guarantees may provide cash first and distribute losses later. A joint projection should check when each constraint actually binds, when alternative financing is possible, and whether the recovery window precedes irreversible harm. Extending a short-term backstop must not be treated as if long-term repayment had already recovered.

## 10.8 Joint State Snapshots

A snapshot is a frozen starting point for projection, not a permanent copy of reality. It must include a timestamp, evidence cutoff, actor and circle references, relation and membership mappings, M/Ψ states, channels, constraints, the five kinds of clock, scale profile, unknowns, residuals, and identity criteria.

Each value in a snapshot should be marked as observed, supported hypothesis, candidate, disputed, unknown, or not applicable. When sources conflict, first retain their respective values, how they were produced, and their versions. A combined estimate and its uncertainty may be reported separately only if there are grounds for both the measured object and the model used to combine results. An arithmetic mean must not be used to close the original conflict automatically. If a circle is internally differentiated, do not represent all positions with a single mean. If necessary, establish subcircles or use a distributional representation, but these must pass object admission again.

## 10.9 Boundary Perturbation and Object Conversion

Multi-circle models can easily mistake chosen boundaries for natural boundaries. At least three kinds of perturbation must be tested. Change the membership threshold and check whether the conclusion is stable. Move the time window and check whether the relation still holds. Replace or remove a circle and check whether the joint model’s gain disappears. If small changes in the boundary reverse the conclusion, support for the object should be downgraded and the sensitivity reported.

If a circle’s K no longer holds after an event, object conversion or dissolution should be registered. For example, a temporary rescue group may become a formal institution, a project team may split into competing alliances, a platform community may migrate to a new platform, or a household’s care arrangements may be reorganized after a member exits. A new object must not overwrite the old object’s failures, responsibilities, or historical traces.

## 10.10 The Complexity Check for Joint States

Adding circles increases the ability to fit the data and also increases error, data requirements, and degrees of freedom in explanation. Each added circle or relation must be assessed on which residual in the original model it explains, whether it has an independently observable channel, and whether it improves out-of-sample explanation or foresight. Its measurement costs and privacy risks must also be specified, and the conclusion checked with that circle or relation removed.

Multi-circle models must be compared with a simple single-focus or single-circle baseline. If a gain in prediction or explanation is claimed but the complex model only tells a fuller story about the materials already observed, without stable comparative support, downgrade the claim of gain and prefer an adequate simpler model. Keeping an object to describe established responsibilities, differences among members, or lawful permissions need not first establish a predictive gain. The independent question it answers should still be stated, recording costs should be controlled, and more data should not be treated as better explanation. Complexity does not amount to maturity. Using one fewer circle is usually better if no important distinction is lost.

## 10.11 An Example of a Joint Object

An analysis of an employee’s public departure from a job can identify the actor, project team, company management, family care circle, professional community, and public opinion on a platform at the same time. The team is nested within the company. The employee belongs to the overlapping team, family, and professional circles. The platform bridges the employee and the public outside the company. The team and management may compete over resource allocation but cooperate in maintaining reputation. A group that forms around the departure to express support may be a temporary circle.

Material conditions include pay, working hours, care, contracts, job positions, and platform traffic. Experience–meaning conditions include dignity, a sense of betrayal, professional identity, trust in coworkers, and perceived legitimacy among the public. The departure immediately changes attention and emotion, then affects trust and handover in the team. Organizational processes may change weeks later, and institutional rules may be written back later still. Any single-track explanation that the person “left because of their personality” or that the departure was “entirely determined by company structure” must be compared with the joint state.

The snapshot makes different paths visible. If the company responds only through public relations without changing working hours or appeal channels, emotions on the platform may subside while internal distrust persists. If resources are adjusted but repair of meaning fails, people may stay while their commitments continue to weaken. If the pressure of family care is lifted, the actor’s available options change. If the professional community provides resources through a bridge, the cost of exit decreases and others may follow the example. The next Part uses event-driven projection to examine which path the evidence supports.

## 10.12 Where This Part Stops

The layer for multi-circle joint states can identify which circles need to be represented together and whether their relations are parallel, nested, overlapping, bridged, competitive, or temporary. It can also describe how memberships and roles are distributed across circles, how material and experience–meaning conditions are represented side by side, and which clocks are out of sync. It can assess whether the complex model offers a gain relative to a simple baseline.

This layer must not answer, on the basis of a structural graph alone, what will necessarily happen in the future. Nor may it decide which circle is more deserving of protection, who should bear the costs, or who has authority to act. Event propagation, branching, and cascades belong to Part 11. Conditional foresight and bounded choice belong to Part 12. Normative judgments, authorization, and protection remain the responsibility of later layers.

Finally, one simplification principle is retained: if an event involves only one actor and one stable circle, and neighboring circles, membership overlap, bridging, and cross-boundary spillovers provide no additional information, do not start the full joint model. Multi-circle modeling is a capability for preserving distinctions where interactions actually occur, not a complexity that every analysis must pay for. When fewer objects, fewer variables, and clearer evidence suffice to answer the question, the framework should actively scale back.

[^p11-tn-circle-tuple]: Translator's note: The Chinese edition writes this as `C* = <圈层 ID，候选边界，成员资格，角色规则，进入与退出，内部状态，外部接口，物质通道，体验—意义通道，约束，时间窗，尺度剖面，同一性判据，证据状态，反例>`.

[^p11-tn-joint-tuple]: Translator's note: The Chinese edition writes this as `MC* = <行动者集合 A，圈层集合 C，圈层关系 Rcc，成员与角色映射 Rac，物质状态 M，体验—意义状态 Ψ，通道与约束 Q，事件流 E，多时钟 T，尺度剖面 SP，证据状态 W，同一性判据 K>`.

[^p11-tn-circle]: Translator's note: *Circle* renders 圈层. Circles may be parallel, nested, overlapping, bridged, or competing, and may also form only temporarily around particular events; the term does not imply a hierarchy.

[^p11-tn-contract]: Translator's note: *Contract* renders 合同 in an analytical sense: a specification registered and frozen before results are known, close to pre-registration. It is not a legal instrument.

<a id="part_11"></a>

# Part 11 Event-Driven Dynamic Projection

Dynamic projection starts from a joint state with evidential statuses recorded. It analyzes state differences, transmission, feedback, and branching after observed, reported, planned, hypothetical, or simulated events. Actions that actually occur can change reality with or without authorization. They must all be recorded according to the evidence. Whether an action occurred, how it is evaluated normatively, any legal or institutional authority, and permission to carry it out on this occasion must each be judged separately. Projection must specify which changes can be established directly, which are only conditional consequences, and which signals would support or weaken the corresponding paths.

## 11.1 The Difference Between Projection and Narrative Continuation

Narrative continuation can choose what comes next on the basis of coherence. Structural projection must preserve conditions, channels, competing paths, and the meaning of failure. If key variables are missing, the projection should stop at an unknown or generate variable candidates rather than fill the gaps with the smoothest story. If several continuations are consistent with existing evidence, keep them as separate branches. If paths are incomparable, do not force an ordering.

The minimum inputs to projection are a frozen snapshot, event records, mechanism hypotheses, parameter ranges, a clock policy, a propagation policy, unknowns, and stopping conditions. The minimum outputs are a path graph, state differences at each step, support status, early signals, contrary signals, residuals, variable candidates, and write-back requirements.

| Input | What Must Be Frozen | Consequence If Left Unfrozen |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Joint snapshot | Actors, circles[^p12-tn-circle], relations, M/Ψ, clocks, and evidence cutoff | The starting point is rewritten after the results |
| Event | Type, time, source, objects reached, and channels | Rumors, plans, and facts are conflated |
| Mechanism | Direction, carrier, delay, threshold, and failure conditions | Each outcome can be explained arbitrarily |
| Parameter | Value, interval, level, or explicit unknown | False precision masks uncertainty |
| Stopping | Conditions involving information, risk, exceeding bounds, and resources | The story continues indefinitely |

## 11.2 The Event Contract[^p12-tn-contract]

There are five kinds of event: observed, reported, planned, hypothetical, and simulated. The type must be included in the record. A reported event can affect people who believe it but must not automatically be treated as having occurred. A plan may change expectations without amounting to execution. A hypothetical event serves conditional analysis. A simulated event belongs only to a model run and must not be written back as a fact about reality.

An event record must include at least the event ID, time or window of occurrence, time of observation, source, affected actors, affected circles, channels, direct changes, evidential status, disputes, competing explanations, reversibility, duration, and subsequent observations.

| Event Type | Permitted Uses in Projection | Factual Status | Required Limits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Observed | Explanation, replay, and forward projection | Holds within the observation contract | Sources, omissions, and measurement error |
| Reported | Propagation of belief and conditional projection | That a report was made is not the same as its contents being true | The reporting party’s position, verification, and disputes |
| Planned | Expectations, preparations, and strategic interaction | Existence of the plan does not amount to execution | Authorization, resources, and conditions for cancellation |
| Hypothetical | Counterfactuals and scenario comparison | No factual status in reality | Conditions, purpose, and feasibility |
| Simulated | Unfolding paths within the model | Model state only | Model version, parameters, and prohibition on extrapolation |

## 11.3 The Joint State Update Formula

The joint state can be written as follows.

`Ω(t) = <A(t), C(t), R(t), M(t), Ψ(t), Q(t), E≤t, SP(t), W(t), K(t)>`.

An update can be written as follows.

`Ω(t+Δ) = F[Ω(t), e(t), u(t), ξ(t) | θ, h]`.

Here `e(t)` is the event, `u(t)` is an externally authorized action that actually occurred, `ξ(t)` is an exogenous disturbance or unmodeled residual, `θ` denotes the frozen hypotheses about mechanisms and parameters, and `h` is the historical term. The formula specifies only what must be recorded. It does not assert that there is a unique true F, and the function symbol must not be used in place of specific mechanisms.

Observed violations, actions beyond authorization, refusals to carry out orders, and unauthorized acts in another’s stead are recorded as `observed/reported` events under `e(t)` with their evidence. The actor, consequences, and permission status must be recorded separately. The lack of permission must not be grounds for deleting an event from the factual record. Violations that have not occurred enter only `hypothetical/simulated` branches. The definition of `u(t)` still requires authorization and actual occurrence. Feasibility in a scenario does not amount to permission in reality. 〔Studies 15.08, 21.03, 21.08, 28.06, 32.08〕

Each update must distinguish directly observed state differences, inferences supported by the mechanism contract, scenario values set to explore a path, and residuals still unexplained. All four retain their provenance in subsequent paths. Appearing together in a snapshot does not give them the same evidential status.

### 11.3.1 States, Observations, and Identified Sets

A joint snapshot is a structured representation of the material currently available, not a direct reading of the full state of the world. Let `z(t)` denote the actual state yet to be inferred for the current task, and `o(t)` the record produced by observation rule H, sampling positions, and conditions of data collection. It can be written as `o(t)=H[z(t),m(t),ε(t)]`. `m(t)` includes who can enter the record, when they are measured, and which fields are withheld from disclosure for protection. `ε(t)` is used only when there is a corresponding error model. Unknowns must not be lumped together as random noise. When conditions allow, numerical measurements may use additive error, whereas text and categorical records use their corresponding generation rules. Ω should preserve the distinction between the records and the judgments, so a change in observation rule is not mistaken for a change in the object.

The same set of records can correspond to several states or mechanisms. Let `Z(o)` denote the set of states still compatible with the record under the stated model and error bounds. If the answer to question q is the same in every state in `Z(o)`, the current material is sufficient for the question. A qualified answer may be given without recovering every latent variable. If the values differ, the output should retain their range, any direction that can be identified, or the parts that genuinely remain unknown. Failure to detect a difference in a finite sample does not automatically establish that only one answer remains over the entire set.

The set also helps plan the next observation. First identify state distinctions that would change the current answer or the comparison of actions. Then check which feasible observations could distinguish them. Adding only precision that leaves the answer unchanged is not necessarily beneficial. A low-cost observation that narrows an important branch may be more valuable than expanding the whole variable table. Data collection remains subject to purpose, necessity, and protection. A need for identification does not confer permission to collect data.

### 11.3.2 Event Processing and State Continuation

Each input retains the time of the event, the time the record was obtained, and the time it entered the model. Material that arrives late can change what is now known about the past, but it must not be used to rewrite the information set available at the time. When correcting a source, append the link to the correction and recompute the states and paths that depend on that source. Retain local results that do not depend on it. When multiple sources recount one event, retain those sources and a single candidate identity for the event to avoid applying the same shock more than once. Events with similar descriptions must not be merged without verification.

State updating consists of two operations. An evidence update revises the judgment about `z(t)`. Mechanism-based progression estimates the change from `z(t)` to a subsequent state. A credible record that “work stopped yesterday” is an evidence update today. The stoppage itself belongs to yesterday’s real events. Rerunning a model and obtaining another response that work “may have stopped” adds only simulated material. Both kinds of update can change Ω, but their reasons and evidential statuses must be kept separate.

Concurrent events may be combined in any order only if the operations commute, shared resources are not in conflict, and neither changes the other’s conditions. If two actions compete for a resource, overwrite the same record, or change each other’s available action set, branches for the possible orders or an explicit rule for concurrency should be retained. Simultaneity must not be presumed from similar timestamps, nor may the causal order of real events be recovered directly from the order in which execution calls return.

## 11.4 The Nine-Step Projection Loop

### 11.4.1 Step One. Freeze the Current State

Record the evidence cutoff, objects, variables, unknowns, disputes, and model version. Information obtained after the cutoff must not be fed back into the starting point. If it needs to be used, create a new version of the run.

### 11.4.2 Step Two. Identify Actors and Circles

Use Parts 9 and 10. If there is insufficient object support for a circle, retain it as a candidate grouping. If there is insufficient support for a personality hypothesis, retain it as a variable candidate. Candidates must not be forced into entities to make the graph complete.

### 11.4.3 Step Three. Separate Conditions in the Two Channels

List the relevant material conditions separately for the task. Also list the relevant experience–meaning conditions and declare the bridges needed between channels. If missing information from the other channel affects the current judgment, retain the unknown. Do not fill the gap by assuming symmetry. A channel genuinely irrelevant to the current task may be marked not applicable with a reason. Do not collect experiential material for the sake of completing the form. Suspend only propagation that depends on the missing channel. Independently established states and paths may still be carried forward.

### 11.4.4 Step Four. Declare Clocks, Thresholds, Capacity, and Delay

Identify each variable’s update clock. If a channel has capacity limits, delay, loss, saturation, reversal, or a recovery window, these should be registered before propagation.

### 11.4.5 Step Five. Inject an Event or Action

A run may include multiple events and actions ordered by time, with their independent sources retained. First determine whether an action occurred, then record the actor, object, consequences, and authorization status separately. Lack of authorization must not be grounds for deleting a fact about what actually occurred. Plans, proposals, and simulated actions that have yet to occur retain their conditional status and must not be recorded as executed. If a model initiates or carries out a real-world action, valid authorization must be obtained separately. A recommendation for deliberation requires only that its evidence, normative premises, price to be paid, gaps in permissions, and conditions for revising the judgment be stated clearly.

### 11.4.6 Step Six. Propagate Along Declared Channels

First record directly confirmable state differences, then analyze return signals, indirect effects, and cross-circle cascades. An identified total effect of an intervention may enter a bounded judgment about the outcome. Unidentified parts of the internal mechanism remain unknown. The total effect must not be used to fill in missing edges of propagation. Explaining specific propagation requires corresponding material about channels and temporal order. Where there is only co-occurrence or correlation, retain common background, selection effects, and other competing explanations. Store state differences derived conditionally from the model separately from those observed in reality.

### 11.4.7 Step Seven. Generate Paths at Branch Points

Conditions, thresholds, actor choices, external disturbances, or uncertainty about mechanisms can all produce branching. Keep mutually exclusive paths separate. Retain parallel paths if they can occur at the same time. Create a merge node only when the conditions for merging are clear.

### 11.4.8 Step Eight. Register Signals, Residuals, and Variable Candidates

List early and contrary signals for each path. State differences that the current variables cannot explain enter the residuals. A residual can prompt an entry in the ledger of variable candidates, but it must not automatically generate a value in reality.

### 11.4.9 Step Nine. Write Back the Results

When actual results arrive, append which paths are supported, when results diverged from the projection, whether a simple baseline performed better, how well the projection was calibrated, and which mechanisms or variables should be downgraded, split, given revised boundaries, or retired. Write-back does not overwrite the original run.

## 11.5 Propagation, Delay, and Thresholds

An account of propagation must at least distinguish the arrival of a signal from an effective change of state. If information is seen but does not change a subsequent transition, register only arrival. If it changes beliefs without prompting action, register the change in experience–meaning state. Register a corresponding structural change only if the signal changes resources, behavior, rules, or relationships.

| Propagation Factor | Question for the Record | Possible Nonlinearity |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Channel | What carrier conveys the impact from the source to the target? | Channel closure, filtering, or substitution |
| Capacity | How much can be carried per unit of time? | Saturation, congestion, and queues |
| Delay | When does it arrive, and when does it take effect? | Delayed feedback or a mistaken finding of no effect |
| Threshold | What conditions must be reached before the state changes? | Abrupt changes, hysteresis, and cascades |
| Loss | What is lost or changed in propagation? | Drift in meaning or dissipation of resources |
| Direction | Is the impact symmetric? | One-way dependence or offsetting effects in the opposite direction |
| Recovery | How does the object return to its previous state or move to a new one after a shock? | Resilience, cumulative damage, and lock-in |

When the same event reaches different circles through different channels, the effects can be opposite. Additional resources may reduce load in one circle but intensify competition in another. A public statement may repair external legitimacy but activate shame or distrust within the circle. A projection should record each channel separately. Net effects must not be used to hide differences in distribution.

## 11.6 Feedback and Cross-Circle Cascades

Feedback requires a prior state or output to change a subsequent state, transition probability, or constraint through a return channel. A cascade requires a local change to trigger changes in other circles through membership overlap, bridging, shared resources, top-down institutional transmission, or network propagation. Both require evidence of temporal order and channels.

| Kind of Cascade | Typical Chain | What to Observe | Stopping Condition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Membership | Overlapping members carry behavior, emotions, or information into another circle | Actual transmission as distinct from shared background | Members stop participating or information is not received |
| Resources | One circle occupies, releases, or reallocates resources | Accounting boundaries, conversion, and loss | Resources are isolated or an alternative source is available |
| Meaning | An interpretation of the event changes identity, legitimacy, or trust | Differences in interpretation across positions | The interpretation does not reach other circles or does not change their experience–meaning states. Effects on action or rules are checked separately |
| Institutions | An appeal, audit, or event changes rules that are then implemented at lower levels | Decisions, execution, and actual write-back | The rule is not implemented or is offset locally |
| Platforms | Recommendations, metrics, or public evaluations amplify propagation | Algorithms, selective visibility, and reflexivity | Exposure stops increasing or the audience does not respond |

The importance of a cascade does not automatically increase with scale. A small circle can have a large impact through a key bridge, while a large circle may have no effective propagation because its channels are blocked. A projection should focus on positions, channels, and thresholds rather than on membership counts alone.

Two local effects can form a transmission chain only with shared conditions for response. Consider two subgroups of equal weight. In the first, the event changes the mediator, but the mediator does not affect the outcome. In the second, the mediator can affect the outcome, but the event cannot change the mediator. The mean effect on each edge is one-half, yet the effect of the event on the outcome across the entire chain can still be zero. This finite construction shows that the mean edge effects cannot be multiplied unconditionally even if the two studies use the same population. It is also necessary to know whether the successive effects occur in objects, states, and times that allow them to connect.

A cascade record should therefore check how the output of the preceding segment enters the next one, the scope of support for joint response, capacity and delay, and offsetting and parallel paths. A limited total effect already identified through an eligible comparison may be retained directly. What is added here is the responsibility to explain the specific propagation, not a requirement to explain all micromechanisms before acknowledging any effect. When an edge has yet to be identified, still retain the independently established upstream changes and terminal effects according to their own evidence.

## 11.7 A Graph of Branching Paths

A path node must include at least the parent node, condition set, triggering event or action, state differences, affected clocks, support status, a probability or ordering expression, early signals, contrary signals, the next node, and the reason for termination. The path graph should be a directed acyclic graph. If there is a feedback loop in reality, represent it with new nodes unfolded over time. A node must not be its own cause and effect within the same run.

Four kinds of uncertainty can produce branches: uncertainty about facts, uncertainty about mechanisms, actor choices, and exogenous disturbances. Different kinds should not be merged into a single vague probability. Give priority to verifying uncertain facts. Address mechanism uncertainty through observations that distinguish the alternatives. For uncertainty about choices, retain strategies and authorization. For exogenous disturbances, use scenario ranges and resilience checks.

| Source of Branching | Example | Appropriate Output | Inappropriate Output |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Facts | Did the event actually occur? | Conditional paths before verification | Choosing one version as fact |
| Mechanisms | Does silence come from fear or strategy? | Signals that distinguish the alternatives and parallel paths | Shutting down inquiry with a personality label |
| Choice | An actor cooperates, resists, or exits | Conditions and consequences of each choice | Claiming that free choices can be predicted precisely |
| Disturbance | Change in policy, prices, disasters, or technology | Stress scenarios and conditions for recovery | Attributing unmodeled residuals to fate |

## 11.8 The Ledger of Variable Candidates

Residuals that the model cannot explain must neither be deleted nor immediately named as real variables. The candidate ledger turns “what might we have missed?” into a testable task. Each candidate record must include at least the source residual, name, possible type, possible channel, observable implications, competing candidates, minimal test, privacy and risk, current status, evidence, and conditions for upgrading or rejecting it.

Candidate statuses include proposed, under test, supported candidate, rejected, and retired. Even with support, a candidate still has to enter the relevant object or variable contract. It must not directly become a stable personality, an effective circle, or a causal mechanism. Candidates proposed after results can improve the next model but must not be used retroactively to support the original prediction.

| Candidate Source | Permitted Action | What Must Be Avoided |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Residuals in the system | Propose several competing candidates and observations that distinguish them | Absorbing all errors into one mysterious variable |
| Account by a person concerned | Preserve the person’s position, the meaning of their account, and its verifiable parts | Automatically downgrading it to subjective noise or elevating it to a complete account of the facts |
| Recurrence across cases | Establish candidate mechanisms and limits of extrapolation | Treating similar narratives as universal laws |
| Model search | Pre-register the next round of testing | Selecting the best results for reporting after seeing them |
| AI suggestions | Generate and compare candidates. Form judgments and analytical recommendations when the evidence, intended use, and explicit normative premises allow | Letting AI output verify facts about reality or authorize action |

On its own initiative, the framework may only identify residuals the current set of variables cannot explain, propose several distinguishable variable candidates, and design a minimal test with acceptable risk for each. It must not decide on its own that a candidate is true in reality.

## 11.9 Scenarios, Counterfactuals, and Simulation

Scenario projection asks, “If these conditions hold, what paths emerge?” A counterfactual asks, “If an event or mechanism had been different, how might the outcome that actually occurred have changed?” Simulation unfolds specified rules and parameters. All three must be kept separate from descriptions of fact.

Counterfactuals especially require comparable conditions. When removing an event, also state which subsequent states, actors’ information, and circle relations change as a result. Do not remove an unwelcome cause while retaining all its consequences. The precision of a simulation must not exceed what its inputs and evidence of mechanisms support. When parameters are unknown, use ranges, levels, or sensitivity analysis. Do not generate unsupported decimal places.

Counterfactuals must preserve the information boundaries at the time. Replacing an event or choice means reassessing the beliefs, resources, orders, and downstream behavior it affects. Do not remove an announcement while holding fixed the attendance and subsequent execution it brought about. Evidence for a short-range branch does not mean an alternative history decades later is already knowable. The actions of ordinary people, the discretion of frontline personnel, and organizational constraints can all enter the explanation. There is no need to assign causal percentages without a basis for identifying them. 〔Studies 03.03, 32.04, 32.08〕

The strength of a counterfactual conclusion depends on whether mechanisms still compatible with the evidence give the same answer under the altered operation. Two mechanisms can both produce observations of `X=M=Y`. In one, X changes Y through M. In the other, X changes M and Y separately. This co-movement alone cannot establish whether setting M directly would change Y. More observations of the same kind can improve the precision of the record but do not necessarily resolve this equivalence between mechanisms. A design that distinguishes the two mechanisms or independently grounded assumptions are needed. If independent materials already identify the total effect of a specified treatment within stated limits, retain that effect even if a particular mediator has yet to be identified.

Counterfactual analysis can therefore produce one answer, a set of feasible answers, or conditional bounds. Treat a direction as robust only when all compatible mechanisms support the same conclusion. If the conclusion reverses across plausible mechanisms, report the conditions under which it reverses and list the evidence needed to distinguish the mechanisms as the next observation. In historical counterfactuals, “removing a person from the post they came to occupy” and “that person never having existed” have different starting points and processes of adaptation, and they must not share the same counterfactual answer.

## 11.9.1 Multi-Order Recursive Projection of the Future

Advancing time over multiple steps, re-entry from a simulated state, and anticipatory reflexivity are three orthogonal dimensions. Advancing time over multiple steps carries a state forward in time within a single run. Simulation re-entry uses a simulated future generated in the first round as the starting point for a child run. Anticipatory reflexivity occurs when actors in the simulation form expectations about the more distant future or other people’s responses and change their current strategies. These three operations must not be conflated under the single term “recursion.”

Three orders are one default way to organize exploratory projection. The first unfolds direct consequences and competing paths. The second checks how those changes alter the available actions, resources, relationships, and adaptation. The third checks institutionalization, lock-in, reversal, and changes in generative conditions. The orders mark responsibilities in projection. They do not amount to three points in time, natural stages, or levels of capability. Do not force recursion for static questions or tasks without a distinct question at the next order. Stop when conditions are insufficient.

The general principle is that the layer that generates possibilities may be bold, the state layer must be strict, the evidence layer must not raise its own status, and the action layer still requires separate authorization. Boldness serves to expand the space of plausible mechanisms, competing paths, and branches with low probability and severe consequences, and also to complete clear judgments within the limits of the evidence. The strength of a judgment is supported only by evidence, conditions of applicability, and the intended use. It must not be raised by tone or by the number of possibilities generated.

### 11.9.2 Recursive Interfaces and Lineage

Before each re-entry, conduct a recasting audit. Record the current order, parent run, parent path, parent node, simulated time, the current order’s time window and the cumulative window, model version, inherited assumptions, new conditions, what is preserved, changed, collapsed, or omitted, new unknowns, effective variables for the target, residuals, identity across re-entry, the return path, and the reason for stopping. The simulated starting point must not be rewritten as present reality.

A child run must inherit the unresolved unknowns, losses, and residuals of its parent run. Selecting a hypothetical branch only narrows the conditions. It does not mean material from reality has eliminated the uncertainty. Increasing the number of draws can at most reduce computational error. It does not increase empirical support about outcomes in reality.

### 11.9.3 Branching, Merging, and Pruning

At each order, check whether there are substantive grounds for the main path, its strongest competitor, a path with low probability and severe consequences, and a route for residuals. Retain all conditions for paths that have grounds. If a category is inapplicable or a path of that kind cannot be constructed, explain why. Do not invent an event to fill a category. Branches must correspond to explainable differences in mechanisms, choices by actors, external disturbances, or unknowns of a specified kind. Retaining a path with severe consequences means only that it calls for investigation or preparation. It does not establish that its real-world probability is known.

Branches may be merged only when object identity is preserved, key states are equivalent within a predetermined tolerance, differences in history do not change subsequent transitions, and the available actions and constraints agree going forward. Pruning rules must be declared in advance, and pruned branches, reasons for pruning, any possible probability mass, and the level of harm must be preserved. Low probability alone must not be grounds for removing a path with severe consequences.

### 11.9.4 Early Stopping

Stop or downgrade if object identity or scale identity fails, key assumptions cannot be inherited, the transition mechanism falls outside its domain, the recursive interface lacks closure, or branches proliferate without signals to distinguish them. Also stop or downgrade if the results reverse across the board under small changes, residuals exceed the threshold for the intended use, a deeper order adds nothing relative to a shallower one and a simple baseline, the calibrated time window is exceeded, a boundary of rights or authorization is reached, or the third-order limit is reached. The stopping record must state what can still be said and what cannot.

## 11.10 Stopping Conditions for a Projection Run

Affected propositions and the paths that depend on them should be suspended or downgraded if a key object lacks even limited support, the event’s factual status is unclear, a propagation channel is missing, a cross-scale mapping fails, or the number of paths grows without signals to distinguish them. The same rule applies if the model is extremely sensitive to small changes in parameters, privacy or safety risks exceed the value of the information, the projection is asked to generate a diagnosis, punishment, or authorization directly, or a simple baseline already performs as well or better. If a shared premise or the overall conditions for use fail, all related runs stop.

Stopping is not a way of concealing failure but part of the output. The stopping record should state where the projection stopped, what has been obtained, which unknowns prevent continuation, which next observation would be most informative, and what risk further projection would add.

## 11.11 An Example of a Cross-Circle Cascade

Continue with the public departure from a job in Part 10. The starting snapshot shows that the employee faces long working hours and the pressure of care work, team trust is declining, company management is concerned with reputation, the professional community provides outside opportunities, and public opinion on the platform has yet to form. The event is the employee publishing an account of their departure. Part of its content can be verified, and part is personal narrative.

Direct effects include the team receiving the information, the platform obtaining a text that can be circulated, and management facing pressure to respond. On the first path, the company makes only a public denial. In the short term, external public opinion may branch toward either fading or amplification. If members inside the company interpret the denial as suppression, team trust declines and further departures occur through bridges in the professional community. On the second path, the company acknowledges the problem and starts verifiable adjustments to resources and appeals. If these are carried out, working hours and processes change on the organizational clock. Trust may recover more slowly on the experience–meaning clock. On the third path, changes in family care conditions ease the employee’s pressure after exiting. This does not directly change the team’s state unless the employee continues to bridge information or resources.

Early signals can include the number of internal appeals, records of hours actually worked, members’ public statements, and changes in hiring and departures. Contrary signals can include no actual change in resources after a policy is announced, increasing internal silence despite declining external exposure, or the professional community failing to form a bridge. Residuals may come from the states of key managers, the platform’s recommendation mechanism, or unobserved legal constraints. These enter only the ledger of variable candidates.

The projection still must not answer which path the company “should” take. It provides conditional consequences and signals that distinguish the paths. Forecast registration, comparison of options, normative premises, and authorization are addressed in the next Part.

## 11.12 Outputs of This Part

A qualifying projection output should include a frozen joint snapshot, event type and evidence, mechanism and parameter assumptions, update steps, and a path graph. For every path, it should record conditions, clocks, support status, and early and contrary signals. It should also include residuals and variable candidates, stopping conditions, a plan for writing back results, and an explicit statement separating prediction and action.

Projection can improve structured preparation for what follows, but it remains conditional. Unless registration before results, a simple baseline, calibration, and continuing write-back are all in place, it must not be used as a basis for claiming that foresight capability has improved. Unless normative grounds and authorization are in place, it must not be used as a basis for claiming that an action should be carried out. Offering an analytical first choice, rejection, or deferral on the basis of explicit normative premises is not the same as claiming that the option has been authorized for execution. For each such judgment, its grounds, price to be paid, opposing case, and conditions for revision must be stated in full.

## 11.13 Action Choice and Updating Conditions

For dynamic questions involving actors, the analysis must not consist only of having a model evaluate a set of static options. At each step, first use the current state to identify actions actually available, actions ruled out, and the source of the restrictions. Then analyze how an actor might choose given their information, resources, relationships, habits, and goals. Capability, belief, willingness, and actual action must be kept separate: being able to act does not amount to acting, stating an intention to act does not amount to having acted, and an analyst’s recommendation does not amount to adoption by the actor.

For each choice with a basis in the material, specify which mechanism it triggers, which states change first, who is affected through which interfaces, and how these changes alter the set of actions available at the next step. State differences must be expressed in concrete terms such as resources, rules, relationships, beliefs, or feasible actions. Feeding the same materials to another model and generating another narrative must not be used in place of updating a state. If the material does not determine the choice, retain several conditional paths. The analysis must not decide for the actor in order to continue the projection.

Later projection starts from explicitly stated conditional state differences and inherits unresolved unknowns and restrictions. When real feedback arrives, first verify its source and event status. Then compare the original path, competing paths, and the simple baseline. Distinguish factual errors, mechanism errors, different choices of action, deviations in execution, and external changes. The corrected result should change the states, parameters, scope of mechanisms, or paths used in the next round. If only the text of the feedback is kept while the same choice rule is retained, it must not be claimed that the model has learned.

[^p12-tn-circle]: Translator's note: *Circle* renders 圈层. Circles may be parallel, nested, overlapping, bridged, or competing, and may also form only temporarily around particular events; the term does not imply a hierarchy.

[^p12-tn-contract]: Translator's note: *Contract* renders 合同 in an analytical sense: a specification registered and frozen before results are known, close to pre-registration. It is not a legal instrument.

<a id="part_12"></a>

# Part 12 Conditional Foresight and Bounded Choice

Projection unfolds conditions and paths. Conditional foresight goes further by making judgments about the future that can fail. Bounded choice compares what can be done, how to do it, when to stop, and when to refrain within the bounds of values, protection, and authorization. This Part places foresight and choice together to form a closed loop of work. The two are given separate contracts[^p13-tn-contract] to prevent “a consequence was predicted” from being covertly turned into “there is therefore authority to make others take on an action.” Comparing and recommending options are analytical outputs and may be completed when normative premises and constraints are clear. Real-world adoption and execution, by contrast, must separately pass the applicable protection, review, and authorization procedures.

## 12.1 Keeping the Four Outputs Separate

Explanation, projection, conditional foresight, and bounded choice must be labeled separately.

| Output | Core Question | Required Inputs | Output Ceiling |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Explanation | What has happened, and why might it have happened? | Object, scale, evidence, mechanism, and counterexamples | An account of the mechanism within stated limits |
| Projection | Which paths could unfold after a condition or event? | Frozen snapshot, update rules, clocks, and branches | Conditional paths and signals that distinguish them |
| Conditional foresight | For the stated target and horizon, which path is better supported as an expectation? | Registration before results, a simple baseline, metrics, and write-back | A judgment that can be calibrated and can fail |
| Bounded choice | Compare and recommend options under explicit normative premises. Real-world adoption separately undergoes protection, review, and authorization | Analysis requires normative premises, evidence, options, the price to be paid, and the people affected. Real-world adoption also requires N, PF, J, O, and C12 | A refutable analytical recommendation or an authorized record of bounded choice, with their statuses kept separate |

Explanation may generate candidates for projection, but it must not be taken as automatically establishing what will happen. Projection may generate candidate forecasts, but it must not count every path as a success. Foresight may enter comparisons between options, but it must not be taken as automatically generating values or permissions. Even a bounded choice that passes its checks does not guarantee successful execution in reality. Stopping, rollback, and outcome write-back are still required.

## 12.2 Conditional Forecast Registration

Each forecast is frozen after the input cutoff and before the results appear. The minimum fields include forecast ID, model version, target, horizon, scope of objects and circles[^p13-tn-circle], baseline time, input cutoff, simple baseline, candidate mechanisms, set of paths, probability or ranking expression, calibration plan, decision threshold, early signals, contrary signals, suspension conditions, retirement conditions, outcome write-back, registration time, and evidence references. Include the decision threshold when it is used for the corresponding decision. Otherwise mark it not applicable.

The target must be decidable. For example, “the relationship will improve” must be rewritten to specify which observables change within a stated window, who observes them, what threshold applies, and what outcomes count as unresolved. The horizon may be a window. There is no need to fabricate a precise date. The scope of the objects and circles must be frozen to avoid narrowing it to the successful subgroup after results appear.

| Registration Item | Minimum Question | Bias It Prevents |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Target | When the horizon ends, how will we know whether it occurred, did not occur, or cannot be determined? | Vague success criteria |
| Horizon | When does the window start and end, and when does input collection stop? | Waiting indefinitely |
| Simple baseline | What is the comparison prediction without multi-circle mechanisms? | Letting a complex story win by its own standards |
| Paths | Which paths are mutually exclusive, parallel, or incomparable? | Post hoc selection |
| Expression | Probability, interval, level, or direction alone? | False precision |
| Signals | What would strengthen or weaken support for a path? | Looking only for supporting evidence |
| Write-back | What is the source of the outcome, and when is it appended? | Letting failures disappear |

### 12.2.1 Limits of Local Predictability

Every predictability claim must specify its object, state region, scale and circles, time window, allowable error, available information, computing and observation resources, model version, baseline, abstention conditions, and failure conditions. This framework cannot directly answer whether the world as a whole is predictable. It can test only whether a particular task gains a limited increment within explicit bounds.

Classifications, levels, intervals, and probabilities bear different responsibilities for measurement and expression. They do not form a ladder of precision that every task has to climb in sequence. The chosen form of expression must be supported by the target, units, generation method, identification conditions, and calibration status. If there is no basis even for a qualitative direction, it should be retained as unknown. Direction must not be treated as the default answer when probability is unavailable. For known probabilities in formal models and for empirical probabilities, state the assumptions and limits of extrapolation separately. Text patterns and AI must not be used to invent real-world parameters. When an existing mathematical or physical theory is cited, its original object, assumptions, mathematical limits or scaling laws, error, domain of validity, object mapping, counterexamples, and exit conditions must also be registered. The theory must not be borrowed only for the form of its equations.

### 12.2.2 Evaluation of Recursive Foresight by Order

Freeze and evaluate the first, second, and third orders separately. They must not be combined into a single overall hit rate. Each order must be compared with state continuation without further recursion, a direct longer-horizon judgment, and a shallower order followed by simple continuation. If the third order adds only narrative without stable out-of-sample gains, it may be retained as an exploratory scenario, but it must not be credited with foresight capability.

Without new external material, the source lineage of every child run still ends at the same frozen starting point. Post-processing can add reasoning within the model and reveal sensitivity and hidden consequences, but it must not manufacture evidence about reality. When actual intermediate outcomes arrive, a new frozen run should be created and compared with the original recursive paths. The outcomes must not be fed back into the old paths.

## 12.3 Simple Baselines and Incremental Capability

A multi-circle model claimed to improve foresight must be compared with simple models suited to the task and with existing methods in the domain under the same information, cutoff, target, and evaluation rules. Make the comparison materials, inference budgets, and opportunities for tuning as comparable as possible. State separately what is contributed by additional information, additional computation, and additional structural assumptions. A simple baseline need not be artificially enlarged to match the size of the complex model. Predictive systems generally require independent testing or out-of-sample review. If neither can be obtained, explicitly retain the status that the capability has yet to be established.

Common baselines include continuing the current trend, carrying forward the most recent state, using historical frequencies, using only the actor state, using only the primary circle state, and using an existing model in the domain. Comparisons should use the same target, horizon, data cutoff, and evaluation metrics. If a complex model improves explanation but does not improve prediction, the outcome should be registered truthfully as “a gain in explanation is present, and a gain in foresight is not supported.”

Model complexity also has costs. More variables increase missing data, measurement error, privacy risk, overfitting, and maintenance debt. If a multi-circle model works in only a few cases, its domain of application should be restricted. If gains disappear over time, the multi-circle model should be downgraded or retired. If the simple baseline performs equally well, the simpler model should be preferred.

## 12.4 Probability, Levels, and Time Windows

Where the evidence permits, one may give a probability or probability interval and state the model, prior, data, and grounds for calibration. When there is insufficient basis for a probability, one may give levels or compare paths to the extent supported by evidence. Sample size alone does not determine the type of output. When even the direction or ordering cannot be identified, the analysis should explicitly retain it as unknown and identify observations that could distinguish the alternatives, rather than treat “giving only the direction” as a waiver of evidential responsibility. The strength of the expression depends on the specific evidence and the intended use, not on how much certainty the user wants.

| Evidential Status | Appropriate Expression | What Must Accompany It |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A probability model or empirical estimate with clear grounds and a decidable target | Probability or probability interval | Method of generation, prior if applicable, data, limits of applicability, and calibration status |
| Insufficient basis for probability, but grounds for path levels or ordering | Levels of support or an ordering | Grounds, incomparable items, and contrary signals |
| The direction can be identified, or it remains unknown | A supported conditional direction, or an explicit unknown | Identification conditions, trigger points, minimal observation, and stopping conditions |
| Target or object is unstable | Withhold the forecast | The contract that must be redefined |
