Time windows should match mechanism clocks. Immediate reactions may use a short window. Organizational or institutional changes require a longer one. Compressing a long-term mechanism into a short period can lead to a mistaken finding of no effect. Extrapolating a short-term signal indefinitely can lead to a mistaken finding of a trend. When several clocks coexist, separate horizons may be registered for different intermediate outcomes.

Real-world probabilities, actors’ subjective beliefs, decision weights, a model’s ordering of paths, and the allocation of computation at the next step are distinct quantities. A real-world probability must specify the target event and the basis for estimation or calibration. For a subjective belief, state who is judging, what information they have, and how they reached the judgment. Decision weights are interpreted according to the descriptive model or value premises in use. Computational priorities specify why further investigation is worthwhile. Frequencies of branches obtained by taking more draws from a model must not be treated directly as real-world probabilities. The path most worth investigating or most hoped for must not be described as the most likely.

When probabilities cannot be determined reliably, a path may be investigated first because its consequences are severe, the response window is short, or it is easy to distinguish. In that case, disclose the reason for the priority and the budget for investigating it, and retain the probability as unknown. Percentages must not be invented to establish an ordering. An action comparison still has to state normative objectives, distributional consequences, and feasible choices separately. An ordering by probability does not directly determine the order of recommendations.

Probabilities and levels have different meanings. A model’s conditional probability under given assumptions, uncertainty in an individual judgment, empirical calibration in a long series of repeated tasks, and normative priority must not use one number as though they referred to the same thing. A posterior can be obtained from a small sample and an explicit prior, but its real-world applicability still depends on the model. Even a large sample cannot repair an incorrect definition of what is observed. Without evidence, even the direction can remain unknown. Reducing the claim to direction alone does not automatically make it reliable. 〔Studies 03.04, 07.07〕

## 12.5 Calibration and Evaluation

Foresight capability is established by results registered over time. It does not follow from a style that sounds accurate. Where applicable, evaluate probability tasks with the Brier score, log loss, calibration curves, resolution, and interval coverage. Use pre-registered ranking metrics for ranking tasks. Use precision, recall, or domain metrics for event detection. Use window coverage and lead time for predictions of timing.

Every metric must match the actual use. If low-probability events with severe harms call for caution, evaluation must not rely only on overall accuracy. If the output is used only to prompt observation, the cost of a false positive differs from its cost when the output is used to compel action. Good performance on a metric does not prove that the model’s explanation is correct, still less that the action is justified.

Once early warning is in use, the object of evaluation must specify the response regime. Is the task to predict actual events under an existing regime, or to ask separately what issuing a warning or taking action changed? Retain records of reach, execution, entries and exits of the objects, and how the data were produced, where these affect evaluation. When the regime changes, state again which outcomes are still comparable. The absence of an observed event must not automatically be recorded as a false positive, nor may it be taken on that basis to establish that protection succeeded. A claim about harm avoided separately requires a causal comparison suited to that claim. Predictions already defined under the current response regime may still be evaluated against the original target. There is no need to turn every prediction task into a study of intervention effects. The original target, horizon, and success rules must not be rewritten after the fact because a response took place (Perdomo et al., 2020). See also §2.4 and §12.6.

| Evaluation Dimension | Question | Action After Failure |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Calibration | Of events assigned 70%, do roughly 70% occur over the long run? | Adjust the expression or downgrade probability outputs |
| Resolution | Can the model distinguish different risks or paths? | Remove variables that make no contribution and return to the baseline |
| Coverage | Do intervals or time windows cover the actual outcomes? | Widen intervals or revise the clocks |
| Gain over baseline | Does it outperform the simple model? | Stop making foresight claims for the complex model |
| Stability | Does performance hold across times, locations, and boundaries? | Restrict the domain of application or retire the model |
| Distribution of error | At which positions do persistent underestimates or false positives occur? | Review by people affected, remedy, and stronger protection |

Three parallel questions can fix the terms of the evaluation.

| Question | What Sufficient Material Can Answer | What Must Be Retained |
| --- | --- | --- |
| How did the original prediction perform on the frozen target? | Compute loss, classification outcomes, or coverage using actual labels defined on the same basis | A single-case score does not amount to long-run calibration. Do not enter zero for missing labels |
| How much did publication or action change the outcome? | Give a qualified effect estimate based on a causal comparison suited to the question | An observed absence of the event cannot by itself distinguish initially low risk from successful prevention |
| Is it appropriate to adopt this prediction and response? | Compare protection, false rejections, delay, distribution, and cost under explicit normative premises | Neither predictive accuracy nor a positive effect by itself confers permission |

For example, suppose a forecast registered in advance assigns an event probability of 0.8 under the current response regime, and the complete label at the end of the horizon is 0. The Brier loss for that case is 0.64. If the threshold for binary classification was set in advance at 0.5, a false positive may also be registered for that target. This limited calculation does not show the risk without action, still less that the response was ineffective. If monitoring was offline and no label was obtained, the same calculation must not be used. Complete labels for people who were approved or received services can directly evaluate only the corresponding population. Extending the evaluation to people who were rejected, were not reached, or exited requires labels for them or well-grounded methods of identification and bounding.

After a change in the response regime, first check whether the original target is still the same question. If the target is retained, report the performance change as observed. If the target shifts to a different response regime or population, create a new registration and retain the old results. Reflexivity must not be used to cancel a score that can be computed. One successful response must not be used to rescue all incorrect predictions after the fact.

## 12.6 Early Signals, Contrary Signals, and Trigger Points

An early signal is not a scaled-down version of the outcome but an observable change that the path’s mechanism should produce first. A contrary signal indicates that a key condition of the path is failing or that a competing path is gaining support. Both must be registered before results to avoid collecting only supporting material.

Trigger points connect foresight to review. Observing a signal calls for reassessing the object, path, probability, normative grounds, and authorization. It does not automatically trigger action. High-risk cases should also have veto triggers, such as a breach of the protection floor, inability of key affected people to exit, distortion of data sources, or deteriorating model calibration.

Use completion of a feasible action as the endpoint when comparing clocks. Detecting the signal, verifying it, publishing the warning, reaching people, understanding the warning, gathering resources, carrying out a transfer, and a measure taking effect each involve a delay. Earlier algorithmic detection does not guarantee that the last person affected can receive protection before harm occurs. If no harm follows an alert, use the frozen target and labeling conditions to decide whether an operational false positive may be registered. Identify separately whether a successful response prevented harm. Keep the denominators separate for the operational false positive rate, the proportion of false alerts among alerts, and the missed detection rate. 〔Studies 31.05〕

## 12.7 Failure Semantics for Forecasts

When the horizon ends, the result can be supported, unsupported, unresolved, target invalid, or not evaluable. Unresolved is the state of insufficient evidence specified by the contract. It is no compromise between success and failure. A result that cannot be evaluated must state what went wrong with the data, object, horizon, or execution. An invalid target means the object of the prediction or its identity criterion has undergone conversion. It must not be counted as a hit.

If the target, horizon, or scope of circles is changed after results, or several paths are merged into “it happened to some extent,” the original forecast must not retain its eligibility to count as a success. A new interpretation may establish a new version, but the old record continues to exist.

## 12.8 The Bridge From Foresight to Bounded Choice

Foresight outputs answer only questions about conditional consequences. An analytical recommendation requires explicit normative premises, evidence, affected positions, options, costs and benefits, and conditions for revising the judgment. A recommendation does not confer permission to execute. Before real-world adoption, the record must also include explicit normative premises, the choosing subject, jurisdiction, affected positions, rights floors, options, the distribution of costs and benefits, authorization, stopping, appeal, rollback, and remedy. N, PF, J, O, and C12 still apply. Prediction provides no shortcut.

Prediction must not be taken as automatically conferring authorization. Even an extremely probable path does not show that anyone has authority to compel others to bear the cost of avoiding it. Even the highest average benefit does not show that the rights of positions in the minority may be ignored. Even if the model recommends taking no action, continuing harm and responsibility arising from inaction still require review.

## 12.9 The Option Set Must Include Action and No Action

When generating options, check the applicability of each of six categories: maintaining the status quo, acting proactively, delaying action, taking a small exploratory action, exiting or transferring, and explicitly taking no action. For each applicable category, develop genuine and comparable options. Explain why any category does not apply. Options must not be invented to meet a quota. If a choice is requested, the explicit no-action baseline must not be removed, and maintaining the status quo must not be merged with taking no action. Maintaining the status quo may require ongoing resources and execution. Taking no action may allow existing conditions to change on their own. Specific tasks may add options. Tasks that do not request a choice need not produce a list of actions.

| Option Type | Main Use | Risks That Must Be Checked |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Maintain the status quo | Protect existing stability and uptake arrangements | Hidden costs, continuing harm, and lock-in |
| Proactive action | Change a path or its conditions quickly | Permissions, irreversibility, and cross-circle spillovers |
| Delayed action | Wait for information, procedure, or the right time | Harm and lost opportunities from delay itself |
| Exploratory action | Obtain information through a small reversible step | Who bears the costs of the trial and whether it can actually be rolled back |
| Exit or transfer | End an unsuitable object or relationship | People unable to exit, responsibilities, and preservation of history |
| No action | Avoid overstepping authority or taking high-risk action | Bias toward the default, continuing consequences, and responsibility for inaction |

Taking no action does not mean that nothing happens. Other actors, circles, resources, and clocks are still changing. The no-action option must have a baseline time, expected path, observation plan, conditions for reopening the decision, and a responsible party.

## 12.10 A Common Card for Comparing Options

Compare each option against the same baseline. Register at least the option description, forecast reference, normative premises, people affected, rights floors, expected path, worst acceptable outcome, cross-circle spillovers, distribution of costs and benefits, value of information, risk of lock-in, reversibility, resource costs, implementer, authorization, stopping, rollback, and remedy.

| Dimension of Comparison | Question | What Must Not Substitute |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Target and path | Which path is the option intended to change? | An abstract promise to “make things better” |
| Protection floor | Who might suffer unacceptable harm? | Average benefit |
| Distribution | Who benefits, who pays, and when? | The aggregate net value |
| Cross-circle spillovers | Does local improvement externalize costs? | Metrics from the primary circle |
| Value of information | Can the option distinguish mechanisms or paths? | The dramatic character of the action itself |
| Reversibility and lock-in | If it goes wrong, can it be stopped, reversed, or remedied? | Verbal promises |
| Permissions | Who may decide, execute, and appeal? | A model’s suggestion or a job title |
| No action | What path will unfold if nothing is done? | An assumption of zero cost |

If options cannot be compared on a single scale, the outcomes on multiple dimensions should be retained and the conflicts disclosed rather than forced into a sum. Conflicts among value premises should be handled by qualified subjects and procedures. The model should fully show where conflicts arise and what their consequences are. It may offer a preference and reasons for deliberation under openly stated normative premises. It must not make a binding final decision in place of a qualified subject.

The value of information can be written as a limited formal object. Let a denote the action, θ the state, Z the information, and U the utility accepted in advance. Then `EVSI=E_Z[max_a E(U|Z)]−max_a E(U)`. EVSI is nonnegative when Z is free to obtain and can be ignored, and when the action set and objective remain unchanged. Actual investigation also consumes time, privacy, and resources. It may miss a window or change the available actions. Its net value should therefore be counted separately. It is also possible that two clues change the action only when both are obtained. The value of knowledge itself for understanding can exist independently and need not be converted in full into this quantity for decision-making. 〔Studies 07.08, formal statement of conditions〕

### 12.10.1 From Identifiable Answers to Feasible Choices

A question can have a sufficient answer while action remains infeasible. There can also be a commonly acceptable action before all states have been identified. For each state z compatible with the available material, let `A_ok(z)` denote the actions acceptable under the disclosed minimum protections, resources, and valid permissions. In a finite, single-round deterministic choice, if the intersection `∩ A_ok(z)` is nonempty, the common options can be compared without first distinguishing all states. This gives one positive condition under which bounded action is still possible while unknowns remain. If the intersection is empty, “there is a solution for every state” is insufficient to establish that the right one can be chosen now. The analysis should check whether obtaining more evidence, waiting, narrowing the task, or taking a protective action with independent grounds could change the conditions.

The intersection concerns only the credible states listed and the explicit constraints. It does not guarantee that unknown states will never arise. Risk probabilities, costs, or normative tolerance can support other choice rules, but they must be stated explicitly. The worst-case criterion must not be concealed as the only rational one. Permissible randomized or sequential strategies must also be analyzed separately for their respective objectives. The single-round condition above does not rule them out.

### 12.10.2 Joint Feasibility of Multiple Options

When several actions are feasible individually, also check whether implementing them at the same time would draw on the same capacity, budget, staff, and stopping channels. If two units of service must both be completed in the current period and it has capacity for only one unit, each is feasible on its own but they are infeasible together. Added capacity next period cannot recover a deadline already missed in this period. Adding capacity in the same period or adjusting the deadline where there are grounds and approval has been obtained can yield a new feasible option, but this must not be used as a basis for rewriting the old task as completed.

Joint comparison should therefore account for resource use, task dependencies, and key deadlines together so different departments do not commit the same resource twice. Parallel work shortens waiting only when conditions allow. Where investigation, authorization, implementation, and taking effect depend on one another in sequence, they must still follow that order. Treating completion of the first node as proof that the last affected person is already protected misjudges both timing and coverage.

## 12.11 Action Ceilings and Trials to Obtain Information

Evidence, reversibility, permissions, and protection together determine the action ceiling. When evidence is weak, risk high, action irreversible, or permissions insufficient, an appropriate output may be to observe first, narrow the action, improve the information, protect people affected, initiate an appeal, or take no action for now.

A trial to obtain information is not a “small action” exempt from responsibility. It must still specify the trial object, people affected, minimum scope, reversibility, stopping, data uses, and remedy. Those in low-power positions must not be used as material for model calibration. Participation must not be described as voluntary where there is no genuine ability to exit.

| Combination of Conditions | Example of the Permitted Ceiling |
| --- | --- |
| Weak evidence, low harm, reversibility, and clear authorization | Small-scale observation or exploratory action |
| Moderate evidence, manageable harm, and a feasible rollback | Phased action with a stringent stopping check |
| Strong evidence but insufficient permissions | Submission to a subject with authority, without independent execution |
| Strong evidence, but the action is irreversible and protection requirements are unmet | Suspension, and completion of the missing procedures and protections |
| Urgent safety threat | Only the minimum protective action under external emergency authorization, reviewed as soon as possible |

## 12.12 Outcome Write-Back and Model Learning

Writing back outcomes for foresight and choices is append-only. The record includes the time of outcome observation, source, actual outcome, match to paths, calibration metrics, comparison with the simple baseline, unexpected effects, review at affected positions, model updates, downgraded assertions, retired variables or paths, and the next review.

Action outcomes must not be evaluated only against the main target. Also check side effects, cross-circle spillovers, cost distribution, appeals, people unable to exit, and long-term clocks. One success does not establish that a mechanism holds universally. One failure may be due to a deviation in execution. Use the frozen contract to distinguish evidence about the mechanism from deviations in execution. Explanations about execution must not be used arbitrarily to rescue the model.

## 12.13 A Complete Example of Foresight and Choice

Continue with the public departure from a job. A conditional forecast could set the target as “whether voluntary turnover in the team over the next three months is clearly above the historical baseline and the baseline for similar teams,” with an input cutoff one week after the public statement. The simple baseline uses historical departure rates and trends in working hours. The multi-circle model adds team trust, management’s response, bridging by the professional community, family care, and platform exposure. Possible paths include public attention fading while internal problems persist, institutional adjustments followed by gradual repair, and external bridging that triggers successive exits.

Early signals include actual changes in working hours, handling of appeals, statements by members, hiring, and outside opportunities. Contrary signals include a policy being announced without execution, or public quiet accompanied by increasing silence internally. After three months, compare the multi-circle model with the simple baseline. Success must not be declared by picking out only one intermediate signal that happened to match.

Bounded choice should compare at least maintaining the status quo, responding only through public relations, immediately adjusting resources, starting with a reversible pilot for working hours and appeals, delaying major reorganization to gather information, allowing team members to transfer, and taking no structural action for now while strengthening protection and observation. Each option requires checking permissions, people unable to exit, the distribution of costs, reputational effects across circles and effects on families, stopping, and remedy. The model can show which options are more likely to change the path under which conditions. The final choice still depends on normative premises, lawful authorization, and procedures for the people affected. Under these conditions, the model should also state its current analytical recommendation, why other options were not selected, the price to be paid, and the conditions for revising the judgment. A recommendation does not change the status of execution permission that has yet to be obtained.

## 12.14 When to Choose Not to Act

Taking no action can be prudent or evasive. Taking no action for now or limiting activity to protective observation can be warranted when the object and facts are unclear, an action is irreversible and evidence insufficient, permissions are missing, or the protection floor is unmet. It can also be warranted when the proposed action is expected to shift costs to people unable to exit, neither the simple baseline nor the complex model can distinguish the paths, or external intervention could interrupt an ongoing recovery.

Taking no action must be subject to stricter review when harm is ongoing, inaction reinforces entrenched advantages, the responsible subject has a clear statutory duty or commitment, or people in low-power positions cannot exit on their own. The same applies when delay would create irreversible lock-in or when “waiting for more evidence” is used to defer indefinitely. In these cases, even if the main action is not taken, minimum protection, disclosure, appeal channels, or temporary uptake may be needed.

## 12.15 Where This Part Stops

The conditional foresight layer may publish judgments with a target, horizon, baseline, signals, calibration, and write-back. When norms, protection, and authorization are all clear, the bounded choice layer may compare action, delay, exploration, exit, and no action, and provide a conditional, withdrawable choice record. Even when execution has not been authorized, an analytical recommendation may still be completed within what the evidence and explicit normative premises allow. It must fully explain the options, opposing arguments, distributional consequences, and conditions for revising the judgment. Its status for real-world adoption must be registered separately.

These layers cannot guarantee the future or eliminate conflicts of value. They must not replace law, professional norms, participation by people affected, or real-world authorization, nor may they turn model accuracy into a qualification to rule. Any use that exceeds these boundaries should be blocked by the tool gate in Part 13 and governed, suspended, or retired under Part 16.

## 12.16 Format for Publication to Readers

When publishing externally, deliver the full analysis by default. Open with the current judgment and its scope, then develop the conditions, evidence, and uncertainty in full. The main text should retain the fields that apply to the current kind of output, including the current cutoff, target and horizon, scope of objects and circles, simple baseline, all substantive path comparisons already performed, the conditions for each path to hold, early and contrary signals, strength of expression, suspension, and the next review. Substantive analysis must not be omitted on the grounds that it does not change the main conclusion, belongs to a secondary branch, or is not a decisive reason. Conditions must not be hidden in footnotes, and “the model thinks” must not stand in for evidential responsibility. Shorten the visible answer only when the user explicitly asks for a brief reply or specifies a shorter length, while retaining the full analysis. If the analysis exceeds the capacity of one delivery, deliver it section by section and provide the complete file, stating what has yet to be delivered. Summaries, links to attachments, and coverage markers must not substitute for the argument in the main text.

An analysis of bounded choice should state explicitly that it has entered the normative layer. It should list the value premises, deciding subject, affected positions, protection floor, available options, distributional consequences of action and no action, authorization status, action ceiling, stopping, rollback, and remedy in full. The current analytical recommendation may come first, followed by the complete reasons for accepting and ruling out each option, the price to be paid, objections, and conditions for revising the judgment. If authorization has yet to be obtained, state clearly that the recommendation is for deliberation and that execution in reality has yet to be authorized. An imperative must not be used to make a recommendation look like a decision that can be executed. The lack of execution permission must not be used as a reason to reduce an analytical choice that already has grounds to a list of questions.

| Publication Status | Permitted Wording | What Must Be Avoided |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Explanation only | “Current evidence supports mechanism A and leaves B open.” | “Therefore, the next step will necessarily occur.” |
| Projection only | “If X continues and Y occurs, path P receives support.” | “The model predicts that P is already certain.” |
| Registered forecast | “Within horizon T, P currently ranks above Q; see contrary signal S.” | Hiding the baseline, horizon, or failure conditions |
| Normative checks not yet passed | “An analytical recommendation may be made under explicit premises. Real-world execution still requires N, PF, J, and O.” | “Authorization has yet to be obtained, but execution can begin immediately.” |
| Externally authorized | “The authorization record permits the subject to execute within the ceiling and stop under the stated conditions.” | Attributing authorization to the model |

Interpretive authority after publication is also subject to governance. If readers mistake conditional foresight for a certain prophecy, a candidate personality hypothesis for a fact, or an option comparison for an order, the publisher should correct the misunderstanding without exploiting it to increase the publication’s impact. Append any update as a new version and keep the original title, probabilities, horizon, and failure record traceable.

[^p13-tn-contract]: Translator's note: *Contract* renders 合同 in an analytical sense: a specification registered and frozen before results are known, close to pre-registration. It is not a legal instrument.

[^p13-tn-circle]: Translator's note: *Circle* renders 圈层. Circles may be parallel, nested, overlapping, bridged, or competing, and may also form only temporarily around particular events; the term does not imply a hierarchy.

<a id="part_13"></a>

# Part 13 Interfaces and Tools

This Part converts the objects, propositions, scales, mechanisms, actors, multi-circle joint states, and dynamic projection requirements defined in the preceding 12 Parts into limited judgments that can be observed, questioned, reviewed, and withdrawn. It adds no facts about the world, nor does it treat the completeness of a form as the establishment of a fact. A D0 candidate object record and a nine-axis scale record must exist before the tool workflow begins. If the object, boundary, scale, time window, identity criterion, or intended use changes, existing judgments must not simply be carried over.

The tool workflow has a two-tier structure: the seven gates are the only main workflow, and the five gates and 13 steps are the layer that elaborates the diagnosis[^p14-tn-diagnosis]. The seven gates answer whether a judgment can proceed to the next layer. In complex cases the five gates and 13 steps spell out the material that must be submitted, but they do not issue a separate “pass” conclusion that competes with the seven gates, nor do they produce a second set of workflow statuses.

The most important boundary is this: the tool layer confers no authorization. Even when the workflow, evidence, and strong-judgment kit are all complete, the outputs are limited to descriptions, candidate mechanisms, comparative judgments, strong-judgment candidates, or requirements for action. Real-world action still requires a separate structured choice record, the 10-component C12 bridge, one and the same valid atomic J authorization, and O1–O4. A tool output must not substitute for any of them.

The following workflow governs structured judgments formed under this framework and their handoff. Reading, checking against the original text, and clarifying a low-impact question may use concise records suited to the task. Established facts in the domain must not be declared nonexistent because the full tool workflow has not been run. When the work enters formal diagnosis, foresight, or high-impact use, however, the corresponding full contract[^p14-tn-contract] must be satisfied, and responsibility must not be reduced by renaming the output.

## 13.1 Four Conversions

The four conversions do not swap theoretical terms for real-world labels; each successive conversion adds responsibility for recording. If any step fails, the outputs that depend on it stop before that step. The formal completeness of a later step must not be used to make up for the failure. Other independent judgments are reviewed separately on their own grounds and intended uses.

| ID | Conversion | Required Result | Where to Stop If It Fails |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| IT1 | Structural variables to observable signals | A record of signals that specifies the object, scale, observation position, time window, measurement protocol, and missing-data status | A conceptual mapping or a gap in the material |
| IT2 | Observable signals to strength of evidence | An evidence record that separates sources, observations, comparison conditions, coverage, counterexamples, alternative explanations, and uncertainty | Ungraded material or a hypothesis to test |
| IT3 | Strength of evidence to level of judgment | A judgment with a proposition contract, limits of applicability, contrary conditions, falsification conditions, and withdrawal conditions | A candidate explanation or an observational assessment |
| IT4 | Level of judgment to requirements and ceiling for action | A list of affected positions, the protection floor, prohibited actions, stopping, appeal, rollback, and outstanding N/J/O/C12 requirements | Description, a need for more evidence, or a requirement to take no action |

IT1 prevents a narrative about structure from being treated as an observed fact. IT2 prevents citations, materials, or declarations of position from being treated directly as evidence. IT3 prevents correlation, analogy, or a story built on a single mechanism from being elevated to a conclusion. IT4 prevents a direct leap from a factual judgment to action. Passing all four conversions leaves authorization status unchanged.

If any IT conversion fails, dependent later conversions may only be marked `not_run`, and the propositions, comparative judgments, strong-judgment candidates, and requirements for action that they support must be stopped or downgraded. Downstream conclusions that share the same gap must not regain their ceiling through renaming, splitting, or opening another branch. A later `not_run` only preserves the chain of failure, and even a complete kit must not be used to repair the break. A judgment may retain its original standing if it does not depend on the failed step, has independent grounds, and its use meets its own requirements. If the gap affects the overall source status, a shared factual premise, the object boundary, or lawful conditions for use of the entire task, all dependent outputs must be stopped or downgraded together. If a gate required for high-impact adoption fails, the same judgment must not retain its original use merely by being relabeled an ordinary opinion.

## 13.2 Using the Universal Primitives and Human Variable Interfaces (11+11)

HV01–HV11 in Part 7 are the 11 interfaces for the human structured world. U01–U11 in Part 3 are the universal structural primitives. The tool layer may invoke both sets side by side in the same case, but it does not interpret matching numbers, equal counts, or similar questions as one-to-one equivalence.

When these interfaces are invoked, at least the following must be registered: the universal or human interface ID, effective object, nine-axis scale, selected support route, instance evidence, alternative explanations, missing-data status, judgment ceiling, and action ceiling. H1/H4/H5 may enter the specified routes only through the corresponding H-instance in each case. G1–G4 may support empirical claims only through a G-instance frozen before results. An interface card can raise questions, but filling in its fields cannot establish facts about the object.

## 13.3 The Seven Gates as the Only Main Workflow

The seven gates are run for each specific proposition, in dependency order. After one gate fails, dependent later gates may only be marked `not_run`, and the corresponding judgments and requirements for action stop or are downgraded. A later `not_run` must not raise the ceiling already lowered by the failed gate. Acknowledging that the object or evidence failed a gate while allowing dependent later procedures to report their own pass is also prohibited. Judgments that do not depend on the failed step must retain independent evidence and undergo review of their intended use. The passing of another proposition must not be used to conceal this proposition’s failure. If overall source status, a shared factual premise, the object boundary, or lawful conditions for use of the task cease to hold, all dependent outputs stop together. When the object, evidence version, affected positions, or intended use changes substantially, rerun the workflow from the earliest gate affected.

| ID | Gate | Core Question | Output Ceiling If It Does Not Pass |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| IG1 | Effective Object Gate | Do the object, boundary, nine-axis scale, time window, and K support the current judgment? | Redefine the object, leave the scale unknown, or review object conversion |
| IG2 | Evidence Traceability Gate | Are sources, materials, observations, evidence, counterexamples, cases, and judgments distinct and traceable? | Organization of materials, candidate questions, or hypotheses to test |
| IG3 | Affected Positions Gate | Which positions will see their circumstances change through direct, indirect, second-order, cross-domain, or intertemporal effects? | Supplement the accounting of impacts. Suspend strong judgments and high-impact uses |
| IG4 | Power and Anti-Retaliation Gate | Can people in low-power positions safely add evidence, rebut claims, refuse, stop, and appeal? | Adverse inferences must not be drawn from silence. Lower the levels of publication and judgment |
| IG5 | Mediation and Reflexivity Gate | Do metrics, platforms, AI, public evaluation, or the act of diagnosis change the evidence and the object? | High reflexivity remains unresolved. Restate the judgment as a conditional state transition |
| IG6 | Strong-Judgment Procedure Gate | Have all SJ1–SJ8 items been submitted for the proposition whose intended use and foreseeable impacts trigger the strong-judgment procedure? Public dissemination requires closer scrutiny, but it does not by itself elevate all analyses to strong judgments | Stop the relevant strong judgments and high-impact uses. Review separately any comparative judgment that stands independently and meets the requirements for its use |
| IG7 | Action Ceiling Gate | What is the most that may be required? Which actions are prohibited, and when are stopping, appeal, and rollback called for? | Description, gaps in evidence, no action, or continued deliberation |

Passing all seven gates indicates only that the corresponding recording checks have no gaps. It does not indicate that the conclusion is true. Empirical propositions are still judged under their proposition contracts and instance evidence. Normative objectives still require explicit N premises. Real-world action still requires choice, C12, J, and O procedures. In particular, IG7 may only record whether the external normative and authorization layers are in place; the tool workflow must not itself record an action as “authorized.”

IG6 has a specific applicability rule. An ordinary L1/L2 output may mark it `not_applicable`, but the record must state why the strong-judgment procedure is not triggered in this case. This does not block IG7. Once IG6 is marked `passed`, the same run record must link a complete SJ1–SJ8, with every item marked `passed`. A “checked” string or an empty rationale must not be used in place of the eight results.

## 13.4 The Five Gates and 13 Steps for Elaborating a Diagnosis

Run the five gates and 13 steps when the object is complex, responsibility is high, reflexivity is high, or the seven gates call for more detailed evidence. The five gates are local checks within this elaboration. They do not generate a second set of main workflow statuses.

| ID | Elaboration Gate | Material That Must Be Developed | Corresponding Main Gates |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| FG1 | Object Gate | Object, boundary, scale, and time window | IG1 |
| FG2 | Evidence Gate | Cost of signals, sources, weights, and review | IG2 |
| FG3 | Scale Gate | Nine-axis scale, objects and evidence at the scale, and changes in scale | IG1, IG2 |
| FG4 | Responsibility Gate | Effects on rights/resources, contrary conditions, repair windows, appeals, and review | IG3, IG4, IG6 |
| FG5 | Observation Gate | Baseline before observation, reactions during observation, reactions after publication, and a ceiling on recursion | IG5 |

The 13 steps form an auditable sequence from the real-world question to write-back.

| ID | Step | Minimum Deliverable |
| --- | --- | --- |
| DS01 | Start With the Question | A real-world question in language outside the framework, the requester, and the intended use |
| DS02 | Identify the Object and Boundary | Effective object, subsystems, higher-level environment, and external channels |
| DS03 | Separate Signal Types | Separate columns for facts, behavior, resources, feedback, narratives, feelings, conjecture, and noise |
| DS04 | Record Participation in Observation | Awareness of being observed, observer power, impacts of publication, and candidate responses |
| DS05 | Identify the Dominant Constraint | Candidate constraints, the selected constraint, evidence for selection, and competing constraints |
| DS06 | Record the State and Window | References to S0–S6 prototypes for applicable objects, positions within the state, parallel subsystems, and contrary conditions. If inapplicable, state why and retain the domain’s own states and windows |
| DS07 | Examine the Core Structure | Anchor, uptake layer, transmission chain, boundary, and feedback write-back |
| DS08 | Identify Key Variables for Protection | Variables to protect, paths to harm, resources, redundancy, and paths for repair/return flows |
| DS09 | Record Uptake and Repayment | Cost bearers, recipients of return flows, rights to stop, long-term overextension, and shifting of harms |
| DS10 | Identify Hidden Risks and Boundary Types | Maintenance debt, mismatches between fast and slow processes, metric substitution, distorted summaries, and candidate object conversions |
| DS11 | Propose Candidate Mechanisms | Competing mechanisms that the material permits and that are sufficient to change the judgment, the evidence each requires, evidence that weakens them, and rules for eliminating them. If no plausible competing explanation exists, state the scope of the search and the failure conditions; mechanisms must not be invented to meet a quota |
| DS12 | Anticipate Branches | Triggers, early signals, contrary conditions, repair windows, and minimum requirements for action |
| DS13 | Produce the Output and Write Back | Output in ordinary language, triggers for revision, scope of the record, review, withdrawal, and rollback |

When DS06 uses S0–S6, it must do so only for human intentional collectives to which the prototypes apply. The prototypes must not be forced onto other objects. S0–S6 must not be treated as levels of maturity or as fate. Identifying an important variable for protection in DS08 may generate only a requirement for protection. Identifying uptake or overextension in DS09 must not generate a duty to make sacrifices or full responsibility. Candidate mechanisms in DS11 must not bypass the causal contract. Branches in DS12 do not describe a certain future. If DS13 lacks review and withdrawal procedures, the judgment must not be entered into organizational records, eligibility, allocation, or public memory.

## 13.5 The Strong-Judgment Kit

Strong judgments are defined by their uses and foreseeable impacts. Tone, length, and confidence of expression do not determine the category. A judgment enters the strong-judgment procedure if it is intended or reasonably foreseeable for use in real-world action or decisions about eligibility, rights, or resource allocation. The same applies if it has a material impact on a specific subject’s reputation and place in public memory, or if it is embedded in automated, organizational, or institutional decisions. When intermediate scores, recommendations, or material screening affect these decisions through later stages, their actual use must likewise be checked. Public dissemination calls for greater scrutiny. It should be assessed in light of the object evaluated, factual allegations, identifiability, reach, and consequences. Publication alone is insufficient to raise every analytical output to a strong judgment. Ordinary evaluations of arguments, explanations of mechanisms, or normative opinions that do not touch the uses and impacts above may state definite conclusions under evidence and review requirements commensurate with their tasks. An output that actually meets the strong-judgment conditions must not be exempted because it calls itself commentary, research, or a draft. A strong-judgment candidate may be registered only at L3, with all seven gates passed and each of the following eight items passed individually. It still confers no authorization to act in reality.

| ID | Component | Required Question |
| --- | --- | --- |
| SJ1 | Contrary conditions | What circumstances would weaken, reverse, or lead to withdrawal of the judgment? |
| SJ2 | Repair window | Where is the window, what supports it, and when does it cease to hold? |
| SJ3 | Evidence requirements | What evidence supports the judgment, what evidence is missing, and what evidence weighs against it? |
| SJ4 | Access to appeal | Who can file an appeal, who is responsible, what is the time limit, and can the outcome change? |
| SJ5 | Anti-retaliation protection | Which activities are protected, how is protection monitored, and how can retaliation be reported independently and remedied? |
| SJ6 | Right to add evidence | How can evidence be supplemented, versions corrected, and downstream records synchronized? |
| SJ7 | Trigger for external review | When is review triggered, who reviews independently, and can they access the materials and stop the result from taking effect? |
| SJ8 | Rollback and write-back | Which state is restored, who carries out the repair, which version is authoritative, and how are updates synchronized? |

If any item is missing, IG6 fails for that strong judgment. The judgment must not enter the category of strong-judgment candidate or retain its original high-impact use. It may be recorded only as an internal hypothesis, an open assertion, or a candidate mechanism within the limits of its materials and actual use. An L2 comparative judgment may remain if it does not depend on the missing item, has independent grounds, and does not trigger a strong-judgment use. Failure of a common premise still blocks all dependent judgments. Renaming or splitting must not be used to evade that limit. Access to appeal does not count as an effective appeal if it cannot change a record or action. Being external in name does not amount to independence. A statement, apology, or release of a new version does not amount to actual rollback and repair.

## 13.6 Diagnostic Levels

| ID | Level | When It Applies | Output Ceiling |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| L1 | Lightweight description and exploratory questions | Low complexity, low impact, reversibility, and limited information | Description, questions, and the next observation |
| L2 | Standard reviewable judgment | A structural comparison is needed, but the judgment will not be used for action carrying high responsibility | A comparative judgment within stated limits or a candidate mechanism |
| L3 | Strong-judgment candidate carrying high responsibility | Judgments with substantively high impact or embedded in automated, organizational, or institutional decisions. Public dissemination is reviewed in light of the object, factual allegations, identifiability, and consequences | A strong-judgment candidate after the seven gates and the kit pass |

A higher level adds responsibility for recording and does not make a judgment “closer to the truth.” The level must be downgraded if evidence coverage declines, the object undergoes conversion, reflexivity becomes uncontrolled, or appeals or anti-retaliation protection fail. Even in an emergency, the object, protection, or stopping conditions must not be removed. When the price of waiting is high, only the narrowest, withdrawable, monitorable requirement for action may be proposed separately, outside the corresponding authorization.

## 13.7 Six Formal Tools

| ID | Tool | Purpose and Limits |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `TOOL-EVIDENCE-LEDGER` | Source–Evidence Ledger | Keep sources, materials, observations, evidence, counterexamples, coverage, and uncertainty separate. It does not prove a proposition |
| `TOOL-OPEN-ASSERTION` | Open Assertion Record | Retain a provisional judgment that can be questioned, falsified, or withdrawn. It must not be used for action carrying high responsibility |
| `TOOL-CLAIM-VALIDATION` | Proposition Validation Form | Register proposition type, evidence, counterevidence, falsification conditions, impacts, and ceilings separately |
| `TOOL-FORECAST-REGISTRY` | Forecast Registration | Freeze the prediction, window, triggers, early signals, contrary signals, and targets for revision |
| `TOOL-STRESS-TEST` | Counterexample and Scale Stress Test | Use boundary cases, adversarial explanations, and changes in scale to locate failures and narrow the scope |
| `TOOL-AI-BOUNDARY` | AI Output Boundaries | Require disclosure of sources, identification of missing material and the responsible human, and recording of uncertainty, prohibited uses, review, and withdrawal |

The evidence ledger must not turn “has a source” into “is supported.” An open assertion must not substitute for proposition validation. A failed prediction must be written back to the relevant proposition. An internal stress test is only a wind tunnel and does not supply external empirical support. Tools may cite one another’s record IDs, but must not validate one another circularly.

Automatic validation is suited to checking closed fields, whether references resolve, consistency of versions and statuses, and whether calculations can be reproduced. It must not replace assessment of materials, testimony from people affected, causal identification, or choices about values. Programs should do the work that can be done mechanically so that readers can examine the actual grounds for judgments. Complete logs and clear prose may be kept separately with references between them.

## 13.8 Limits on the Use of AI

AI may organize materials, check fields, compare versions, generate candidate questions, list alternative explanations, and find inconsistencies in formatting. Where the evidence and normative premises allow, it should also compare mechanisms, form refutable judgments, recommend options, and draft complete institutional arrangements, plans, and commentary. AI must not invent sources, treat information recalled from training as current evidence, express consent on someone’s behalf, interpret silence as consent, take the place of a deciding subject or authorizing authority, or hide uncertainty. It must not make high-risk real-world decisions independently.

AI outputs must state what materials were used, what is missing, which content is observation/inference/analogy, who bears responsibility for the final judgment, how it may be used, and when it is to be reviewed or withdrawn. Where disclosure of identity, retaliation risk, or justified opacity is involved, protective omissions must remain distinguishable from “no evidence.”

Responsibility for the use of AI should also be assigned, according to the actual division of work, to those who provide materials, those who decide objectives and permissions, those who maintain systems, those who carry out actions, and those who review. Facilities and procedures that perform technical steps do not become subjects capable of bearing moral or legal responsibility merely because they output recommendations or execute instructions. Organizations legally capable of bearing a specified responsibility, by contrast, must be assessed separately according to their institutional status and the matter at issue. Greater automation does not put all responsibility on the person who clicks last. Reviewers must have sufficient materials, time, capability, and stopping permissions that can genuinely take effect. The organization must also provide the resources needed for these conditions.

## 13.9 Run Records and Failure Semantics

Each tool run creates a `flow_execution_record` with a closed set of fields. It registers object and scale records, the L1–L3 level, IT1–IT4 results, IG1–IG7 results, whether the five gates and 13 steps were run, whether SJ1–SJ8 were triggered, evidence, alternative explanations, uncertainty, output categories, judgment and action ceilings, normative handoff, appeal, rollback, and status.

The action ceiling uses a closed lattice of output ceilings: `description_only`, `diagnostic_only`, and `requirements_only`. Each grade has fixed permitted output categories and has `can_authorize=false` and `can_execute=false`. The caller must not claim “already authorized” in free text or expand the permitted categories on its own. `action_requirements` only specifies conditions for the external choice and authorization procedures. It is not an executable command.

A normative handoff must link to verifiable external choice, C12, and authorization records, or state explicitly that these are missing. References must specify the object, action, scope, time, and actual grounds for verification. Vague labels, self-reports of having passed, or circular references to the same record cannot establish permission. The implementation determines the specific reference format. This does not change the boundary that the tool must not issue authorization on its own.

Outcome statuses must remain distinct. `passed` means the recording check for that item passed. `failed` means the step ran and failed. `not_run` means it was not run because of an earlier gate or a scope restriction. `unknown`, `not_applicable`, `not_observable`, and `withheld_for_protection` remain four distinct missing-data statuses. Failure must not be rewritten as inapplicability. Concealment for protection must not be rewritten as absence.

The run record’s `authorization_effect` is always `none`. Even when external references for normative choice, C12, J, and O exist, this record only checks them and hands them off. It does not issue authorization on its own. All reviewed outputs must retain an accessible appeal and a means of actual withdrawal or correction. A tool conclusion that cannot be withdrawn must not be put to a high-impact use that is difficult to reverse.

## 13.10 The Nine Gates for Multi-Circle Projection

The seven gates are the only main workflow for explanation and diagnosis. When a task explicitly calls for dynamic projection, conditional foresight, or bounded choice, elaborate that workflow with the nine gates. The nine gates must not bypass the seven gates’ requirements for the object, evidence, affected positions, power, reflexivity, the strong-judgment procedure, and the action ceiling.

| Gate | Core Check | Output on Failure |
| --- | --- | --- |
| DF1 Freeze the Facts | Event type, source, time, evidence cutoff, and disputes | List of factual questions |
| DF2 Joint Object | Actors, circles, relations, membership, and K | Candidate groupings or a return to a single focus |
| DF3 Two Channels and Clocks | Material and experience–meaning conditions relevant to the task, clocks, and unknowns. Mark irrelevant items inapplicable with a reason | Retain the state snapshot. Suspend propagation that depends on missing conditions and review independent paths separately |
| DF4 Propagation Through Mechanisms | Channels, thresholds, delay, feedback, and cascades | Candidate mechanisms and observations that can distinguish them |
| DF5 Branching Paths | Conditions, parent and child nodes, and early and contrary signals | Parallel paths without forced ordering |
| DF6 Forecast Registration | Target, horizon, simple baseline, calibration, and write-back | Scenario projection only |
| DF7 Normative Choice | N, PF, affected people, options, and no action | Suspend recommendations blocked by the gap. Retain evaluations that stand independently, conditional recommendations under explicit premises, and requirements for more evidence. This confers no permission to execute |
| DF8 Authorization and Execution | J, O, responsibility, stopping, rollback, and remedy | External handoff without execution |
| DF9 Outcome Write-Back | Results, comparison with the baseline, deviations, downgrades, and retirement | Retain the failure and open a new version |

The tool may propose candidate circles at DF2 and variable candidates at DF5, but every candidate retains the status `candidate`. If DF6 does not pass, outputs must not use forecast language such as “will happen” or “expected to prove accurate.” If DF7 or DF8 does not pass, outputs must not give executable instructions. Under DF9, overwriting the original run is not permitted.

## 13.11 Limits on AI in Dynamic Runs

AI may organize materials, check original passages that can be located, find gaps in fields, generate and compare competing paths, check contract consistency, and calculate registered metrics. Under explicit normative premises it may also form well-grounded local judgments and recommendations and draft complete texts. AI must not certify a real-world event solely on the basis of its own generation, self-report, or internal procedure. It must not diagnose personality, disclose information on behalf of those concerned, use its own classifications to decide circle boundaries that bind others, treat simulations as facts, settle disputed value premises for others, or issue authorization. Whether external material supports a particular fact must be judged separately according to the material’s status, method, counterexamples, and intended use. Faithfulness to a source does not automatically establish that what it says about the world is true. For a variable automatically proposed by AI, the system must record its status as a model candidate, the minimal test, and prohibited uses. The variable must not be upgraded before the corresponding external evidence is available.

### 13.11.1 Checking AI Materials and the Chain of Tool Actions

When checking a generated answer, examine separately whether the source exists, whether the citation details are correct, whether the original passage supports the sentence, and whether the fact asserted in the sentence has adequate grounds. A given source’s silence on the matter does not mean that the sentence is necessarily wrong. Faithfully restating a mistaken source does not amount to having established the facts. Where local knowledge, oral accounts, or protected materials have no public webpage, appropriate means of verification and limits on public disclosure should be retained. Such materials must not be treated outright as nonexistent.

Retrieval augmentation can improve access to materials and performance on a task. Consistency or uncertainty checks can also detect certain errors. Their effectiveness remains limited by retrieval coverage, shared sources, and the types of error. An error repeated reliably can appear consistent. Several models repeating the same inaccurate material do not supply several independent pieces of evidence. A formal checker can confirm a derivation under stated premises, but cannot establish that those premises hold in reality.

For automated action, the recommendation, tool request, application execution, external state, and consequences should be recorded separately. The model writing “stopped” does not establish that the executor received a stop instruction. An interface showing “deleted” does not establish that downstream copies are gone. A person changing a recommendation does not establish that the change took effect before an irreversible action. Permissions for actual protection and correction must be exercisable in the corresponding system.

### 13.11.2 Task Endpoints and a Verifiable Execution Chain

The endpoint for verifying a tool chain is determined by the user’s request or the institutional task. If the request is only for a draft, delivery can be complete when the draft has been saved correctly, can be opened, and its content meets the requirements. If the task requires changing an external record, success returned by the tool must still be confirmed by the state of the corresponding external object. If the task requires actual remedy, it must also be verified that the resources, rights, or services reach those concerned. Actual completion of a narrower endpoint need not wait for every consequence in people’s lives before it is recognized. A success receipt in the front end must not substitute for completion of a broader endpoint.

| Stage | Minimum Object of Verification | Typical Break |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Form the recommendation | Objective, materials, object to be changed, and permitted scope | Treating generated text as already adopted |
| Make the tool request | Specific action, parameters, current object version, and permissions | Sending a correct recommendation to the wrong object |
| Execute in the application | Whether the request was received, executed, or rejected, and how repeated requests are handled | A request arrives but is queued, fails, or is executed repeatedly |
| Check external state | Whether the target and dependent objects changed as intended | A local marker changes while actual execution still reads the old state |
| Check the task outcome | The specified benefit or protection endpoint and remaining problems | A corrected record takes effect, but the missed opportunity is not recovered |

For concurrent operations across tools, operations that can read independently, operations that can modify independently, and operations that write to the same object should first be distinguished. Changes to shared state must preserve ordering, version prerequisites, or conflict handling. Before an irreversible action, permissions and stopping conditions must be verified again to confirm that they are still valid. A request timing out means its outcome is unknown. The request must not simply be treated as unexecuted and repeated. The request identifier or object state is checked first, and the decision on whether to retry comes after that check. These are responsibilities for execution. They do not require every application to use the same log format.

Stopping has its own completion conditions: the issuing of a stop instruction, acknowledgment by the executor, termination of actions already in progress, and remedy of consequences that have already occurred are each registered separately. If only the first two steps are confirmed, the corresponding scope must be stated accurately. If winding down safely requires a bounded action under a separate responsibility, that action continues on its own grounds, and the necessary continuation must not be abandoned because the main task has stopped. When repair fails, the object for which repair failed and the real-world consequences must be kept on record, and overwriting them with a new, normal output is prohibited.

[^p14-tn-diagnosis]: Translator's note: Diagnosis here denotes a nonclinical assessment within the framework.

[^p14-tn-contract]: Translator's note: *Contract* renders 合同 in an analytical sense: a specification registered and frozen before results are known, close to pre-registration. It is not a legal instrument.

<a id="part_14"></a>

# Part 14 Normative Choice

The preceding structural grammar, empirical question families, scale transformations, mechanisms of operation, and human variables address “how objects exist, operate, and change, and what grounds we have for explaining them in these ways.” This Part answers a different question: among several possible directions, who may choose, on what grounds, whom does the choice bind, and how do we stop and repair if it fails?

The two kinds of question must be kept separate. An explanation can be highly reliable and still be insufficient to confer authority to act. A goal can receive wide support and still breach rights floors, shift costs to low-power positions, or exceed the decision maker’s jurisdiction. Normative choice is therefore not an appendix to diagnosis but a separate layer of contract[^p15-tn-contract] between explanation and real-world action.

## 14.1 Facts Do Not Entail What Ought to Be Done

Factual propositions describe “what is.” Normative premises state “what is worth pursuing, protecting, or prohibiting.” Claims that “a structure is stable,” “an option is more efficient,” “the system retains a certain type,” or “a risk is rising” cannot directly entail “therefore this should be done.” This does not deny that facts matter for choice; it limits their role: facts determine whether an option is possible, what its consequences might be, and the extent of risk and uncertainty. Facts do not choose values on their own, nor do they create authorization on their own.

Any process that moves from explanation or diagnosis to real-world action must therefore go through four conversions. O1 freezes the facts, evidence, scale, and uncertainty to be used. O2 makes explicit the N1–N5 premises adopted at runtime, conflicts of value, beneficiaries, and those who bear the costs. O3 compares options including no action and reviews the J axis, authorization, jurisdiction, validity period, and conflicts of interest. A separate normative choice record then freezes the decision. Only then can O4 turn the approved option into bounded execution that is reversible, monitorable, stoppable, subject to appeal and rollback, and ends on expiry.

The severity of a diagnosis provides no direct route to action. High model confidence provides no route to broader permission. Stronger evidence can raise the ceiling for descriptive judgments, but cannot raise the action ceiling by itself.

C12 is the validity check for the normative bridge, but C12 does not choose values for you. It checks only whether the descriptive conclusion, current evidence, explicit normative premises, conflicts of value, the protection floor, the J axis, options, no action, O1–O4, the action ceiling, and conditions for correction all hold at the same time. Least harm and proportionality must be explicitly selected, explained, and reviewed in the specific choice record. They must not be left implicit in C12.

## 14.2 Three Distinct Processes of Selection and Choice

Selection and choice cover at least three structurally distinct processes. Conflating them personifies natural or distributed processes, expands personal preferences into public power, or reduces public procedures to the will of those with power.

`SEL-SYS` has no unified deciding subject. It refers to variation or differences producing different outcomes through environments, feedback, competition, replication, and retention mechanisms. Examples include natural selection, differential retention in markets, and content spreading as a result of algorithmic rewards. The unit of selection, environment, retention mechanism, time window, and stochastic null model must be registered. One may say that a form is retained, replicated, or eliminated. One must not say that the system “considers it better.” Retention by itself does not establish normative justification, nor do survival, stability, popularity, or efficiency. The action ceiling for `SEL-SYS` is a description of the selection process. It must not enter `authorized` or include an `external_action` option.

`SEL-AGT` is decision-making by a subject under constraints. The deciding subject must be identifiable and may decide only within their own permissions, genuine options, territory, and validity period. An apparent “choice” must not be recorded as free consent if it arises from coercion, lock-in through defaults, deprivation of information, insufficient capability, unsafe refusal, or blocked exit. Failure to exit does not amount to consent. Entering a process does not amount to continuing consent. One subject’s choice does not automatically bind others.

`SEL-GOV` is collective governance authorized through a procedure. It addresses common affairs, but “common” does not mean that the majority can represent everyone, nor does it mean that the scope of impact is the scope of jurisdiction. A governance decision must make it possible to trace its boundaries, how the agenda was formed, who has authority to represent, which positions can participate, how dissent is preserved, how minorities are protected, how conflicts of interest are handled, who executes and reviews it, and when authorization expires. None of these—numbers, loudness, efficiency, claims to represent, or legal form—may by itself substitute for bounded public authorization.

The three types have strict boundaries. `SEL-SYS` must not acquire intent or normative justification by borrowing the language of subjects. `SEL-AGT` must not decide for others beyond the subject’s own permissions. `SEL-GOV` must not bypass procedure or present governance as an enlarged version of personal choice.

## 14.3 Registering N1–N5 Premises at Runtime

N1–N5 do not operate automatically as background assumptions. Each choice record must list in `public_value_premises` the IDs actually adopted for this choice and say whether each is an objective, a constraint, or a veto check. A normative premise that was not registered must not appear silently in the conclusion.

N1, “Explanation Does Not Confer Authority to Act,” vetoes any direct move from facts to action. On its own, N1 can yield only no action, a need for more evidence, or continued deliberation. It cannot generate an affirmative proposal.

N2, “Protection Must Not Be Downgraded,” requires scale elevation and action to maintain or strengthen existing protections at low-power positions. If the infrastructure for protection does not hold, the action is narrowed rather than the protection.

N3, “High-Impact Judgments Must Be Correctable,” requires genuine access to appeal, independent review, stopping, withdrawal, rollback, remedy, and version write-back.

N4, “Love and Undertaking Must Not Be Conscripted,” prohibits invoking love, responsibility, care, or the persistence of a system to demand unilateral, unlimited sacrifice without an option to exit.

A note on bounded responsibility: what N4 opposes is the use of love, care, or system persistence to demand unilateral, unlimited sacrifice without an option to exit. It does not cancel, across the board, bounded commitments, obligations of support, contractual duties, or remedial responsibilities that already have independent grounds. Ending or transferring a responsibility should specify the actual uptake for dependents, its duration, and the remaining risks. This requirement must not be used to force those harmed to remain exposed to harm. Capability, causal contribution, benefit, fault, and duties of remedy should be argued for separately. 〔Studies 06.06, 09.02, 10.07, 21.06, 23.01–23.07〕

N5, “Persistence and Systemic Selection Do Not Amount to Normative Justification,” limits stability, efficiency, coherence, and outcomes of systemic selection to evidence about consequences. They must not substitute for a judgment of normative justification.

Premises drawn from N can conflict. For example, quickly limiting losses can conflict with full participation. Expanding public benefits can conflict with risks to the rights of those in minority positions. A short reversible trial can conflict with a long-term goal of stability. Conflicts must not be smoothed over with a total score. Each conflict must have a stable ID and list the conflicting premises, affected positions, objections, decision rule, and current status. An unresolved substantive conflict may lead to suspension, a narrower trial, or retention of no action. It must not be “resolved” by deleting objections.

`rights_floor` records the PF IDs actually in force for the current choice. It is not a preference freely exchangeable for efficiency but a boundary that each option must pass first. The rights floor and N premises have distinct roles. N states the grounds for choice, and PF states the conditions of protection that no grounds may override.

## 14.4 Generating Options, Consequences Across Scales, and Distribution

Choice begins by generating a set of comparable options. It does not consist of finding reasons for a preselected option. Each option has a stable `option_id` and freezes its `option_kind`, action type, target object, territory, validity period, reversibility, expected impacts, and distributional impacts. A change to an option’s action, object, territory, or validity period should produce a new version or ID. The same ID must not be quietly altered after review.

Each set of options must have exactly one `no_action`, uniquely referenced by `no_action_option_id`. The no-action option is the baseline path in which the proposed new action under consideration is not carried out. It is not the same as nothing happening. Existing maintenance, statutory duties, actions already under way, and the conditions for their continuation must be recorded separately. Choosing `no_action` does not remove existing obligations. The resources and arrangements needed to actively maintain the status quo are compared as a separate option, and the natural changes, accumulating risks, opportunity costs, and distributional consequences of the baseline path are accounted for in the same time window. Whether intervention is necessary can be judged only if no action is included. Removing it often amounts to assuming in advance that “something must be done.”

Another option type for `SEL-SYS` is `system_variant`, which compares different outcomes under different environments or retention mechanisms. It does not let the system decide. `SEL-AGT` and `SEL-GOV` may include `external_action`, but a single external action option must be frozen on entering `authorized`. The external `selected_action` record must then match the same option, subject, object, action, territory, and validity period field by field.

Consequences must be recorded across scales. For every option, at least the nine axes A, X, T, O, C, R, I, N, and J must be used to specify the object level, spatial extent, time span, organizational level, causal level, resolution, scope of influence, network topology, and scope of authorization where consequences occur. An immediate local benefit must not be taken to represent long-term system benefits. An average improvement must not be used to cover a local exclusion zone. Stability at a higher level must not be used to make up for actual losses suffered by subjects at a lower level.

The distribution ledger must record benefits, costs, risks, impacts on rights, and shifts in burdens at each position. It must explicitly mark `power_position` as low, medium, high, or unknown. `affected_positions` lists all positions affected directly, indirectly, at second order, across domains, and across generations. `low_power_position_ids` identifies in particular the positions where it is harder to speak, exit, provide evidence, or shift costs. Every option must state who benefits, who bears the costs, who has decision-making authority, who is unable to refuse, whether costs shift from positions with more power to those with less, and whether unobservable positions are being mistaken for absent ones.

Least harm and proportionality are two explicit principles of choice. Each record must specify the principle ID and version, selected option, all options compared, evaluation dimensions, adequate reasons, evidence references, reviewer, time of review, and status. `least_harm` must instantiate `NSP-LEAST-HARM`. It must explain which harms can be avoided across all feasible options and no action, and why the selected option is the narrowest. `proportionality` must instantiate `NSP-PROPORTIONALITY` and check suitability, necessity, benefits and the price paid in rights, intensity, scope, and duration separately. Neither principle is a synonym for a “good objective.” The two principles must not be written up as slogans that cannot be reviewed or as tokens carrying only a name.

Evidence for an instance of a principle must resolve to the evidence set for the current run. The reviewer must be distinct from the deciding subject and be linked to an independent party responsible for review. Review must take place no later than the transition to authorization. The version may refer only to the current registered version. Expiry, the validity of all ten PF items, and the timing of further review must all cover the validity period of the selected option. An expired review record must not be used to maintain `authorized`.

## 14.5 Coercion, Defaults, Agendas, Representation, and Minority Protection

Before a subject says “yes,” the conditions under which they can say “no” must first be checked. Both `SEL-AGT` and `SEL-GOV` must create a `coercion_and_choice` record. It must examine whether direct or indirect coercion exists, whether the default locks in the result through inertia, complexity, or penalties, whether information is understandable and symmetric, whether the subject has decision-making capability, whether refusal is safe, and whether exit is genuinely feasible. Each check must give a status, rationale, and evidence references. `unknown` does not count as passing, and `not_applicable` must likewise state why it genuinely does not apply.

Default design is not a neutral background condition. Who sets the default, how much switching costs, and how silence is interpreted can all change the distribution of choices. Coercion also takes forms beyond commands. Tying access to essential resources to compliance, retaliation through relationships, identity exposure, withholding information, unaffordable exit costs, or moralizing care responsibilities can turn formal options into a single path.

`SEL-GOV` must also create a `governance_procedure` record. Agenda formation checks who can raise questions, who can exclude options, and when emergency procedures end. Representation checks the source of authorization, the scope of representation, whether it can be withdrawn, and who is unrepresented. Participation checks safety, accessibility, information, and actual capability to influence decisions. Minority protection checks rights that majority decisions must not cancel, local exclusion zones, and conditions for suspension. The conflict-of-interest check asks whether the people who decide, execute, evaluate, and benefit are the same. Accountability for execution checks whether responsible parties can be reviewed, stopped, and replaced.

Formal participation does not amount to genuine participation. A public meeting, a complaint channel, or a majority vote cannot by itself establish that people in low-power positions can safely express views and influence the decision. Each objection must retain a stable ID, claim, position, evidence, reasons for how it was handled, deadline for response, and safe submission channel. Before a record enters `authorized` within the framework, substantive objections capable of changing key facts, minimum protections, option feasibility, or valid permissions must be addressed. If the relevant reasons still have not received a commensurate response, the affected part must be suspended. What is decisive must be judged by criteria published in advance and open to appeal; where a conflict of interest exists, that determination must be referred to independent review. Objections must not be excluded because few people raise them, because a majority deems them “unimportant,” or because they are inconvenient to handle. For differences in values that persist after a reasoned response, the minority view must be preserved under an explicit decision rule that meets the requirements; fabricating unanimity in order to pass is not required.

`authorized` is an internal status marking the scope within which the framework itself adopts a choice.
